- Each step of the funnel should keep at least about 50% of users. Example: 100 add-to-carts should produce about 50 checkouts and 25 sales. A bigger drop points to hidden costs, a broken checkout or a mobile problem. (Source A, 2021, older.) [L0853.066]
- Stage rates are ratios of consecutive steps:
  - Landing page to add-to-cart = add-to-cart visitors ÷ landing page visitors
  - Add-to-cart to checkout = checkout starts ÷ add-to-cart visitors
  - Checkout to purchase = purchases ÷ checkout starts
  - Example: 12 add-to-cart visitors out of 200 landing page visitors is a 6% add-to-cart rate, which is the benchmark threshold in this example. [L1259.058]
- Shopify's own analytics shows visitors, pages opened and time on site, enough to diagnose early traffic problems. On average the first sale comes around 700 visitors, so about 5,000 visitors with no sales signals a serious issue. [L1380.038]
- CTR is one of the first and often weakest points in the funnel. Doubling CTR from 1% to 2% doubles incoming target-audience traffic without changing targeting, which can proportionally double revenue if conversion stays constant. [L0327.004]
- The lesson's quick math uses three levers: a 20% CPC discount on Shopping ads from a CTR boost, a 26% conversion rate lift from dynamic product ads (DPA), and a 10% boost in customer lifetime value (LTV). [L1031.036]

### Click volume expectations

- Expected clicks per conversion = 100 ÷ conversion rate in percent. At 2%, expect 1 conversion every 50 clicks. At 2.59%, expect 1 every 38 clicks (100 ÷ 2.59 ≈ 38.6). Expected conversions per 100 clicks = 100 × conversion rate. [L0745.014]
- Days to first conversion = clicks needed for one conversion ÷ clicks per day, where clicks per day = daily budget ÷ cost per click. Examples: at a 1% rate, 100 clicks are needed; at $10/day and $1 CPC that is 10 clicks/day, so 10 days to one conversion. At 0.5% it takes 20 days; at 0.25% it takes 40 days. [L1341.005]
- Visitors typically need 2 to 3 days from first click to purchase, because promotions and offers affect the buying timeline. Account for this delay when you compare ad spend to sales. (Source A, 2020, older.) [L0961.069]
- At a 2.5% rate, 385 visitors should yield 3 to 4 sales over seven days once the campaign and store are optimized. (Source A, 2022, older.) [L1092.078]
  - Source inconsistency: 385 × 2.5% ≈ 9.6 sales, not 3 to 4. Check the visitor base, rate or period before using this figure in a plan. [L1092.078]
- Example: 100 qualified clicks to a product page should produce 2.5 sales at a 2.5% rate. If sales are fewer, the product page, pricing or product selection may not match qualified traffic. Optimize the page or pause traffic to that product. [L0253.036]
- To determine how long to wait before expecting 2-3 sales per week:
  1. Select one qualified product page (receiving relevant keyword traffic). [L0253.047]
  2. Calculate spend per click (Ad Spend ÷ Clicks). [L0253.047]
  3. Calculate clicks needed for 2.5% conversion (40 clicks = 1 sale). [L0253.047]
  4. Calculate weekly clicks needed for 3 sales (120 clicks). [L0253.047]
  5. Example: if $2 per click, 120 clicks = $240 weekly spend needed. [L0253.047]
  6. Scale your daily budget to match this; give it 2-4 weeks of consistent traffic. [L0253.047]
  7. If conversion rate is lower than 2.5%, optimize the product page before concluding traffic is ineffective. [L0253.047]
- Organic traffic growth from zero: a realistic six-month target is around 10,000 monthly organic visits. 50,000 is unrealistic for most people starting from that baseline. (Source B, 2021-22, older.) [L0447.013]
- About 95% of ecommerce store visitors are new each day, and each visitor sees the discount offer only once. Plan traffic as new visitors, not returning ones. [L0137.005]

### Visitors and units needed

- Units needed per month = target monthly net profit ÷ net profit per sale. Example: a $3,000 monthly target needs 1,000 sales at $3 profit each, or 10 sales at about $300 profit each. Ten sales in a 30-day month is roughly one sale every three days. [L0474.005]
- Low-ticket vs. high-ticket example: for $3,000 profit, low-ticket products ($10) need 1,000 sales per month, while high-ticket products ($1,000) need only 10 (about one sale every 3 days). [L0012.004]
- Required monthly visitors = required monthly sales ÷ conversion rate. At 2.5%, 1,000 monthly sales need about 40,000 visitors per month (over 1,000 per day), and 10 monthly sales need about 400 visitors per month. [L0474.008, L1147.020]
- Traffic needed at 2.5% for $3,000 monthly profit: about 400 qualified visitors per month for a $300-profit product, versus about 40,000 for a $3-profit product. Buying 400 visitors costs far less than buying 40,000, so paid advertising is far more affordable for high-ticket products. (Source A, 2020 and undated sources.) [L0012.006, L1147.022, L0474.009]
- Monthly orders = monthly unique visitors × conversion rate. Example: 191,000 visitors × 2.5% = 4,775 orders per month. [L0524.004]

### Affordable cost per visitor

- Traffic is purchased only when profit per sale covers the cost per visitor, because not every visitor buys. A product earning $3 profit cannot support paid traffic at about $1 per visitor, while a $300 profit can. [L1147.021]
- Maximum affordable cost per visitor = profit per sale × conversion rate. At 2.5%, a product earning $3 can afford about $0.075 per visitor, and a product earning $300 can afford about $7.50. Paid traffic only makes sense below this limit. [L1147.027]
- Typical clicks should cost 20 to 50 cents. Example: at $1 per click, 1,000 clicks cost $1,000; the same traffic produced 20 sales on a generic website, 50 on a good website and 150 on a product-specific landing page, so page design changes cost per sale. (Source A, undated.) [L0643.038]
- Ad spend for traffic = visitors × CPC. Example: 500,000 visitors × $0.50 CPC = $250,000 spend. ROAS = revenue ÷ ad spend, so $1,000,000 ÷ $250,000 = 4x. [L0871.031]
- Judge return on ad spend only for ads with at least 20 purchases. [L0649.047]

### Early months and ramp-up

- A first-month revenue target of $20,000 is achievable only when the store is live, supplier products are uploaded, conversion is optimized and paid traffic is running. Missing it early does not mean the model failed; stores keep improving over time. (Source A, 2019, older.) [L0663.078]
- The first month after launch is mainly for collecting data with small ad budgets. Revenue grows after campaigns are optimized, so do not expect the first month to match later months. (Source A, 2025.) [L0667.085]

### Revenue and profit forecasts

- Revenue = visitors × conversion rate × AOV, where AOV = total revenue ÷ number of orders. Example per 100 visitors: 2% at a $60 AOV gives $120; 3% at a $50 AOV gives $150. [L1164.043]
- Calculate changes carefully: $150 against $120 is 25% more revenue, not 30%. Moving conversion from 2% to 3% is a 50% rise, not a doubling. [L1164.045]
- Estimated monthly revenue = sales per month × AOV. Example: 20 sales a month at an average order of $800 gives about $16,000 per month. Use this to check whether a high-ticket store grows as expected. [L1472.059]
- Estimated monthly net profit = monthly orders × customer value × net margin. Example from the session: about $622,000 gross customer value per month; at a 20% net margin, roughly $124,000 per month. Recompute with real data, because rough inputs multiply into large errors. [L0524.005]
- Present revenue and profit estimates as a range, not a single number. Rerun the estimate with a higher conversion rate (for example 3.5%) and with a higher average order value from bundles to see the spread. [L0524.006]

### Refunds and guarantees

- Net sales = gross sales − refunds, where refunds = gross sales × refund rate. Without a guarantee: 100 sales at a 5% refund rate gives 95 net sales. With a guarantee: 130 sales at a 10% refund rate gives 13 refunds and 117 net sales. [L0683.012]
- In this worked example, the guarantee produced 117 net sales against 95 without it: 22 more sales, roughly a 23% revenue increase. The result assumes the guarantee lifts orders from 100 to 130 while the refund rate rises from 5% to 10%. [L0683.013]
- Test a guarantee by calculating net sales under the expected higher conversion rate and the expected higher refund rate together. A guarantee is worth adding when net sales still rise after refunds are subtracted. [L0683.014]

#### Further details

- The minimum benchmark is 2 sales per 100 unique visits, a 2% conversion rate. [L0582.003]

## Ad metrics glossary and how to read them

### Delivery: impressions, reach, frequency and CPM

- **Reach** is the total number of unique people who have seen the ad. **Impressions** is the total number of times the ad was shown, so one person seeing it twice counts as two impressions. [L0290.018]
- Impressions count every display, including several to the same person across placements such as Facebook and Instagram. Reach counts only unique Meta accounts, so four impressions to one person equal one reach. [L1024.028]
- **Frequency = Impressions ÷ Reach**, showing how many times the average person saw the ad. A value of 1.2 means each person saw the ad about 1.2 times on average. [L0290.018, L0950.021]
  - Example: 2,017,463 ÷ 862,845 = 2.34. [L0797.023]
- Frequency checks:
  - A frequency above 2 means the same ad is shown too often and may tire. Watch it closely, especially with image ads that are cheap per click and served repeatedly. [L0950.020]
  - In longer-running campaigns, frequency normally rises from about 2.5 to about 4. The expected range depends on how long the ad has been live. [L1030.005]
  - Top-of-funnel frequency is about 1.03 to 1.2. Low frequency does not rule out fatigue. [L0398.041]
  - Reading the two figures together: treat 2 as the point to check the ad. In a campaign that has run for a while, a frequency of 2.5 to 4 is normal drift, so judge it against campaign age. [L0950.020, L1030.005]
- **CPM (cost per thousand impressions)** is what you pay for 1,000 ad impressions across all placements. It is your cost of advertising. [L0290.019, L1024.027]
  - Formula: CPM = (ad spend ÷ impressions) × 1,000. [L0874.007, L1021.002, L1259.059]
  - Example: $50 spent over 1,500 impressions gives a CPM of about $33.33. [L0874.007]
  - Facebook formula: CPM = amount spent ÷ impressions × 1,000, the cost paid for 1,000 impressions across Meta placements. [L1024.027]
  - Cost of impressions = (impressions ÷ 1,000) × CPM. Example: 500,000 impressions at a $15 CPM cost $7,500. The lesson's figure of about $33,000 for the same case is an arithmetic error. [L0871.032]
  - CPM means cost per mille, the cost to show the ad to 1,000 people. It counts impressions, not clicks or engagement. It appears as a column in Meta Business Manager. [L1407.022]
  - CPM is set by the auction. Better ad rankings can lower it, so the creative is the main lever for cost. [L0638.043]

### Clicks: CTR and CPC

#### CTR (click-through rate)

- **Formula:** CTR = clicks ÷ impressions × 100, as a percentage. [L0327.001, L0874.009, L0968.070, L0808.057, L1021.003, L1024.035, L1271.011]
  - Examples: 5 clicks from 100 impressions = 5% CTR. [L0327.001]
  - 100 link clicks over 1,000 impressions = 10%. [L0874.009]
  - 2 clicks from 100 viewers = 2%, meaning 2 people out of 100 impressions clicked through to the website. [L1021.003, L1024.035]
  - Older (2021): 100 clicks from 1,000 impressions = 10%, and raising it to 12% is the optimisation goal. [L0968.070]
- **Google Search organic CTR:** Impressions are how often the store appeared in organic search results, and clicks are how often people then clicked through. Both are shown in Google Search Console > Performance. [L1271.011]
- **Link clicks vs. all clicks:** Use link clicks, not all clicks, when calculating Facebook CTR and CPC, because link clicks show people who went through to the store. [L1220.041] Ads Manager can also show a CTR (all) figure that counts all clicks. Confirm you are reading the link-click version before comparing against the 3% to 7% benchmark. [L0874.019]
- **Unique metrics:** Unique metrics count each person once. Repeated clicks by one person count as one unique click. Non-unique clicks count every click. Use unique CTR and unique clicks for analysis. [L0640.005]
- **Facebook CTR variants:**
  - CTR (all) measures attention, meaning how many people noticed the ad. Unique Outbound CTR measures interest, meaning how many different people clicked. [L0258.021]
  - Outbound Unique CTR is the main CTR metric, since it counts unique clicks that send users to the website through the call-to-action. [L0639.009]
  - Use the unique link click-through rate, not the standard CTR, when judging Facebook ads. [L0502.036]
- **Scope in Google Ads:** CTR can be measured at account, campaign, ad group, ad, keyword, PLA (Product Listing Ads) or search term level, and across different time periods. [L0327.002]
- **Data sufficiency:** CTR analysis needs enough data. If an ad has run for one day with minimal impressions, extend the analysis period. [L0327.003]
- **Variation:** CTR varies by ad type and keyword position. Top-of-funnel keywords have different CTRs from bottom-of-funnel keywords. Search ads have different CTRs from PLAs, display ads or YouTube ads. [L0327.006]
- **Cold traffic:** Link clicks, CTR and CPC are top-of-funnel metrics, measured before the click. They show how cold prospects who have never seen the product react to the ad. [L1024.034]
- **Early test signal:** A CTR of 2% to 3% held over 7 to 10 days shows the hook is working. It does not prove the offer converts. [L0524.064]
- **Lead generation ads (older, 2021 to 2022):** Lead generation ads are expected to reach very high CTRs compared with competitor product ads on the same term, because the unusual angle makes them stand out. [L0922.021]

#### CPC (cost per click)

- **Formula:** CPC = ad spend ÷ link clicks. [L0874.004, L1021.004, L1220.040, L0808.056]
  - Examples: $50 spent with 100 clicks = $0.50. $50 with 25 clicks = $2.00. [L0874.004]
  - $8.92 for six clicks = about $1.49 per click. [L0874.006]
  - $50 spent with 25 link clicks = $2.00, above the 1 USD target, meaning the CTR is too low and the ads need to change. [L1220.035]
  - Average CPC = total ad spend ÷ total clicks. Example: $330 over 430 clicks = about $0.77. [L0808.056]
- **Formula through CPM and CTR:** CPC = CPM ÷ (1,000 × CTR as a fraction). [L0774.030, L1024.036, L1259.004, L1260.003, L1375.014]
  - CPC = CPM ÷ (1,000 × CTR). Example: CPM of $20 and CTR of 1.5% gives about $1.33. A low CPM does not guarantee a low CPC when the CTR is low, so use this formula to find the source of a high CPC. [L0774.030]
  - CPM of $10 and CTR of 2% gives 20 clicks per 1,000 impressions, so each click costs $0.50. [L1024.036]
  - CPM of $30 and CTR of 2% gives 1,000 impressions, 20 clicks and a CPC of $1.50. [L1259.004, L1260.003]
  - CPM of $10 and CTR of 1.5% gives a CPC of about $0.67. A CPC below $1 alone does not guarantee the 1.5% CTR target. [L1375.014]
- **Worked check:** At a CPM of $10, $50 of spend gives about 5,000 impressions. If 25 link clicks come from them, the CTR is 0.5%, below the 1.5% target, and the CPC is $2, above the 1 USD limit. [L1220.040]
- **CPC is an outcome of CPM and CTR.** A rising CPC can come from a higher CPM (costlier placements) or a lower CTR (a weaker ad). Diagnose which one drives it. [L1024.037, L0502.037]
  - A high CPM means fewer impressions and fewer people reached. A low CTR makes the CPC high because few people click. Judge them together. [L0502.037]
  - A high CTR with a high CPM can still produce a high CPC. [L0773.003]
  - A high CPM can still produce a low CPC when CTR is strong. [L1259.059]
- **CPC on its own:** Treat a CPC above 1 USD alone as no clear sign, since sales and return on ad spend matter more. [L0502.037]
- **Google Ads CPC:** Quality Score work is not about the lowest CPC. Relevance raises Quality Score, which can lower CPC. See the Google Ads section. [L0220.002, L0510.058]

### Video attention: hook rate, hold rate and retention

#### Definitions

- **Hook rate (3-second version)** = 3-second video plays ÷ impressions × 100. It is the share of impressions that watched past the first three seconds. [L0398.042, L1024.030]
  - It measures how well the first 3 to 5 seconds stop the scroll and grab attention. [L1024.030]
  - Video attention rate is the same calculation: (3-second video plays ÷ impressions) × 100. It shows what share of impressions watched the first three seconds of a video ad. [L0644.001, L1426.007]
  - Hook rate applies to video ads only, not to static images. [L0640.002]
- **Hold rate** = ThruPlays ÷ impressions × 100. ThruPlays are plays of at least 15 seconds or plays that reached the end. [L0398.042]
  - Through-plays are views beyond 15 seconds, so the metric shows how well the video keeps viewers after the hook. [L1024.032]
  - Use hook rate and hold rate together to judge the hook and the body of a video. [L0398.042]
  - **Conflicting definition:** Hold rate is also described as the share of people who watch the video up to 50% of its length. [L0640.004] Check which definition your report uses.
  - Hold rate is only meaningful with large traffic. Longer videos naturally show lower hold rates than short ones, for example 15 seconds against one minute. [L0640.004]
- **Hook and hold together:** Hook rate measures how well the first seconds stop viewers. Hold rate measures how long they keep watching. Read them with CTR and CPM. [L1213.017]
- **Main test metric:** Hook rate is the main metric for hook performance in a creative test. Compare it across the hook variations to find the winner, then check hold rate for how long viewers keep watching. [L0902.023]
- **Retention in steps (analysis framework):**
  - Hook rate (25% version) = 25% plays ÷ impressions. [L0613.049]
  - Retention 25 to 50% = 50% plays ÷ 25% plays. [L0613.049]
  - Retention 50 to 75% = 75% plays ÷ 50% plays. [L0613.049]
  - Retention 75 to 100% = 100% plays ÷ 75% plays. [L0613.049]
  - Completion rate = 100% plays ÷ impressions. [L0613.049]
  - **Conflicting hook definition:** The framework defines hook as 25% plays, while the other units use the 3-second version. Name the definition you use. [L0613.049, L0398.042]
- **Drop-off points:** Video ad analytics show viewer completion at 75% watched (three-quarters through) and 95% watched (nearly finished), along with where viewers most commonly drop off. This shows which hooks and problem-solution formulas hold attention. [L0280.046]
  - Drop-off can be analysed at 25%, 50% and 75% views to see exactly where viewers leave. This deeper analysis is only worth it when the account has a lot of traffic. [L1426.014]

#### Benchmarks for hook rate

The sources disagree on hook benchmarks. Give one set, state which set you used, and apply it to every ad so scores stay comparable. [L0613.052]

| Source reading | Benchmark | Ids |
|---|---|---|
| Source C scorecard | average about 40%; above 50% is great | [L0398.041] |
| Attention table | poor <15%, average 15-25%, good 25-40%, excellent >40% | [L0613.050] |
| Funnel reference | poor <10%, below average 10-20%, average 20-30%, good 30-40%, excellent >40% | [L0613.051] |
| Attention rate (hook retention) | 40% or more is very good | [L0639.030] |
| Facebook video ads | above 30% good, above 40% excellent, above 50% outstanding | [L0640.009] |
| Diagnosis (mainly when sales are weak) | 25% or below not good; above 30% the target; 40-49% very good; above 50% excellent | [L0652.007] |
| 3-second video attention | 25% or more acceptable, 30% or more good, 40% or more very good | [L0644.003] |
| 3-second video attention | about 25% acceptable, 30% and above good, 40% and above excellent, around 20% low | [L1426.009] |
| Custom metric in Facebook | above 30% good, 40% to 50% very strong, 50% means half the audience watched, below 20% bad, 15% weak | [L0950.015] |

[L0398.041, L0613.050, L0613.051, L0639.030, L0640.009, L0652.007, L0644.003, L1426.009, L0950.015]

- Use the 30% line as the target for Facebook hook rate. The sources treat high attention as a rule of thumb only. [L0652.007, L0640.009]
- High attention alone does not guarantee sales. The audience must still be relevant. [L0640.009]
- A low-attention ad can still sell when the traffic is relevant. Treat the thresholds as rules of thumb. [L0644.003]
- Benchmarks vary by industry. Compare against your own historical metrics in your ad account first, and improve those before judging against general figures. [L0652.008]

#### Benchmark table: video attention

| Metric | Poor | Average | Good | Excellent |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Hook rate | <15% | 15-25% | 25-40% | >40% |
| 25% to 50% retention | <50% | 50-65% | 65-80% | >80% |
| 50% to 75% retention | <40% | 40-55% | 55-70% | >70% |
| 75% to 100% retention | <30% | 30-45% | 45-60% | >60% |

[L0613.050]

#### Funnel benchmark reference

| Metric | Poor | Below average | Average | Good | Excellent |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Hook rate | <10% | 10-20% | 20-30% | 30-40% | >40% |
| CTR | <0.5% | 0.5-1% | 1-1.5% | 1.5-2.5% | >2.5% |
| LPV per click | <60% | 60-70% | 70-80% | 80-90% | >90% |
| ATC per LPV | <3% | 3-5% | 5-8% | 8-12% | >12% |
| Purchase per checkout | <30% | 30-45% | 45-60% | 60-75% | >75% |

[L0613.051]

#### Hold rate benchmarks (scorecard)

- Hold rate average is about 6% to 8%. 8% is good, above 10% is very good, and 10% to 15% is excellent. [L0398.041]

### Sales and funnel: CVR, add-to-cart, CPA, ROAS and purchases

#### Conversion rates

- **Ad conversion rate (CVR)** = purchases ÷ link clicks, as a percentage. [L0290.003]
  - For Facebook analysis, use purchases ÷ unique outbound clicks, shown as a percentage. Facebook tracks page views poorly, while outbound clicks reliably reach the website. [L0640.003]
- **Store conversion rate** = (purchases ÷ visitors) × 100. Example: 10 visitors with 1 purchase = 1%. [L1021.007]
  - **Conflicting definition:** Ad CVR divides by clicks, and store conversion rate divides by visitors. Use the ad version when judging the ad and the store version when judging the site.
  - A website conversion rate of about 2% is treated as a good benchmark. [L0639.030, L0639.008]
- **Store check:** A strong CTR with a weak site conversion rate points to the landing page or offer, not the ad. [L1315.019]

#### Add-to-cart and checkout

- **ATC%** = add to carts ÷ link clicks, as a percentage. [L0290.004]
- **IC%** = initiate checkout ÷ link clicks, as a percentage. [L0290.005]
- Read add-to-cart ratios in the Shopify backend:

| Channel | Add-to-cart ratio | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| Facebook ads | Minimum 5% | Read in the Shopify backend |
| TikTok ads | Minimum 3% | Read in the Shopify backend |
| Google Ads | Around 7% | Higher because search is intent-based |

[L1194.012]

- Add-to-cart and sales checks: cheap clicks that produce no add-to-carts and no sales are a warning sign, not a success. Judge campaigns on downstream funnel metrics, not on click metrics alone. [L0361.005]

#### Cost per acquisition (CPA)

- **Formula:** CPA = total ad spend ÷ number of conversions. Example: $500 ÷ 18 conversions = $27.78. [L0327.008]
- CPA is also called CPP or cost per purchase. It is the ad cost of one sale: ad spend ÷ number of purchases. [L1021.006]
- Cost per result (purchase objective) = amount spent ÷ website purchases. [L0797.023]
- Cost per purchase shows the spend per purchase. [L1024.043]
- CPA should not be your primary profitability metric. ROAS is more important. [L0327.008]

#### Return on ad spend (ROAS)

- **Formula:** ROAS = revenue from ads ÷ ad spend. A spend of $1 returning $3 gives a ROAS of 3. [L1021.005]
- ROAS equals the revenue from an ad set divided by the ad spend over the same period. A ROAS of 4 to 5 means about $4 to $5 of revenue for every $1 spent on that ad set. [L0524.063]
- **Purchase ROAS** = purchase conversion value ÷ amount spent. A ROAS of 2 means $2 is returned for every $1 spent. A ROAS of 1.7 means $1.70 is returned for every $1 spent. [L1024.041]
- Decide on scaling only by comparing ROAS and cost per purchase against the breakeven and desired values, not by delivery numbers. [L0445.028]

#### Purchases and revenue

- Purchases shows the number of purchases. Cost per purchase shows the spend per purchase. Purchase conversion value shows the revenue from those purchases. Together they show whether the sales are worth the ad spend. [L1024.043]
- Platform revenue = website purchases × AOV (average order value). Example: 306 × $129.20 = $39,535.20. [L0797.023]
- **Content views** fire when a visitor lands on the product page the ad links to. Cost per content view shows what each landed visitor costs. [L1024.039]

### Google Ads: Quality Score, Ad Rank, impression share and YouTube

#### Ad Rank and Quality Score

- **Formula:** Ad Rank = Quality Score × Bid Amount. Your position in Google Ads auctions is determined by this product, not by bid alone. A high Quality Score on a smaller bid outperforms a low Quality Score on a large bid. [L0220.001]
- **Ad relevance** is the match between your copy and the search intent, and it makes up a large part of Quality Score. When headlines and descriptions directly address the search query, Quality Score rises, impressions increase, cost per click decreases, and you can outrank competitors who bid significantly more. [L0220.002]
- The goal of optimising Quality Score is not the lowest possible CPC. It is the ability to bid more than competitors while staying profitable at the target ROAS. Advertisers who can afford higher bids dominate auctions in the niche. [L0510.058]
- Lowering bids as far as possible is not the goal of paid traffic. Cheaper clicks should come from better ad quality, not from underbidding. [L1031.035]

#### Search impression share (older, 2021 to 2022)

- Search Impression Share is the percentage of relevant search queries your ads show for, given current bids and negative keyword lists. It is the main measure of how competitive your bids are. [L0744.043]

#### YouTube in-stream pricing

- Example: about 3,500 views for about 4 cents each, which is about $0.0011 per view. [L1510.003]

### Decision thresholds: when a campaign is a winner

- **Clear winner:** at least 3 sales with positive ROAS. One sale may be an exception. [L0398.041]
- **Scaling signal:** a creative used in 8 or more ads means Meta is scaling it. [L0398.041]
- **Profitable sales before scaling an interest:** 6 or more. [L0639.030]
- **Expansion threshold** to push a new ad to the next audience tier: 3% CTR or 5% engagement. [L0639.030]
- **Data before judging:** 3 to 5 days. Avoid judging on one day. [L0639.030]
- **Chargeback rate:** about 1.5%. Above this, fines and processor problems follow. [L0639.030]
- **Scorecard table** (from the Source C scorecard):

| Metric | Benchmark | Reading |
|---|---|---|
| Outbound CTR | above 2% | good; 1.5-2% borderline in some cases; below 1% means the creative is not working |
| CPC | below $1.5 | good (reference for 2023) |
| CPM | above $30, sometimes $35+ | competitive audience or weak creative that forces views |
| Hook rate (3-second plays / impressions) | average about 40% | above 50% is great |
| Hold rate (ThruPlays / impressions) | average about 6-8% | 8% is good; above 10% is very good; 10-15% is excellent |
| Frequency (top-of-funnel) | about 1.03-1.2 | low frequency does not rule out fatigue |
| Clear winner | at least 3 sales with positive ROAS | one sale may be an exception |
| Scaling signal | creative used in 8 or more ads | Meta is scaling it |

[L0398.041]

### Reading the metrics together

- Judge CTR, CPM and CPC together, and always check CPC, conversion rate and margin before deciding. [L0773.050, L0502.037]
- **Healthy baseline for Meta:** CTR above 1.5%, CPC below 1 USD and CPM below 25 USD together. If all three hold but sales are missing, the work moves from the ads to the store. [L0774.004]
- **Store problem:** If the CTR is above roughly 3% to 5% and the ads still produce no results, the problem is almost always the store, not the ad. [L0774.001]
- **Traffic is cheap, audience right:** Benchmark link-click metrics over a few days included a CPM of $23.28, a cost per link click of $0.93, a CTR of 2.49% and 313 link clicks. Such top-of-funnel numbers signal that traffic is cheap and the audience is right. [L1225.067]
- **Brand-specific targets:** Top-of-funnel metrics such as CPM, CPC and CTR are common benchmarks. Cost per purchase and ROAS targets are unique to each brand, so compare each brand against its own margins. [L1225.068]
- **Own history first:** Compare against your own past numbers in your own ad account before judging against general figures. [L0652.008, L0640.010]
- **Low CTR is not automatically a failure** if the product converts well for its qualified buyers. Look for improvements relative to your own past numbers. [L0640.010]
- **Relevance and attention:** A creative with low video attention but good CTR or sales still needs review. Low attention with sales may mean the traffic is relevant but small. [L1426.013]
- **Hook and sales:** A high CTR does not prove a good ad when no purchases follow. A high attention rate with irrelevant traffic gives misleading signals. Check whether audience and message are aligned, not only the percentage. [L1426.015]
- **Platform comparison:** Compare platforms by CPM and CTR together, not by CTR alone. Snapchat can deliver more clicks that do not necessarily bring quality traffic. [L0562.010]
- **Post-test check:** After the first test spend, check CPM, CTR and CPC. A CTR above about 3% is a strong signal, but it must be read together with conversion rate and store data. [L1161.044]

#### Diagnosis sequence for video ads

1. Check the video attention rate (3-second plays divided by impressions) first, to judge whether the hook works. [L1426.011]
2. Check the click-through rate, to judge whether the middle of the video and the offer hold attention. [L1426.011]
3. Check add-to-cart and purchase data, to judge whether the page and sale convert. [L1426.011]
4. Use the combined picture of these metrics to decide what to improve. [L1426.011]

[L1426.011]

Hook benchmarks are rules of thumb, applied mainly when sales are weak. [L0652.007]

#### Build a new creative from hook and hold data

1. Add the Hook Rate and Hold Rate columns to the dashboard. [L0398.044]
2. Find the video with a strong hook rate and the video with a strong hold rate. [L0398.044]
3. Take the first three to five seconds from the strong-hook video. [L0398.044]
4. Take the rest of the video from the strong-hold video. [L0398.044]
5. Combine them into one new video and test it as a new creative. [L0398.044]

[L0398.044]

### Benchmarks: Meta

#### Meta CTR

- **Minimum and test targets:**
  - Test target for new ads: at least 1%. [L1194.012]
  - Facebook CTR target: at least 1.5%. A CTR above 0.7% is acceptable, but 1.5% is the target value. [L1220.032]
  - A CTR of about 1.5% is a decent guideline for Meta ads. Anything lower is generally considered weaker. Treat this as a guideline, not a hard cut-off. [L0644.009]
  - A CTR of at least 1.5% is the guideline for Meta ads. Lower CTRs need attention to the middle of the video and the offer. Ads with a CTR around 0.7% to 1.0% still produced sales for some stores. [L1426.010]
  - A CTR around 1% was considered weak in one example, while 1.83% and 1.24% were seen on other ad sets. A CTR of 3% or above is a strong result in the examples given. [L0650.047]
- **Good readings:**
  - A good CTR on Facebook is above 1.5%. Winning products usually sit between 2% and 2.5%. Below 1.5% combined with no sales is a warning sign. [L0773.049]
  - On Facebook ad sets, a CTR above 1.5% signals that ads are performing. CTRs of 3% to 5% show strong relevance but still need sales to be meaningful. Winning products in the lesson hit roughly 2% or more. [L0773.002]
  - A CTR above 1.5% on Meta ad sets shows the ad is attracting clicks. [L0774.001]
  - Good Facebook CTR range: 1.5% to 2.0%. [L0229.012]
  - Industry-standard CTR on Facebook is about 2%, meaning roughly 20 clicks per 1,000 people reached. [L0639.008, L0639.030]
  - A CTR above 2% is considered good, and the goal is to push it higher over time. [L0652.009]
  - Facebook CTR target: aim for above 2%. [L0640.010]
  - Good performance on Meta ads means a CTR above 2% and a CPC below $1. The audience is interested and clicks through to the store. [L1172.032]
  - With strong creative, a CTR over 2% and CPC around $1 to $1.40 are the cited benchmarks. [L1315.019]
  - For T4 and T5 markets, a healthy Meta ad has a CTR around 2% or above. [L1260.004]
  - Unique link CTR above 1.5% is considered good. [L0502.036]
- **Higher readings:**
  - CTRs of 3% to 5% are good on Meta, as rough reference points that depend on product and market. [L0361.004]
  - The ideal Meta ad CTR is 3% to 7%. Unlike CPC and CPM, a higher CTR is better. [L0874.010]
  - A CTR of 2.2% to 2.4% is acceptable if you let the ad run another day or add a bit more spend. [L0874.010]
  - In one real campaign, a CTR of 2.12% per impression was below the ideal range. [L0874.011]

**Reconciling the CTR figures:** The units give 1.5% as the floor for link CTR, about 2% as the good line, and 3% to 7% as the ideal. The 3% to 7% range is the Source D reading, and the 1.5% and 2% lines appear across Source C and the other units. Always confirm which CTR variant you are reading (link CTR, unique outbound CTR or CTR all) before comparing. [L0874.019, L0639.009, L0502.036, L0874.010]

#### Meta CPC

| Reading | Value | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Target, Meta ads | below 1 USD | [L0774.002, L1220.033, L0773.003] |
| Winning products | usually 0.30 to 0.40 USD | [L0774.002, L0773.003] |
| Warning sign | near 1 USD | [L0774.002] |
| Dropshipping target range | $0.50 to $1.50; below that range is excellent | [L0640.011] |
| Good | about $1 | [L0874.005] |
| Relatively good | $1.10 to $1.20 | [L0874.005] |
| Bad | $2 or higher | [L0874.005] |
| T4 markets | under 1.50 dollars is good; under 1 dollar is ideal | [L1260.005] |
| Rough reference for Meta | 0.60 to 0.67 USD very good; about 1.50 USD or more is the higher end | [L0361.004] |
| Good, rough benchmark | under $1.50 to $2.00 | [L0229.012] |
| Good, scorecard (2023 reference) | below $1.5 | [L0398.041] |
| Early-test target | around $0.50 ideal; old ad account showed $5 to $8 | [L1194.012] |
| Benchmark example | $0.93 cost per link click | [L1225.067] |

[L0774.002, L1220.033, L0773.003, L0640.011, L0874.005, L1260.005, L0361.004, L0229.012, L0398.041, L1194.012, L1225.067, L0774.030]

- Do not focus too much on high CPC if the campaign is profitable and ROAS is strong. [L0640.011]
- A CPC above 1 USD alone is no clear sign. Sales and return on ad spend matter more. [L0502.037]
- A CPC near 1 USD is a warning sign for winning products. [L0774.002]
- **Reconciling the CPC figures:** The sources range from under 1 USD (working target) to 1.50 USD (upper good limit in T4 markets) and $2 (bad). Use under 1 USD as the working target for Meta. Use the 1.50 USD upper limit when the market is one of the T4 markets (the USA, Canada, Australia and the UK). Treat $2 or more as bad in every case. [L0774.002, L1260.005, L0874.005]

#### Meta CPM

- Average CPM on Facebook is about $10. At that CPM, a $10 daily ad set budget reaches up to about 1,000 people per day, usually fewer. [L0639.007]
- Target: under $10, ideally near $5. Old ad account showed $20 to $50. [L1194.012]
- Healthy setup: CPM below 25 USD. [L0773.003, L0774.003]
- For T4 and T5 markets: a Meta ad is healthy when CPM is under 30 dollars. [L1260.004]
- A CPM above $30, sometimes $35 or more, points to a competitive audience or weak creative that forces views. [L0398.041]
- Reference point for a low CPM in a real campaign: $31.52 per 1,000 impressions. Compare your CPM against this kind of figure, keeping in mind that a lower CPM means cheaper reach. [L0874.018]
- Source D reading for 2026: a CPM of about $50 is decent, and anything from about $70 upward is poor. A lower CPM is always better because reaching 1,000 people costs less. Benchmarks may rise as competition increases. [L0874.008]
- **Reconciling the CPM figures:** The sources range from about $10 (average), under $25 and under $30 (healthy limits) to about $50 (decent) and $70 (poor). The Source D 2026 figures are its own reading of current competition. The Source C limits (under $25) and the T4/T5 rule (under $30) are the stricter checks. Apply the one that matches your market and period, and state it. [L0639.007, L0773.003, L1260.004, L0874.008]

#### Meta CPM table, funnel and other rules

- Top-of-funnel benchmark table:

| Metric | Benchmark | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| CPM (Meta) | about $10 | Average cost per 1,000 impressions |
| CTR (all) | about 2% | Industry standard; roughly 20 clicks per 1,000 people reached |
| Outbound Unique CTR | main metric | Clicks that send users to the website |
| Conversion rate (website) | about 2% | Treated as a good benchmark |
| Expansion threshold | 3% CTR or 5% engagement | Needed to push a new ad to the next audience tier |
| Attention rate (hook retention) | 40% or more | Described as very good |
| CTR (soft metric) | above 2% | Good sign for a creative |
| Profitable sales before scaling | 6 or more | Needed before scaling an interest |
| Data before judging | 3 to 5 days | Avoid judging on one day |
| Chargeback rate | about 1.5% | Above this, fines and processor problems follow |

[L0639.030]

- **Facebook quality rankings:** Ads Manager shows a quality ranking, an engagement ranking and a conversion ranking for each ad. Each is graded below average, average or above average. Use them to judge how the creative performs against competitors. [L0638.042]
- Facebook's auction sets CPM from how the ad performs in these rankings. Better rankings can lower CPM. [L0638.043]
- **Facebook metrics can contradict each other,** for example total play counts and average play time. Cross-check several metrics and the actual video length before drawing conclusions. [L1215.013]
- Facebook hook rate as a custom metric is built in Ads Manager columns and applies to video ads only. [L0640.002]

### Benchmarks: TikTok

- **CPC:** target below $1 per click. [L1375.002]
  - For a US audience, a CPC of about $0.50 to $0.62 is good. [L1382.042]
  - Any CBO campaign or ad group with a CPC above $1 should be cut. [L1382.042]
- **CPM:** target below $10 per 1,000 impressions. [L1375.003]
  - TikTok ads have much lower CPM than Facebook. The main difference between platforms is CPM, not the ideal CTR. [L0773.038]
- **CTR (conflicting readings):**
  - Target: above 1.5%, meaning the share of viewers who click the ad. [L1375.004]
  - In TikTok product tests, a CTR above 1% is strong. A like rate of 1% or more signals engagement. [L1382.042]
  - A CTR around 0.8% can be acceptable on TikTok if traffic, conversion rate and profit are good. [L0773.038]
  - Apply the 1.5% target as the default. Use the 1% and 0.8% readings only when traffic, conversion and profit support them. [L1375.004, L1382.042, L0773.038]
- **Formula:** CTR is clicks per impression, so CPC = CPM ÷ (1,000 × CTR) and CTR = CPM ÷ (1,000 × CPC). Example: at a CPM of $10 and a CTR of 1.5%, the CPC is about $0.67. A CPC below $1 alone does not guarantee the 1.5% CTR target. [L1375.014]
- **Add-to-cart ratio:** minimum 3%, read in the Shopify backend. [L1194.012]
- TikTok ads usually cost more per impression than Pinterest for similar targeting. [L0788.058]

### Benchmarks: Pinterest

- **US cost benchmarks:** CPM between $3 and $5, sometimes as low as $2. [L1134.012, L1135.060]
- Early-stage quality is usually poor because Pinterest is still learning the audience. [L1134.012]
- Early traffic tests stay cheap compared with Facebook or TikTok. [L1135.060]
- For similar targeting, Pinterest usually has lower CPM and CPC than TikTok. Costs are lower in the UK and Europe than in the US. The US has the largest user base and therefore more competition for the same audience. [L0788.058]
- No Pinterest CTR benchmark is given in the units for this section. [none in this set]

### Benchmarks: Google Ads

- **Average CTR:** about 1%. CTR above 1% is considered good. [L0327.005]
- **Varies by ad type and position:** top-of-funnel and bottom-of-funnel keywords differ, and search ads differ from PLAs, display ads and YouTube ads. [L0327.006]
- **Add-to-cart ratio:** around 7%. Google is higher because search is intent-based. [L1194.012]
- **Scope:** check CTR at account, campaign, ad group, ad, keyword, PLA or search term level. [L0327.002]
- **CPA:** see the sales section. CPA is not the primary target; ROAS is more important. [L0327.008]
- **Quality Score and impression share:** see the Google Ads section. [L0220.001, L0220.002, L0510.058, L0744.043]
- No Google CPC benchmark (beyond the average CPC formula example) is given in the units for this section. [L0808.056]

### Benchmarks: Bing and revenue per click

- No knowledge unit in this set gives Bing benchmarks. [none in this set]
- No knowledge unit in this set defines revenue per click. [none in this set]

### Other platforms: Snapchat

- Rough early-test benchmarks for paid traffic: a cost per click under 50p and a cost per 1,000 impressions of about 2 to 3 pounds. [L0562.038]
- CTR benchmarks differ by platform. In one test, Snapchat reached nearly 2%, while most other platforms stayed below 1%. Compare each platform against its own numbers. [L0562.038]
- Snapchat has a lower CPM than Facebook, and it can deliver more clicks that do not necessarily bring quality traffic. [L0562.010]

### Setting up custom metrics in the ad platforms

- **Hook Rate and Hold Rate in Ads Manager** (procedure):
  - Open the column or custom metric option and choose create custom metric.
  - Name the first "Hook Rate", set the format to percentage, and use 3-second video plays divided by impressions.
  - Name the second "Hold Rate", set it to percentage, and use ThruPlays divided by impressions.
  - Add both columns to the dashboard to compare creatives side by side. [L0398.043]
- **Funnel analysis metrics:** In Facebook Ads Manager, go to Columns > Customize columns > Custom metrics to create the custom metrics used for funnel analysis. Set the Hook rate metric as a percentage of impressions where viewers watched the first three seconds. [L0950.014]
- **Hook rate definition as a custom metric:** people who watch the first 3 seconds of a video divided by all impressions, as a percentage. [L0950.015]
- **Video attention metric:** a custom metric calculated as 3-second video plays divided by impressions, as a percentage. In Meta Ads Manager, create it via Customize columns > Create custom metric, name it "Video attention", set the format to percentage, and enter the 3-second video plays field divided by the impressions field. [L1426.007]
- **Video attention, step by step:** open Columns > Customize columns, click Create custom metric, name it "Video attention", set it as a percentage, and use 3-second video plays divided by impressions. Save it and add it next to click-through rate in the reporting view. [L1426.008]
- **Frequency:** found in the Facebook Ads columns. [L0950.021]
- **Video attention benchmark for the first three seconds:** about 25% is acceptable, 30% and above is good, and 40% and above is excellent. A value around 20% is considered low, and the hook should be improved. [L1426.009]

### Checking numbers by hand

Ad set metrics can be checked from the raw totals:

- Frequency = impressions ÷ reach. Example: 2,017,463 ÷ 862,845 = 2.34. [L0797.023]
- CPM = amount spent ÷ impressions × 1,000. Example: 10,832.32 ÷ 2,017,463 × 1,000 = $5.37. [L0797.023]
- CPC = amount spent ÷ link clicks. Example: 10,832.32 ÷ 27,761 = $0.39. [L0797.023]
- CTR = link clicks ÷ impressions. Example: 27,761 ÷ 2,017,463 = 1.38%. [L0797.023]
- Cost per result (purchase objective) = amount spent ÷ website purchases. Example: 10,832.32 ÷ 306 = $35.40. [L0797.023]
- Platform revenue = website purchases × AOV. Example: 306 × $129.20 = $39,535.20. [L0797.023]

If you estimate impressions from spend, use impressions ≈ spend ÷ CPM × 1,000. Example: at a CPM of 10 USD, 50 USD of spend gives about 5,000 impressions, so 25 link clicks is a CTR of 0.5%, below the 1.5% target, and a CPC of 2 USD, above the 1 USD limit. [L1220.040]

### Common mistakes

- Spending on Facebook ads without knowing what CTR and CPM mean makes results impossible to read and wastes budget. Learn these metrics before testing products. [L0519.027, L1176.021]
- Meta Ads Manager metrics such as CTR, CPC and CPM describe ad delivery and click efficiency, not profitability. Decide on scaling only by comparing ROAS and cost per purchase against the breakeven and desired values. [L0445.028]
- A high CTR does not prove a good ad when no purchases follow. [L1426.015]
- Judging hook benchmarks from two different sets without naming which one you used. Pick one set, state it, and apply it consistently. [L0613.052]
- Reading the CTR (all) figure as link CTR when comparing against benchmarks. Confirm the variant. [L0874.019]
- Treating a low CPM as proof of a low CPC. CPC also depends on CTR. [L0774.030]
- Chasing the lowest possible bid rather than better ad quality. [L1031.035]
- Using an arithmetic error from a source as a benchmark. In one lesson the figure of about $33,000 for 500,000 impressions at a $15 CPM is wrong; the correct cost is $7,500. [L0871.032]

#### Further details

- Click-through rate = clicks / impressions. An impression is counted each time the ad is shown, so a low result signals that the ad, image or price is not attracting clicks. [L1002.050]

- In the book niche, sustainable CPC and CPM sit around $0.80 to about $2. Costs in this range keep early tests affordable. [L1175.031]

## Setting up Google tracking: Analytics, Search Console, Merchant Center, Ads

### Same Google login for every service

- Use the store's Google Workspace (business) email for Google Analytics, Search Console, Merchant Center and Google Ads, never a personal address. Log in with it before starting any Google service setup. It prevents permission problems, makes linking far easier, and lets the properties share access. [L0169.001, L0667.042, L0722.003, L0723.004]
- Older advice (2021): with the same email for Analytics, Ads and Search Console, site ownership verifies immediately. [L0962.114]
- A personal-email account and a business-email account for the same service are a common reason data and audiences go missing. Check that every linked account number is the intended one. [L1204.008, L1204.055]
- The store build team usually creates these accounts and links them. Link Google Analytics, Google Ads and Merchant Center to the store, and when the setup offers GA4 alongside the pixel, connect both platforms. [L0667.043]

### What Google Analytics reports and why to set it up early

- Google Analytics is free. It reports visitor numbers, traffic sources and countries, pages viewed, time spent on each page, general behaviour patterns, which products and traffic sources produce revenue, and where buyers were referred from. [L0032.003, L0169.002, L0722.004, L0723.001, L0964.073]
- It measures the return on Google Ads spending. [L0032.003]
- Older advice (2021-22): Analytics records visits, sales and user behaviour. It can only analyse what is fed into it, so every connected source should send data to it. [L0726.002]
- Set up analytics before traffic arrives, even if you do not analyse the data yet. Data then accumulates for future use and for integration with Google Ads, Search Console and Merchant Center. From the first visitors you can see which sources, pages and products work and which need improvement. [L0169.003, L0722.004, L0723.002]
- Analytics attributes each sale to the product, the order amount and the traffic source. Example: a $1,000 sale shows what was bought, the $1,000 spent and where the buyer came from. Review revenue by source and by product over time to decide which traffic to increase and which pages to improve. [L0723.020]
- Connecting Analytics to Shopify attributes every sale to its traffic source, so you can see which channels are profitable and which are not. [L0597.006]
- Google Tag Manager manages tracking tags. It is set up together with Google Analytics and GA4 so that all tracking runs from one place. It does not replace analytics. [L1288.010, L1213.069]

### Account, property and data stream

- The hierarchy is account, then property, then data stream. One account can hold several properties, and one property is enough for one store. [L0723.007]
- An account represents the business and a property represents one website. Use one property per store unless the business runs several websites under one parent company. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0726.009]
- Older advice (2018): one Google account can manage several stores, each added as a separate property, and you switch between them with the drop-down menu at the top left. [L0466.058]
- A data stream is the source that sends information to Analytics. For a store without an app, select the Web stream type and enter the store URL. [L0722.007]
- Account, property and data stream names are internal labels that nobody else sees. They can match the store name but do not need to match the URL. [L0723.005]
- Enter the URL of the live store, not a development or test version. Google must be able to reach the site to collect data. [L0722.012]
- Time zone and currency should match the business location. Example in the course: United States, Eastern time, US dollar. [L0723.009]
- The Analytics currency must match the currency the store takes payments in. Otherwise e-commerce sales are reported in another currency and will not match the store's own figures. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0726.010]
- If the store already has a Google Analytics account, skip sign-up and account creation. Connect the existing GA4 property to Shopify and choose the property that belongs to this store from the drop-down. [L0723.019]

### Procedure A: GA4 account and Shopify connection through the Google & YouTube app (24 steps)

**Procedure A: create the GA4 account and connect it to Shopify. Source: Source A, undated.** [L0169.004]

1. Go to analytics.google.com
2. Click Start Measuring
3. For Account name, enter your store name (for your reference only)
4. Leave all sharing settings checked
5. Click Next
6. For Property name, enter your domain name (e.g., yourstore.com)
7. Select your correct time zone
8. Confirm the currency is correct
9. Click Next
10. For Industry category, select Shopping
11. For Business size, select Small (1-10 employees)
12. For Business objectives, select Drive sales and Understand web traffic (you can select multiple)
13. Click Create and accept Google's terms
14. Choose Web as the data source
15. Enter your website URL
16. Name the data stream (internal reference only)
17. Leave Enhanced Measurement turned ON
18. Click Create and Continue
19. When Google detects your Shopify site, click Next to install the Shopify integration
20. In Shopify Admin, search for the Google & YouTube app
21. Install the app on the free plan
22. Copy your Measurement ID / Tag ID from your Google Analytics account
23. Paste it into the Google & YouTube app when prompted
24. Use the Shopify/Google video tutorial (Option 1) to complete the connection

### Procedure B: GA4 account and web data stream (19 steps)

**Procedure B: create a GA4 account and web data stream for a Shopify store. Source: Source A, undated.** [L0722.001]

1. Open analytics.google.com while signed in with the Google Workspace account of the store.
2. Click Start Measuring.
3. Enter an account name. It is only for your reference.
4. Under the sharing settings, keep all four options checked.
5. Click Next.
6. Enter a property name (required). Naming it after the website domain is a sensible choice.
7. Set the time zone to the time zone where you operate.
8. Check that the currency matches the currency your shop uses, and change it if the default differs.
9. Click Next.
10. Under business details, choose Shopping as the industry.
11. Choose small, one to 10 employees as the business size.
12. Click Next.
13. Under business objectives, tick Drive sales and Understand web and/or app traffic. Ticking more options does not change the account.
14. Click Create.
15. Read the Google Terms of Service, accept them, and accept the policies.
16. On the data collection screen, choose Web, because no app is involved.
17. Enter the store URL and name the data stream. Only you will see the name.
18. Keep Enhanced measurements switched on with all default items enabled.
19. Click Create and Continue.

Notes to Procedure B:
- Keep all four sharing options checked. [L0722.005]
- Keep Enhanced measurements on with all default tracking items enabled. [L0722.006]

### Procedure C: GA4 account and link to Shopify (12 steps)

**Procedure C: create a GA4 account and link it to Shopify, in this order. Source: Source A, undated.** [L0723.003]

1. Open the Google Analytics sign-up page and click Get started today. Only do this if the store does not already have a Google Analytics account.
2. Sign in with the business email address.
3. Enter the account name. It is internal only, so the store name is fine.
4. Leave data sharing enabled and click Next.
5. Enter the property name, for example the store name.
6. Set the reporting time zone to the place where the business is based and set the currency (the example uses US Eastern time and US dollar).
7. Do not select the Universal Analytics option under advanced options. Skip the advanced options and continue.
8. Fill in the business information: Industry category Shopping, business size usually 1 to 10 employees for a small store, and any intended-use options that apply. These answers only inform Google and do not change the account.
9. Leave App installs unchecked if the store has no app.
10. Click Create, check both boxes for the Google terms of service and click I accept.
11. Choose the platform Web, enter the store URL (with www, for example www.example.com), enter an internal stream name, keep Enhanced measurement on, and click Create stream.
12. Do not use the manual installation code shown in the pop-up. Connect the stream to Shopify through the Google channel instead.

Notes to Procedure C:
- Keep data sharing enabled so that Google platforms added later can exchange data with each other. [L0723.006]
- Industry category Shopping and business size 1 to 10 employees for a small store. Leave App installs off when there is no mobile app. [L0723.010]
- Keep Enhanced measurement on. It tracks events such as page views and other interactions automatically. [L0723.011]
- Do not install Analytics on Shopify with the manual code from the pop-up. [L0723.012]
- Do not create a Universal Analytics property from the advanced options. Create only the GA4 property. [L0723.008]

### Business size and objectives: two versions

- For a small store, use Small (1-10 employees). Source: Procedures A, B and C. [L0169.004, L0722.001, L0723.010]
- Older advice (2021-22): the course set business size to Small to medium and selected these intended uses: measure customer engagement, optimize site, optimize advertising cost, increase conversions and analyze online sales. [L0726.008]
- Business objectives in the current procedures: Drive sales and Understand web traffic (A), or Drive sales and Understand web and/or app traffic (B). Ticking more options does not change the account. [L0169.004, L0722.001]

### Older method: Universal Analytics plus GA4 (2021-22)

Do not use this method now. Universal Analytics stopped processing data in July 2023. The steps are kept for reference.

**Older procedure, 2021-22: create a GA4 and a Universal Analytics property at once.** [L0726.008]

1. Open Google Analytics, click Start measuring and enter the business name as the account name.
2. Leave the default account data-sharing options unchanged.
3. Name the property after the website. One account can hold several properties, but a single store needs only one.
4. Set the reporting time zone to the location of the business.
5. Set the currency to the currency of the country you sell in.
6. Click Show advanced options.
7. Select Create both a Google Analytics 4 property and a Universal Analytics property. This was the recommended method in the 2021 setup.
8. Enter the base website URL.
9. Click Next. Set the business size to Small to medium and select the intended uses: measure customer engagement, optimize site, optimize advertising cost, increase conversions and analyze online sales.
10. Select your country, tick the terms of service box and accept.
11. Close the data stream setup screen without selecting any options. They are not needed at this stage.

Older notes (2021-22):
- Creating both properties at once creates two properties with connected site tags, so the website is tagged only once. Universal Analytics measures websites only, not apps. [L0726.026]
- In 2021 GA4 was new and integrated poorly with Shopify, which is why both properties were created. [L0726.011]
- Older e-commerce path (2021-22): Admin, then the View column, then Ecommerce Settings. Turn on Enable Ecommerce and enhanced e-commerce reporting, then save. Without this, Analytics does not record sales data. [L0726.014]

### Connecting Analytics to Shopify (Google & YouTube app)

- The Google & YouTube app is used only to connect Google Analytics. Do not use it to connect Merchant Center or Google Ads through its basic setup, because those need different setup with more control. If either is connected automatically, disconnect it in the app Settings. [L0169.005]
- Newer course (2024): the app can also be used for Google Ads conversion tracking, and it tracks all sales in GA4. The 2023 rule above is therefore a stricter version of the same setup. [L0856.096, L0856.097]
- Do not use the advanced manual setup option when connecting Analytics through the app. Always use the Shopify/Google video tutorial (Option 1 only) so the Measurement ID / Tag ID is configured properly. [L0169.006]
- Paste the Measurement ID into the Analytics field of the store and save. Shopify then adds the tracking code, so no theme or Liquid edits are needed for basic Analytics tracking. [L0391.013]
- The YouTube option is not needed. Only the Google option needs to be on if you do not use YouTube. [L0835.087]
- A Google sales channel can show a calendar icon in Shopify. This is normal and only means the app is installed. [L0835.088]
- GA4 is connected through the Google & YouTube app, so no extra tagging is needed to track sales. If test sales do not appear, check the GA4 connection. [L1287.063]

**Check the connection: Shopify Admin, Sales Channels, Google & YouTube.** [L0169.007]

1. In Shopify Admin, go to Sales Channels > Google & YouTube
2. Verify that Google Analytics shows as Active
3. Verify that Google Ads shows as Inactive
4. Verify that Merchant Center shows as Not connected
5. If Google Ads or Merchant Center are connected, open app Settings and disconnect them

A correct connection shows Google and YouTube under Sales Channels, with Google Analytics active, Google Ads inactive and Merchant Center not connected. [L0722.011]

### Enhanced ecommerce and data sharing

**Enhanced ecommerce through Shopify (newer method).** [L0391.008]

1. In Shopify, go to Online Store > Preferences and open the Google Analytics section.
2. Click to create a new Google Analytics account and enter the store URL and a category.
3. Under data sharing, keep all options checked: early access to data, new features, Google Analytics specialist debug access and Google Ads linking.
4. Accept the terms of service and copy the generated Google Analytics measurement ID.
5. Paste it into the Google Analytics field in Shopify and save.
6. Mark the Enhanced Ecommerce option in Shopify and save again.
7. In Google Analytics, link the property to the Google Ads account using the same email; name the link after the store and share all views.
8. Enable demographics and interests reporting, enable remarketing and advanced reporting features, and turn on individual user ID tracking under tracking info data collection; save each change.

- Keep all data-sharing checkboxes active: early data access, new features, Analytics specialist access for debugging, and sharing with Google Ads. Beta features and debugging support then stay available. [L0391.009]
- With Enhanced Ecommerce switched on in the Shopify integration, sessions, product views, add-to-cart events, checkout steps and transactions are tracked automatically, with no custom code. [L0391.010]
- Confirm that Use Enhanced Ecommerce is ticked in the Shopify Analytics field and that the connection records ecommerce data. [L1145.027]
- Older method (2020): in Analytics, open the gear icon for Admin, then under your website's data open E-commerce settings. Toggle on Enable e-commerce and Enable enhanced e-commerce reporting, then Save. In the Shopify back end, open the Google Analytics settings and check Use enhanced e-commerce. [L0852.071]
- Conflict to know: the older method (2020, 2021-22) uses the Universal Analytics path, while the newer method switches on Enhanced Ecommerce in Shopify and tracks automatically in GA4. Use the newer method for a new store. [L0852.071, L0726.014, L0391.010]
- Older advice (2021-22): Enhanced Ecommerce is only available after upgrading to the latest version of Google Analytics. [L0370.020]
- Do not use an ID that starts with UA-. It belongs to Universal Analytics, which stopped processing data in July 2023, so it no longer records sales. Connect a GA4 property and verify that it captures purchases. (Older advice in the 2021-22 source, still valid.) [L1145.028]
- Before launch, Analytics must be connected to Shopify and ecommerce tracking must be active. The traffic-value and ad-performance decisions depend on this data. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L1145.029]

### Data collection, retention and demographics

- Set Data settings > Data collection: turn on every data collection option and accept the user data collection acknowledgement. This passes Analytics data to Google Ads at the highest sharing level. [L1520.004]
- Set Data settings > Data retention: change Event data retention from the default of 2 months to 14 months, then click Save. The longer period lets audiences draw on a longer user history. [L1520.005]
- Mistake: leaving Event data retention at 2 months limits how far back user data is kept, so audiences built on it are smaller. Raise it to 14 months before creating remarketing audiences. [L1520.006]
- Demographics and interests reporting can be enabled under data collection. It shows age, gender and interest groups, which can be used as audience ideas in Google Ads. [L0391.011]
- Older path (2021-22): Admin > Property Settings > Tracking Info > Data Collection. Turn on the option that collects additional data, which enables demographics and interest reporting for other ads, such as Google search text ads. Click Save. [L1390.026]
- Individual user ID tracking is switched on under tracking info data collection settings, then saved. It lists the behaviour of individual users on the site, not only aggregated sessions. [L0391.014]
- Enhanced measurement is on by default and tracks events such as page views automatically. Keep it on with all default items enabled. [L0723.011, L0722.006, L0169.004]
- Older caution (2021-22): enhanced measurement automatically records interactions such as link clicks and embedded video plays. Make sure no personally identifiable information is sent to Google through these page elements. The setting can be left on or switched off in the property details. [L0726.025]

### Universal Analytics sunset and migration

- Universal Analytics stopped processing data in July 2023. A new store needs a GA4 property connected through Shopify's GA4 option. [L0726.011, L0934.080, L0934.122, L0964.072, L1092.080, L1213.070]
- Google Analytics 4 replaces Universal Analytics. New tracking must be set up in GA4. (Source dated 2022.) [L1092.080]
- The GA4 setup in Shopify should use Shopify's native setting, not custom code. The course described this as the expected approach from March 2023 onward. [L0934.080, L0934.122]
- Earlier, the course expected the Shopify update for the direct integration in March 2023, after which the setup would be a few clicks. [L0964.071]
- Migration time differs by source: about 33 minutes for Shopify accounts (2023) [L0855.036], and about 45 minutes to an hour when you follow a step-by-step process alongside your own setup (2023) [L0970.150]. Both are course estimates.
- Mistake: do not plan around an older timeline. The speaker expected removal around mid-2023, but Universal Analytics stopped processing data in July 2023, and GA4 is the current property. [L1213.070]
- Mistake: do not create a Universal Analytics property from advanced options. [L0723.008]

### Linking Analytics and Google Ads

- Link the property to Google Ads with the same email, name the link after the store and share all views. [L0391.008]
- Linking shares data in both directions and shares remarketing lists between the platforms, so audiences do not need to be built separately in each one. [L0391.012]
- Linking Google Ads and Analytics before launch gives Analytics the Google Ads data it needs to value traffic and measure ad performance. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L1145.049]

**Link Google Ads and Google Analytics so audiences can be shared. Source: Source B, 2021-22 (older).** [L1390.025]

1. Open Admin in Google Analytics.
2. In the property column, go to Product Linking and open Google Ads Linking.
3. Check whether the accounts are already linked; if not, link them.
4. Turn on auto-tagging as part of the link.

**Checking audiences that are missing from Google Ads. Source: Source A, 2023.** [L0934.081]

1. Open the Analytics account and click the gear icon (Admin) at the bottom left.
2. Under Audience definitions, choose Audiences.
3. Confirm the audience is listed.
4. Check that the Google Ads account is linked to Analytics.
5. Under audience destinations, click Edit and select the correct Google Ads account.
6. In Google Ads, under shared audiences, remove any filters.
7. If it still does not appear, create a couple more audiences to check whether the issue is specific to one audience.

### Linking Search Console

**Link Search Console to Google Analytics. Source: Source A, undated.** [L0736.011]

1. Log in to Google Analytics with the same email used for Search Console.
2. Click the gear icon (Admin) at the bottom left.
3. Scroll down to the Product links column in the middle.
4. Click Search Console links.
5. Click Link.
6. Click Choose accounts and select the correct Search Console property.
7. Click Next, Next, Next, then Review and submit (Confirm).
8. Click Save.

- After linking, organic search data flows into Analytics reports, including which search keywords led to sales. [L0736.012]
- Search Console reports only organic search results. Paid search traffic appears in Analytics when you filter by traffic source. (Source dated 2022, older.) [L1113.066]

### Google Ads conversion tracking

**Principles**

- Proper conversion and remarketing tracking enables accurate purchase attribution, ad-level profitability reporting, remarketing audiences of past visitors, and full visibility across Analytics and Google Ads. [L0184.009]
- Conversion tracking connects website purchases to Google Ads campaigns, so ad spend is linked to real returns. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0731.033]
- Conversion tracking tells you which ads are profitable. Without it, ad optimisation is guesswork. (Older advice, 2020.) [L0852.073]
- Conversion tags let Google Ads see visits, add-to-cart events and purchases from ads. Linking Analytics to Google Ads also shows in Analytics which sources generate sales. [L0718.011]
- Once conversion tracking is active, Google Ads reports which sales came from Shopping ads. [L1165.018]

**What counts as a conversion**

- A conversion in Google Ads can be defined as anything: product view, add-to-cart click, page views, time spent on site or repeat visitor. Check what the account actually tracks. Google's smart goals and automated conversions may not represent actual sales. [L0327.009]
- Google Ads counts every action marked as a conversion, including page views, add-to-cart and initiate checkout. This can distort cost per conversion. Review all conversion actions and count only the intended ones, such as purchase. [L0470.002]
- Older advice (2021-22): create one purchase conversion action, because only purchases need to be tracked. Conversions appear under Tools and settings > Conversions. [L0731.032]
- The conversion tag on the thank you page records a conversion each time a visitor reaches it. It uses cookies to associate the purchase with the original traffic source and campaign. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0256.002]

**Simprosys app for Shopify (Source A, undated)**

**Procedure: set up conversion tracking for Google Ads with the Simprosys app.** [L0718.010]

1. In the Shopify store open the Simprosys app and log in.
2. Hover over Tracking Tags and click Google dynamic remarketing and conversion tracking.
3. Run the app's wizard, first connecting the newly created Google Ads account by selecting it from the list of accounts.
4. Confirm the connected account details shown from Google Ads.
5. Answer yes when asked whether to set up the Google Ads tracking tags; the app then creates them in the Google accounts automatically.
6. Check that the tags show as unverified at first and change to active once traffic reaches the site.

- The Google Ads account must already exist before the wizard runs, because the first step connects an existing account. Creating it later would require repeating the tag setup. [L0718.016]
- After the wizard is completed with the tag option set to yes, the app creates the tags itself. The merchant does not need to build them manually or revisit this setup. [L0718.017]
- Newly created tags stay unverified until real traffic arrives; an unverified status right after setup is normal. [L0718.012]
- Conflict on verification: in the 2021-22 course the tag stays unverified until the first sale is made on the website, and that sale verifies it. (Older.) [L0731.033] In the Simprosys procedure above, the tags become active once traffic reaches the site. [L0718.010]

**Feed app for Google Shopping (Source A, undated)**

**Procedure: set up conversion tracking in Feed for Google Shopping.** [L1165.017]

1. Open the feed app and hover over the tracking tags link at the top.
2. Choose Google dynamic remarketing and conversion tracking.
3. If no Google Ads account is linked yet, click the button to link one. If the wrong account is linked, click change and choose the correct one.
4. On the permissions screen, click allow so the app can manage Google Ads campaigns, product listings and accounts, and the domains you control.
5. In the drop-down, select the Google Ads account for this store and click link your account.
6. Under what needs to be included in the script, make sure dynamic remarketing, conversion tracking, add to cart tracking and begin checkout tracking are all selected.
7. Click update conversion tags. If the account is new, the button may say create conversion tags; click it and the tags will be created.

- Tags installed through the app keep working even if the Shopify theme is changed. The app route is considered the more reliable and simpler option. [L1165.020]
- Manual setup is also possible by adding the Google remarketing tag to the Shopify theme yourself, following Google Ads support instructions. It works but is more work and more error-prone. [L1165.021]
- Mistake: if the site already has a Google dynamic remarketing tag, remove it before using the app's tracking. Keeping both tracks every sale twice. [L1165.019]
- If conversion tracking was set up through a tracking app, do not add the tag to the site manually. A warning about no conversions can appear simply because no conversion has been recorded since the tag was added. [L1115.103]

**Older feed-app methods**

- Older (2020): install the Feed for Google Shopping app from the Shopify App Store, open the app settings, scroll to Google remarketing and conversion tracking, click in to link your Google account, which adds the conversion tracking pixel. Use the reported data to see where sales come from inside Google. [L0852.072]
- Older (2021-22): in the feed app, click Google dynamic remarketing and conversion tracking, enable only Dynamic remarketing and Conversion tracking, not the other two options. Log in to Google Ads, allow access and link the account so the app inserts the tags into the site. [L0731.031]
- Older (2021-22): add the Google Ads conversion tag through the Feeds for Google Shopping app so Google Ads can record the sales and conversions from your traffic. [L1145.047]

**Remarketing tag code (Source B, 2021-22, older)**

```html
<!-- Google Ads global site tag: replace AW-XXXXXXXXX with your Google Ads ID -->
<script async src='https://www.googletagmanager.com/gtag/js?id=AW-XXXXXXXXX'></script>
<script>
  window.dataLayer = window.dataLayer || [];
  function gtag{dataLayer.push(arguments);}
  gtag('js', new Date);
  gtag('config', 'AW-XXXXXXXXX');
</script>

<!-- Remarketing page_view event: replace value and item id per page -->
<script>
  gtag('event', 'page_view', {
    'send_to': 'AW-XXXXXXXXX',
    'value': 'replace with value',
    'items': [{
      'id': 'replace with value',
      'google_business_vertical': 'retail'
    }]
  });
</script>
```

[L1390.043]

### Checking tags with Tag Assistant

- Google Tag Assistant is a Chrome extension that shows which Google tags and conversions fire on each page. Use it to confirm conversion tracking is set up correctly. (Older advice, 2021.) [L0247.115]
- It is used to check that the Google Ads remarketing tag, the global site tag and the Analytics code are live on the store. Refresh the store page after activation to confirm all tags load. [L0391.007]
- Tag Assistant is a tool for debugging and verifying that Google tracking tags are installed correctly on the store and fire on customer actions. [L0032.004]

### Merchant Center: linking, reports and rules

**Linking and performance**

- Link Merchant Center and Google Ads to Analytics so data is shared, and check that the linked account numbers are the correct ones. [L1204.008]
- Google's Top Seller rating appears to be achieved by meeting the score thresholds shown in Merchant Center. Applying to the program is recommended to check eligibility. A 50-review threshold was mentioned for one Google form. (Source dated 2024.) [L0666.044]

**Shopping Actions reports (Source B, 2021-22, older)** [L0738.026]

| Report | Fields |
|---|---|
| Today's Snapshot | Pending shipment, Missing tracking information, Wrong tracking information, Unshipped orders, Item fill rate, Ordered item sales, Orders, AOV, Returns (last 24 hours) |
| Sales & Orders | Ordered item sales, Orders, AOV, segmented by product, brand or category |
| Returns | Returns, Returned items |
| Operations | Shipped orders, Days to ship, Shipped items sales, Shipped items, Item fill rate, Unshipped orders, Unshipped items, Rejected items |

- Example (older, one 2020 account): diagnostics showed 549 active items (88%), 0 expiring items and 77 disapproved items (12%) out of 626 submitted items. Disapproval rates like this show how much of the feed is still eligible. [L0738.029]

**Compliance, feed and shipping rules (Source A, 2022, older)**

- Following the compliance steps greatly reduces suspension risk. Still, a Merchant Center account can be suspended at any time, for example after a site change, a hack, or a crawler misclassifying a page. [L0963.023]
- If an initial batch of feed products is approved and no suspension appears, the rest can be uploaded. Keep running the business normally while uploading. [L0963.024]
- Do not run the Shopify Google sales channel alongside Feed for Google Shopping or another feed app that submits to Merchant Center, because they conflict. If you try the Google channel, disconnect Feed for Google Shopping first. [L0963.027]
- The Google channel asks for fields such as GTIN. The same fields are in the feed settings of Feed for Google Shopping, which may leave less to chance. [L0963.028]
- Made-to-order products with long lead times, such as 17 weeks, can be listed as custom products. The estimated shipping time can be set in days directly in Merchant Center. [L0963.029]
- Final-sale products should be set up as an exception in Merchant Center. State on the product page and on the shipping and returns policy page that these products are not eligible for return. [L0963.030]
- Shipping settings in Merchant Center must match the store's shipping exactly. Mismatches cause rejected products and suspension errors. [L0963.031]
- For a postcode-based shipping setup, for example an Australian free-shipping rule for remote areas, ask Shopify live chat how to mirror it in Merchant Center. Do not guess the configuration. [L0963.032]

**Merchant Center resets (Source A, 2022, older)**

**Procedure: reset a stuck Merchant Center account, only after the compliance issues are fully fixed.** [L0963.025]

1. Remove the feed and all store information linked to the account.
2. Delete the Merchant Center account.
3. Create a new Merchant Center account.
4. Upload the feed again and see whether it passes the filters. Do not apply this reset to the Google Ads account.

- If Google Ads is fine but Merchant Center stays stuck for a long time without help, another Merchant Center account can be linked to the Google Ads account. [L0963.026]

### Microsoft Advertising UET tag and conversions

**Procedure: set up the Microsoft UET tag and conversion tracking via the Shopify feed app, in this order. Source: Source A, undated.** [L1036.006]

1. In the feed app header menu, open Microsoft UET tag and conversion goal setup.
2. Click Let's get started.
3. Confirm that you want conversion tracking for Microsoft Advertising.
4. Before continuing, remove any previously added conversion tracking scripts (UET or other) from the Shopify Additional scripts section, since duplicates can conflict.
5. Leave all tracking options checked on a new account.
6. Click Continue.
7. Under the Microsoft Advertising account, choose Create a new tag rather than an existing tag so the app creates the tags automatically.
8. Verify the status shows conversion tracking as active for Dynamic Remarketing, Add to Cart and Begin Checkout.

- The UET tag inserted by the app runs through Shopify scripts, so it keeps working when the theme is changed or replaced. Remove existing UET or other conversion scripts first to avoid duplicate tracking. [L1036.007]
- The automatically created Microsoft conversion types are Dynamic Remarketing, Add to Cart and Begin Checkout. The app generates the tags and their names when Create new tag is chosen. [L1036.008]
- Mistake: choosing an existing UET tag instead of creating a new one can be an error in a new setup, because the app can create and configure the full set of tags automatically. [L1036.009]

### Troubleshooting and verification
