### Seasonality, volatility and red days

- Mixed green and red days are normal, so do not kill a campaign on one red day. [L1195.051]
- When a campaign has a red day, pump out more creatives, sharpen the messaging through research, and keep CBO 2 and CBO 3 fed so the system can spend and learn. [L1195.052]
- Do not judge a TikTok test on one day of data; give ad groups at least two days. [L1377.005]
- Single days can be off, so spread the spend over three to four days rather than one day when testing an offer or ad. [L1315.025]
- Cut a product if demand is weak, the messaging does not work, or the market is saturated in Meta Ad Library, but only after testing for at least three days. [L1195.050]
- Per-country differences can drag down the overall CTR, so check creatives by country before deciding. [L1432.021]

## Diagnosing the store funnel: conversion, bounce and on-site behavior

### Verify tracking and data before you judge

- Record how many visitors arrive per day, how many of them are organic, and where they come from before you diagnose a conversion rate. [L0663.016] (2019, older)
- If traffic is high but sales are low, verify the numbers first. Example: 2,000 visits (1,500 from Google Ads and 500 direct) should produce dozens of sales at a healthy conversion rate. [L0663.072] (2019, older)
- Blended conversion rates from Shopify or Google Analytics are misleading because they include bot traffic. [L0249.079] (2022, older)
- Blended store conversion rate also mixes bot traffic, organic searches and repeat visits, so measure each paid channel separately, such as Google Shopping, Facebook Dynamic Remarketing and Google Remarketing. [L0272.069] (2022, older)
- The conversion rate in the default Shopify dashboard blends all sources and understates the performance of controllable sources, especially Google Ads. [L1109.047] (2018, older)
- Track add-to-cart, checkout and purchase rates in Shopify to find the exact step where shoppers leave. Dropshipping is largely about reading these numbers after each change. [L0388.043]
- Shopify shows average order value (AOV), add-to-cart percentage, initiate-checkout percentage and session conversion rate in the Analytics section of the dashboard. [L0445.008, L0875.008]
- Add-to-cart percentage shows how well the landing page and offer work, because it measures how many visitors take the first purchase step. A weak value points to a page or offer problem before the ads are blamed. [L0445.011, L0875.009]
- Store analytics show which products customers view, add to cart, buy and ask about. Google Ads and Google Analytics data are the most valuable sources for understanding real customer behavior. [L1446.012]
- Shopify analytics show website visitors and the pages they open, so no extra analytics tool is needed at the start. [L1304.007]
- Review Google Analytics data about every two weeks to track performance trends and make data-driven optimization decisions. [L0293.001]
- When diagnosing a store with few sales, compare Google Analytics traffic week over week or month over month since launch. This shows whether the problem is traffic volume or conversion. [L0807.042]
- Analyze traffic by source over 30 days, such as paid Shopping, organic social, organic search, referrals and unassigned, to find where sales come from before changing anything. [L0855.037]
- Review two numbers in analytics: where traffic goes (most visited pages) and where sales come from (source attribution). Together they show which pages to optimize and which channels to scale. [L0972.056]
- When judging a store, focus first on high-intent actions: add to cart, initiate checkout and completed purchase. Traffic source matters mainly when you diagnose a tracking problem or spot unwanted traffic. [L0363.081]
- Conversion rate was the top priority for merchants in the audience poll, but AOV and customer lifetime value should be tracked alongside it when optimizing a cart. [L1231.003]
- Track revenue per visitor and per sale. A store with about 8,711 visitors and only 8 sales is a warning sign that ad spend is not reaching buyers. [L0854.096] (2022, older)
- Unverified industry figures are often quoted: average cart abandonment around 70%, and roughly 70% of website traffic eventually returns to the homepage. Use them only as rough benchmarks and compare against your own checkout and analytics data. [L1208.012]

### Sample size and traffic bursts

- Judge a store funnel only with at least 150 to 200 visitors; 50 to 100 visitors is too little data to conclude anything. [L1345.006]
- Judge a store's conversion rate only after about 100 to 200 or more visitors per day; 30 to 50 visitors are too few for a reliable conclusion. [L1377.044]
- If traffic is below that level, run ads for two more days before changing the landing page. [L1377.044]
- Check the store after 100 to 150 visitors. If add-to-cart is still low, change the offer; if it stays low after several offers, switch to the next product. [L0363.042]
- Ad platforms send an initial traffic burst when a campaign starts. Remove it from the visitor count before calculating add-to-cart rates. [L1345.007]
- If the burst cannot be separated exactly, an approximation based on the timing of the burst is acceptable. [L1345.007]
- Launch ads six to seven hours before the planned running start time, so the initial burst happens at a known moment and can be excluded. Once the burst is identified, all other visits count as genuine visitors. [L1345.008]

### Core store rates and benchmarks

**Formulas**

- Add-to-cart rate = add-to-cart events / landing page visitors (sessions that reach the product page). [L0361.003]
- Example: 100 visitors and 4 add-to-carts gives 4%, which is the minimum benchmark. [L0361.003, L0361.002]
- Per Session Value (PSV) = revenue / sessions. [L0389.018]
- Average Order Value (AOV) = revenue / transactions. [L0389.018]
- Conversion rate = transactions / sessions. [L0389.018]
- Check: $57,286 / 50,970 sessions = $1.12 PSV, and $57,286 / 1,019 transactions = $56.22 AOV, which matches the control figures in the case. [L0389.018]
- Revenue per session is the metric that matters most in conversion rate optimization, more than conversion rate alone. A test that raises conversion rate can still be unprofitable if it attracts lower-spending customers or lowers AOV. Measure every A/B test against revenue-per-session improvement. [L0197.025]

**Benchmarks for add-to-cart and conversion**

| Metric | Benchmark | Applies to | Source ids |
|---|---|---|---|
| Add-to-cart rate, minimum | At least 4% of landing page visitors | Any store; anything clearly below is a very bad sign | L0361.002 |
| Add-to-cart rate, winning product | About 4 to 5% of store traffic | A product that is already winning | L0773.004, L0774.006 |
| Add-to-cart rate, product page | About 5% of visitors is a reasonable benchmark | Product page check | L1216.004 |
| Initiate-checkout rate, winning product | About 4 to 5% of all traffic | A product that is already winning | L0773.004, L0774.006 |
| Store conversion rate | About 2% to 3% is ideal depending on industry | General e-commerce | L0640.012 |
| Store conversion rate, dropshipping | Above 4% is good | Dropshipping | L0640.012 |
| Paid channel conversion | 2.5% or more for Google Shopping, Facebook Dynamic Remarketing and Google Remarketing | Each paid channel separately (2022, older) | L0272.069 |
| Product page conversion | Above 1.5% is the target | A product page getting clicks | L1172.033 |
| Facebook traffic conversion (practice) | Roughly the same percentage as CTR, so 1.5% CTR leads to roughly 1.5% conversion | Rough practice benchmark | L1017.015 |
| Example store | About 100 visitors per day gave 3 to 4 sales per day, roughly 3 to 4% conversion | Example only | L0372.002 |

- In dropshipping, the add-to-cart rate should be roughly double the purchase conversion rate because shipping times are long. [L0640.012]
- Use the benchmarks as a guide for where to look, not as a verdict on their own. Each benchmark is valid only with enough traffic behind it. [L1345.006, L1377.044]
- A slow traffic week with a healthy conversion rate points to a traffic problem, not a product or checkout problem. [L0372.002]
- A store with 20,000 visits and no sales has a conversion issue on the site rather than an ad issue. [L1225.069]
- Strong top-of-funnel traffic with no add-to-carts usually means the bottleneck is the website. [L1225.069]

### Reading the funnel stage by stage

- Diagnose a weak store in this order: ads shown, clicks, visits, add-to-cart, checkout starts and completed purchases. [L0471.065]
- The largest drop between two stages shows where the problem sits. Fix that stage before changing anything else. [L0471.065]
- Example: many checkout starts with no purchases points to checkout or trust issues rather than to ad targeting. [L0471.065]
- Analyze the funnel in this order: pre-click ad metrics, then website and landing page metrics, then checkout, then customer support and supply chain. If the ads perform well, the problem is usually further down the funnel. [L1260.002]
- Interpret the funnel in the Shopify report by checking sessions per landing page, add-to-cart rate and conversion rate. This locates the leak between ad click and purchase before you change creatives. [L0640.016]
- A typical funnel drop from ad click to add-to-cart to final checkout is about half at each stage. Shipping costs are the most common reason people leave at checkout. [L0640.017]
- Check funnel ratios per product and per country: CTR, CPC, CPM, conversion rate, add-to-cart ratio and initiate-checkout ratio. [L1185.018]
- The add-to-cart rate is driven mostly by what happens on the store after the click (site quality, product and offer), not by the traffic source alone. Evaluate it per product and per store rather than per ad platform. [L1508.012]
- The add-to-cart rate used to judge a product page is read in the Shopify backend. Check that the page structure is good enough before duplicating it. [L1194.013]
- In Business Manager, check add-to-cart and initiate-checkout rates per ad asset and compare them with the Shopify dashboard. A drop at one step shows where the funnel breaks. [L1017.023]
- A profitable funnel needs both parts healthy: strong Meta metrics (CPM, CTR, CPC, cost per purchase) and a converting website. A good website cannot compensate for expensive traffic, and cheap traffic cannot compensate for a website that does not convert. [L1354.018]

**Funnel leak table**

| Leak location | Indicator | Likely root cause | Action |
|---|---|---|---|
| Pre-click | High CPM and low CTR | Creative not resonating | New hooks or angles |
| Click to LPV | More than 30% drop | Slow page or accidental clicks | Fix page speed |
| LPV to ATC | Under 5% | Page does not match the ad | Improve message match |
| ATC to checkout | Under 30% | Cart friction | Improve cart experience |
| Checkout to purchase | Under 50% | Checkout abandonment | Simplify checkout |

[L0613.053]

**Procedure: funnel check with a 3-step order**

1. Check the number of visitors arriving at the store first. [L1508.024]
2. Check whether those visitors add products to the cart. [L1508.024]
3. Check whether people are buying. [L1508.024]
- Use these results to decide whether the price, the product and the website are converting, and look at all three together. [L1508.024]

**Procedure: diagnosing a failed campaign**

1. Check whether tracking is correct by comparing the ad platform numbers with the store analytics, for example Shopify. [L0361.009]
2. Check CTR and CPC. A very high CPC points to a creative or audience problem. [L0361.009]
3. Check the add-to-cart rate against the 4% benchmark. If it is low, the creative, offer or landing page is the suspect. [L0361.009, L0361.002]
4. If add-to-carts are fine, check the step from add-to-cart to checkout: shipping costs, trust signals, and whether the checkout works at all. [L0361.009]
5. If people reach checkout but do not buy, test the checkout yourself and review the purchase step. [L0361.009]

**Four-scenario diagnosis from the tracker**

1. Ads and website both healthy: all metrics in green, ROAS well above break-even, stable for 5 to 7 days. Action: scale. [L1354.019]
2. Ads and website both weak: high CPM, expensive clicks, no or few sales, everything in red for 5 to 7 days. Action: improve both parts of the funnel. [L1354.019]
3. Ads strong (good CPM, CTR and CPC) but website weak (few or no add-to-carts, low conversion rate such as 0.5%). The bottleneck is the website. [L1354.019]
4. Website strong but ads weak (high CPM, CPC near $6). The funnel is not scalable until traffic cost is fixed. [L1354.019]

**Examples from the same tracker**

- Optimal funnel: $100 daily Meta spend, ROAS of 4.5 (versus break-even 1.30), 10 add-to-carts, 3 purchases, and a cost per purchase below the $115 target. [L1354.022]
- Weak funnel: $100 daily spend with high CPM, traffic costs too high, some add-to-carts but few or no sales, and all values in the red. Action: fix both the ads and the website. [L1354.023]
- Website bottleneck: $100 spend with good CPM, CTR and CPC, but zero or very few sales and a conversion rate around 0.5%. Action: focus on the website, such as trust elements (testimonials), the chosen price point, and what stops people from buying. [L1354.024]
- Ads bottleneck: a website with many add-to-carts, purchases, a positive ROAS and a good conversion rate, but terrible Meta metrics such as high CPM and a cost per click near $6. Action: the funnel is not scalable until traffic cost is reduced, even though the website is good. [L1354.025]

### Add-to-cart and checkout drop-offs

- Check the funnel when add-to-carts are low or when there is a large drop between add-to-carts and initiate-checkouts. These drop-offs point to causes in the store or offer. [L0349.022]
- If add-to-carts are at or above 4% but there are zero sales, the problem lies after add-to-cart: checkout, shipping costs, trust or checkout setup. A problem in the checkout area is far more likely than a problem in the ad or product page. [L0361.007]
- Track add-to-carts alongside sales, because a store can show cart activity without enough purchases. In one example, four carts led to two sales, which pointed to the checkout experience and trust factors as the next place to look. [L0650.071]
- Add-to-cart and checkout activity can be high while sales are zero. The speaker suggested this is unusual and worth investigating. Add-to-cart to checkout conversion of around 71% was considered strong. [L0552.026]
- Use the 71% figure as the strong end of add-to-cart to checkout conversion. The leak table treats under 30% as cart friction, so below 30% is a clear problem and 71% is a clear strength. [L0552.026, L0613.053]
- When add-to-cart and checkout are both healthy and click-through is healthy too, but sales are still missing, the remaining problem is more likely the product or the ad angle. [L1382.072]
- Healthy add-to-cart and checkout rates suggest the landing page works, which moves the problem toward the offer, the creative or the product. [L1436.088]
- When CTR is high but add-to-cart and checkout ratios are low, the product page is failing to hold the visitor. Typical causes are an unattractive offer, a weak product description or any reason that makes visitors leave before adding to cart. [L0774.009]
- If ads drive add-to-cart activity but few visitors complete checkout, the problem may be the offer rather than the ad. Test a clearer or stronger offer first. [L0918.022]
- When an ad gets clicks but no add-to-carts or purchases, the page or checkout is a likely cause. Testing the same ad again after a fix helps separate an ad problem from a checkout or page problem. [L1426.016]
- A product page that gets clicks but does not convert needs work on the product page, price, bundles or copy. [L1172.033]
- If content views are far below link clicks, the landing page may load too slowly, so visitors leave before the product page registers. Content views should normally be somewhat lower than link clicks, and a big gap is a warning sign for site speed. [L1024.038]
- A good add-to-cart rate with zero sales points to a problem after the cart, not in the ad or the product. [L1216.004]
- Track each step against a benchmark before deciding. The stage with the largest gap to its benchmark is where you start. [L0471.065, L0613.053]

### Site versus ad: where the problem sits

**Core split between ad and store**

- Check CTR and CPM first in Facebook ads. High CTR with no sales points to the offer, not the ad. [L0519.028]
- Track CTR for ads and store conversion rate for the website separately. A good CTR with a low conversion rate points to the website or offer. A poor CTR points to the ad. [L0650.046]
- A strong CTR with no sales means the ad is working and the store or offer is the problem. [L1017.015]
- If CTR is at least 1.5% and CPC is below 1 USD but there are still no sales, the problem is the store. If CTR or CPC are off target, change the ads. [L1220.034]
- If ads reach a click-through rate above 1.5% but produce no add-to-carts or sales, the problem is likely the offer. Test different offers before changing anything else. [L1399.001]
- A good CTR of around 3% with many add-to-carts but no sales points to a problem after the add-to-cart step, such as currency or checkout. [L1185.018]
- After you have a good CTR and a good offer, check store conversion rate and then decide whether to scale or move on, in that order. [L0650.048]
- Apart from the creative, the only other lever under the advertiser's control is the post-click part: landing page and funnel. Improving the conversion of visitors into buyers is a way to win the same audience pocket as competitors. [L0639.012]
- When ad CTR and CPC are acceptable but sales are missing, check the add-to-cart and initial checkout rates on the store. [L1436.088]

**Hook, desire and interest**

- Diagnose a funnel problem in this order: first check that attention (hook rate) is strong. If clicks are good but sales are poor, the problem is in desire. [L0950.018]
- Possible causes of poor desire are an ad that does not sell the idea, unexpected fees, a price that is too high, a product page that does not sell well, a functional problem on the page, or a lack of trust. [L0950.018]
- A high click-through rate with poor sales shows real interest, so the next step is to fix the product page, checkout fees or trust signals rather than the ad. [L0950.019]

**Case: a launch with clicks and no sales**

- A product launched with a wow factor and a strong landing page got €190 in ad spend over four days without a single sale. Sessions were about 50 per day, then rose to 100 to 120 per day after new ad sets were added, but sales fell to zero. [L0950.025]
- Before the drop, the account had ROAS of about 2.2 and 4.4x return on ad spend, with a click-through rate of about 2 to 3%. [L0950.025]
- The first step was to check that the page and checkout functioned correctly and to use Lucky Orange to watch screen recordings. [L0950.025]

**Low conversion despite traffic**

1. Check which keywords are driving impressions and clicks in your Shopping campaign keyword report. [L0210.031]
2. Search Google for the product yourself and analyze what competitors show: messaging, offers and product photos. [L0210.031]
3. Compare your product page with competitor pages: pricing, descriptions, images, shipping and returns. [L0210.031]
4. Fix product page conversion elements, not just the ads. [L0210.031]

- A low conversion rate on a product page often means the page's traffic is at an awareness stage the page does not address. Check the traffic source and stage before changing the page itself. [L0481.016] (2021-2022, older)
- A high bounce rate on a high-traffic page can also point to a gap between what visitors expected and what the page shows. [L0473.019] (2021-2022, older)

**Unexplained sales drop**

1. Check whether the drop is seasonal or market-wide by comparing with previous years and with competitors. [L0949.028] (2021-2022, older)
2. Look at what competitors are doing, including new products, better offers and stronger traffic. [L0949.028] (2021-2022, older)
3. Check your own traffic, conversion and offer for changes, and fix what has shifted. [L0949.028] (2021-2022, older)
- When a profitable store turns unprofitable after months of work, identify the root cause with data before changing things. Persistent problems usually come from specific parts of the funnel. [L0963.143] (2022, older)
- Before you change the store, check whether a poor result is a traffic problem or a conversion problem by comparing against the previous period. [L0807.042]
- When asking a mentor or coach to diagnose weak ad performance, send screenshots of the ad results and screenshots of the Shopify analytics showing how visitors behave on the store. This lets the reviewer see whether the problem is in the ads or in the store. [L1347.040]

**Advertorial route (see also the advertorials section below)**

- Check how many visitors move from the advertorial to the offer page. [L1131.017]
- If that number is low, rewrite the advertorial copy to strengthen the argument. [L1131.017]
- Alternatively, split test sending ad traffic directly to the offer page against the advertorial route, and keep the stronger one. [L1131.017]

### Traffic source, country and product breakdowns

**Traffic source**

- Measure conversion rate by traffic source separately (Google Shopping, Facebook Remarketing and organic) rather than using blended figures. [L0249.079] (2022, older)
- Each paid channel should reach 2.5% or more conversion. These channels are Google Shopping, Facebook Dynamic Remarketing and Google Remarketing. [L0272.069] (2022, older)
- If one traffic source converts well and another does not, leave the working source alone and focus the investigation on the weak one. Compare conversion rate by traffic source in Google Analytics to see which is which. [L1456.040] (2021-2022, older)
- Measure conversion rate and return on ad spend per traffic source, especially Google Ads, to see what is working. [L1109.047] (2018, older)
- Track ROAS, conversion rate, bounce rate, cost per sale and spend per product group together. A high bounce rate can be normal for product-page traffic that already knows what it wants. [L0804.082] (2023)
- Judge the bounce rate by paid traffic segments when a new store launches, and do not change the store before paid traffic starts. [L1281.096] (2022, older)

**Country**

- Google Analytics shows the country distribution of visitors, which tells you which currency and pricing defaults to use. [L0838.005]
- Procedure: check conversion rate per country in Shopify.
  1. Go to Analytics. [L1316.007]
  2. Open Reports and then Online store sessions. [L1316.007]
  3. Click View report. [L1316.007]
  4. Set the columns to Sessions, Conversions (converted sessions) and Conversion rate. [L1316.007]
  5. Set the time grouping to None so the data is not split by hour or day. [L1316.007]
  6. Choose the Country grouping and compare the rows. [L1316.007]
  7. Filter or drill down to Region and City to see which area converts best. [L1316.007]
- For market analysis, the usable columns in the Online store sessions report are Sessions, Conversions, Conversion rate, Country, Region and City. Filter these to see which country, region or city performs best. [L1316.008]
- After you find a strong country, drill down to region and city. Put more ad budget into the best-performing city, for example Melbourne in Australia, rather than spreading spend evenly across the country. [L1316.010]
- The Markets overview shows each market's share of total sales, sales and conversion rate for the last 30 days. It also counts how many countries are not in any market (209 in the example). [L1167.035]
- Check funnel ratios per product and per country, not only in total. [L1185.018]

**Product**

- When two or more products run on one store, give each product its own campaign and UTM tag. Compare add-to-cart and initiate-checkout counts per product alongside purchases. This shows at which funnel step each product loses buyers, which a shared campaign cannot show because it can credit the same purchase to several products. [L1347.037]
- When running several product pages or stores from the same TikTok account, track each page separately to see which product converts. [L0561.058]
- Focus price and page optimization on the top 20% of product pages by traffic, since they drive most of the results. [L1090.131] (2021, older)
- Google Analytics > Engagement > Pages and screens shows which pages already get the most traffic. Use it to decide which product descriptions to rewrite first. [L1190.016]

### Bounce rate and time on page

**Definitions**

- Bounce rate is the percentage of visitors who leave after visiting only that single page without further interaction. [L0293.004]
- Average Time on Page shows per URL how long visitors remain on each product page in your store. [L0293.003]
- Bounce rate is the share of visitors who leave without further interaction. An average time on site well below 10 seconds together with a very high bounce rate indicates that the visitors are the wrong audience for the offer. [L1254.005]
- Google Analytics is used to check the bounce rate of the store, which is the share of visitors who leave the site without any action. [L1085.048] (2018, older)

**Thresholds (note the different sources and years)**

| Context | Threshold | Meaning | Source ids |
|---|---|---|---|
| General store (2018, older) | 60% or lower | Healthy | L1085.049 |
| General store (2018, older) | 80% to 90% | Most visitors leave right away; the landing page needs a fix | L1085.049 |
| Organic landing pages (2021-2022, older) | 50% or below | Good | L1099.017 |
| Organic landing pages (2021-2022, older) | 80% to 90% | Page problem or mismatch between page and search terms it ranks for | L1099.017 |
| New store with paid traffic not yet started (2022, older) | 78% to 82% with average session of 53 seconds | Not a reason to change the store before paid traffic starts | L1281.096 |

- Use the 2021-2022 organic thresholds when you evaluate landing pages from organic search. Use the 2018 general thresholds only as an older reference. [L1085.049, L1099.017]
- Judge user interaction only on organic traffic. Paid traffic, especially from Google Shopping, usually has a higher bounce rate and different time on page, so filter it out by Source/Medium before evaluating a page. [L1099.016] (2021-2022, older)
- Time on page under 30 seconds is not good for organic landing pages. Above 30 seconds is acceptable, and longer is better. [L1099.018] (2021-2022, older)
- A very high bounce rate on a high-traffic page, with many visitors leaving within about 10 seconds, points to a problem with the offer or to a gap between expectations and what the page shows. Behavior data does not always reveal a problem, so use it to guide the investigation, not as proof. [L0473.019] (2021-2022, older)
- Before judging a high bounce rate on a new store, check which search terms and countries Google Search Console shows for the store. Traffic from unrelated search terms or distant countries often explains a poor bounce rate better than the bounce figure alone. [L1281.130] (2022, older)

**Procedure: diagnosing a high bounce rate in Google Analytics (2021, older)**

1. Open Behavior. [L0247.116]
2. Open Content. [L0247.116]
3. Find the pages with the highest bounce rates. [L0247.116]
4. Check which traffic sources send visitors to those pages. [L0247.116]

**Procedure: checking user interaction on landing pages (2021-2022, older)**

1. Open Google Analytics in the default browser. [L1099.019]
2. Load the custom report and open it under Custom reports in the left menu. [L1099.019]
3. Filter by secondary dimension Source/Medium and keep only organic traffic. [L1099.019]
4. Compare bounce rate and time on page per landing page against the thresholds of 50% bounce rate and 30 seconds time on page. [L1099.019]
5. For pages above 80% bounce rate or under 30 seconds, check search intent match, above-the-fold content, overlays, readability and interaction points. Adjust the search terms if the keywords themselves are mismatched. [L1099.019]
6. Re-measure after changes. [L1099.019]

- The custom report supplied with the course shows bounce rate and time on page for all landing pages. Filter it by the secondary dimension Source/Medium to isolate organic traffic. [L1099.015] (2021-2022, older)
- A high bounce rate on a product page can be normal for traffic that already knows what it wants. [L0804.082] (2023)

### Device: mobile versus desktop

- Track conversion and return on ad spend by device. Desktop often converts best, but mobile carries most traffic and sales, and many mobile visitors buy later on desktop, so mobile should not be switched off. [L1085.052] (2018, older)
- Check Google Analytics bounce rate and session duration, separated by mobile and desktop, before changing the store or ads, since device differences often explain most of the problem. [L1090.132] (2021, older)
- Relative mobile conversion rate = mobile conversion rate / desktop conversion rate x 100. It shows how well the mobile site converts compared with desktop, so it can be read without being distorted by differences in campaign sources. [L0390.002]

**Example**

| Metric | Mobile | Desktop |
|---|---|---|
| Traffic share | 58% | 42% |
| Conversion rate | 1.79% | 4.44% |
| Relative mobile CR | 40% (1.79% / 4.44%) | - |

- Relative mobile conversion rate equals the mobile conversion rate divided by the desktop conversion rate. In the example, 1.79% divided by 4.44% gives 40%. [L0423.003, L0423.004]

### Page speed and reliability

- One second of speed improvement can add about 7,000 USD in daily revenue for a store with 100,000 USD daily revenue. [L0390.033]
- The target First Contentful Paint (FCP) is under 3 seconds. It is the main speed metric to watch because it reflects the first view a customer sees. [L1410.033]
- Page speed scores in the Shopify admin are less accurate than GTmetrics. Use an outside tool to check page speed before making design decisions. [L0468.170] (2020, older)
- PageSpeed Insights shows field data only when the Chrome User Experience Report has enough real-user data for that exact URL. Otherwise it shows an origin summary for the whole domain, so check individual page URLs to see page-specific results. [L1266.033] (2021-2022, older)
- Run WebPageTest at least three times and compare the first view runs. Treat a slow metric as a real problem only if it appears consistently across the runs, not only in a single outlier run. [L1266.045] (2021-2022, older)
- Treat uptime monitoring as business insurance, not an expense. A store that is down for days loses sales unnoticed, and Shopify outages can happen even on a cloud platform. [L0575.024]

### Google Analytics and Shopify page reports

- Google Analytics > left sidebar > Behavior > Site Content shows which URLs visitors view, which helps decide which product pages to optimize first. [L1275.039] (2019, older)
- In Google Analytics, go to Sidebar > Behavior > Site Content > All Pages to view detailed page-level performance metrics displayed in red columns. [L0293.002]
- Menu names differ between versions of Google Analytics, so use the path your account shows. [L0293.002, L1190.016]
- Google Analytics shows which pages get views and a basic conversion rate per page. [L0561.058]
- Google Analytics is used to compare page versions at the end of a test. Relevant page metrics are bounce rate, time on page, exit rate, conversion rate and page value. [L1364.031] (2021-2022, older)
- Procedure: compare results after a test (2021-2022, older).
  1. Open the two page versions in Google Analytics. [L1364.038]
  2. Compare bounce rate, time on page, exit rate, conversion rate and page value for each version over the test period. [L1364.038]
  3. Check whether the difference is statistically significant before making a decision. [L1364.038]

### On-page behavior: heatmaps and recordings

- A heatmap shows how far visitors scroll and where they click. It tells you whether key content, such as reviews or the add-to-cart button, is actually being seen. [L0409.014] (2021-2022, older)
- Deep orange areas in Lucky Orange heatmaps show where most visitors click on your pages. If a non-clickable section receives heavy clicks (deep orange), visitors probably expect it to be interactive, or the design confuses their intentions. [L0293.010]
- Use Lucky Orange screen recordings to see how shoppers move through the page and checkout when sales are missing. [L0950.025]

### Advertorials and landing-page routes

- Advertorial decision logic when it underperforms:
  1. Check how many visitors move from the advertorial to the offer page. [L1131.017]
  2. If that number is low, rewrite the advertorial copy to strengthen the argument. [L1131.017]
  3. Alternatively, split test sending ad traffic directly to the offer page against the advertorial route, and keep the stronger one. [L1131.017]
- Google Optimize could run headline tests on advertorial and sales pages to find the headline that grabs the most attention. It was installed as a snippet placed after the page builder (PageFly) loader in Shopify. [L0995.011]
- Google Optimize was discontinued in 2023, so the A/B testing described in that lesson needs another current testing tool, such as a Shopify app or a native testing platform. [L0916.034]

### Upsell, cross-sell and bundle analytics

**Upsell metrics**

- Track the dollar value each upsell adds, not just its conversion rate. An offer that converts less often can still produce more revenue if its price is much higher. [L0378.087]
- AOV is the key metric for upsells and cross-sells, because they raise revenue per existing customer rather than acquiring new ones. Track AOV to judge whether an offer works. [L1411.003]
- Track AOV to measure the effect of bundles, cross-sells and upsells. In the example, AOV rose from $40 to $70 with a two-unit bundle, and from $39 to $49 with a three-tier bundle ladder. [L0434.018]
- Example with a single-product store: average order value of 40 USD. After switching to a bundle offer of buy two and get an extra 20% off, two items cost about 70 USD, and AOV rises from 40 USD to about 70 USD. Shipping per unit also falls with two units, which raises average profit further. [L0435.010]
- The lesson estimates that about 80% of buyers do not uncheck pre-selected cart extras. About 60% of orders then add 3 to 5 USD, so each order can gain 5 to 10 USD in total from extras. [L1412.019]
- The lesson estimates that a cart upsell app such as Monster Cart Upsell can lift AOV by about 5 to 10 USD, and by 20 to 30 USD with extensive customization. These are the lesson's own estimates, not verified results. [L1412.020]
- Conversion rate is not the only measure for upsells. Track AOV and profit per order together. [L1231.003, L0378.087]

**Upsell options and tactics**

| Upsell Tactic | Tool/Method | Cost | Setup Time |
|---|---|---|---|
| Comparison charts | Create in Shopify or design app | $0 | 1-3 hours per chart |
| Product upgrade (Bold Upsell app) | Bold Upsell | $9.99-$89.99/month* | 15-30 minutes per upsell |
| Extended warranty | Bold Upsell (included) | Included in app | 5 minutes |
| Priority shipping | Shopify checkout setting | $0 | 5 minutes |
| Post-purchase phone call | Staff time | $0 | 10-20 minutes per call |

*14-day free trial available. [L0240.016]

**Bundle tests**

- When testing a bundle structure, track average order value and profit per customer alongside ad performance. Judge bundle changes by profit per customer, not by the front-end price alone. [L0347.020]
- After adding bundles and the ebook bonus, track average order value and conversion rate, and compare them with the period before the change. This shows whether the offer change actually improved results. [L0860.026]

**Kaching Bundles and Upsell reports**

- Kaching Bundles overview shows Added revenue and Orders with bundles for 24 hours, 7 days, 30 days or all time, plus per-deal columns for Visitors and AOV. Use these to compare the performance of each bundle. [L0862.015]
- Track AOV and orders with bundles per deal in Kaching Bundles analytics. Compare each deal's AOV and visitor count to decide which bundle to keep, change or archive. [L0861.022]
- The Kaching Bundles overview shows Added revenue and Orders with bundles for a selected period. A Performance metrics section lists Revenue per visitor and Total revenue. Use Revenue per visitor alongside AOV to compare how much each bundle earns per shopper who lands on the store. [L0861.041]
- Kaching Upsell analytics report AOV, Add. rev (revenue added by upsells), Total rev and Rev/visitor for the chosen period. These figures show how much the upsell and cart changes add to each order and to each visitor. [L0560.031]
- The funnel dashboard shows Views, Conversion, Revenue and Rev./Visit for each offer, with a Last 30 Days filter. Compare these per funnel to decide which funnels to keep, improve or remove. [L1141.022]
- The Store Performance panel shows Total OCU revenue, Today's upsell revenue, Today's AOV with OCU upsells, Today's AOV without OCU upsells and Today's avg. upsell value. Compare the two AOV figures to measure the real lift from upsells. [L1141.023]
- Post-purchase funnels in the lesson example pushed AOV to about 300 USD, and into the 380 USD range in the best case. Use these as upside targets and measure against your own store's AOV without upsells, not as a guarantee. [L1141.024]

**Rebuy and cross-sell dashboards**

- The Rebuy dashboard shows widget analytics and Rebuy sales as a percentage of total sales. In the example, the figure was 4.23%, but no benchmark was given because results depend on many variables. [L1231.057]
- After launching cross-sells, review the app's dashboard to see which options customers click and where they leave the funnel. If buyers do not follow through, change the offers, the order of steps or the products shown. [L1076.034]

### Post-purchase offers

- The Rebuy post-purchase report shows these columns per offer: Views, Conversion (%), Revenue ($), Rev/User ($), and status (ACCEPTED, DECLINED or A/B TESTING). Each widget in a flow carries its own row with these figures. [L1413.013]
- In the example merchant's flow, about 84% of customers declined the initial offer, and only about 15% of the 14,000 customers who reached the final downsell converted. Low acceptance rates are normal for post-purchase offers and can still produce meaningful revenue at scale. [L1413.014]
- Additional analytic event delay is a setting on post-purchase widgets. It is left at 0 seconds in the example flows. [L1413.015]
- The Post Purchase Flows list shows these columns per flow. The flow-level figures summarize all offers in the flow, while the per-offer report breaks the same measures down by widget. [L1413.019]

| Flow (name and ID) | Status (LIVE / NOT LIVE) | Total Visitors | Conversion | Revenue | Revenue Per Visitor | Last Viewed | Created Date |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|

[L1413.019]

- Give each flow and widget a name that states its test purpose, such as Gift Card offer test, so the report shows which variant is which. Clear names make it easier to compare conversion and revenue per visitor across tests. [L1413.022]

### A/B test metrics and significance

**Core metrics and reading the report**

- Measure all A/B tests against revenue-per-session improvement to judge profitability accurately. [L0197.025]
- Visually's test report shows status, start date, last edit, pages, audience, device and allocation at the top. It lists sessions, transactions, conversion rate, AOV, PSV, revenue, profit and odds to win for control and each variation, with the primary goal marked. Metrics not selected at setup can be added later to the report. [L0389.020]
- For product recommendations and product upsells, Visually splits revenue into assisted revenue (a shopper clicked an upsell item and bought something later) and direct revenue (the upsell item was bought directly). Best-selling items for control and each variation are listed further down the report. [L0389.021]
- When setting up a Visually A/B test, the goal menu offers many options, including revenue, conversion rate, profit, AOV, subscriptions and returns. You can select several goals, but one must be marked as the primary goal. Events can also be sent to Google Analytics with a checkbox. [L0389.024]

**Significance**

- Wait for a statistically significant result before choosing a winner in Visually. Keep the test running while the report shows no statistically significant results yet. A variation with a high chance to win, such as 80% odds to win, is not enough on its own. [L0389.019]
- Do not claim double-digit conversion gains after only a few days of test data. [L0389.023]
- In the matching snapshot, conversion rate was around 2.0% vs 2.05% and not statistically significant, so the reported gain was not visible there. [L0389.023]

**Case: mobile cart upsell test on a watch-band Shopify store**

- The test ran from 1 Nov 2024 (last edit 10 Nov 2024, version 1-14 Nov 2024) with an even 50% split, All Pages, All Visitors and Mobile targeting. [L0389.022]
- Control: 50,970 sessions, 1,019 transactions, AOV $56.22, PSV $1.12, revenue $57,286 and profit $42,105. [L0389.022]
- Variation: 50,753 sessions, 1,039 transactions, AOV $57.15 (+1.7%), PSV $1.17, revenue $59,375 (+3.6%) and profit $43,804 (+4.0%). [L0389.022]
- Visually still showed no statistically significant result and 80% odds to win for the variation, so the test needed more days before a decision. [L0389.022]

**Lift calculation**

- Relative conversion lift = (new conversion rate − old conversion rate) ÷ old conversion rate × 100. [L0916.038]
- Example: a rise from 1.85% to 4.25% equals (4.25 − 1.85) ÷ 1.85 × 100 ≈ 129.7%, which is an increase of about 130%. [L0916.038]

**Older headline testing note**

- Headline tests on advertorial and sales pages, installed as a snippet after the PageFly loader, belonged to the Google Optimize setup described in older material. Google Optimize was discontinued in 2023, so use a current testing tool instead. [L0995.011, L0916.034]

#### Further details

- If the conversion rate is too low, improve it first (conversion rate optimization) so that the business can afford to spend more on traffic. [L1495.006]

## Google Ads and Shopping reporting and optimization

### Core metrics and definitions

- Three core metrics measure Google Ads performance: ROAS (Return On Ad Spend), CPC (Cost Per Click) and conversion rate. [L0013.003]
- ROAS = conversion value ÷ cost, so $15 in sales from $1 of ad spend is a 15x ROAS. [L0750.020, L0746.003]
- ROAS = revenue generated from ads ÷ ad spend. [L0665.099] (2022, older)
- ROAS = conversion value ÷ ad cost. Use this formula in the Google Ads keyword report to rank products and keywords by return. [L0856.093]
- Google Analytics displays ROAS as a percentage: 10x shows as 1000%, and 8.25x shows as 825%. [L0721.004]
- Revenue per click = conversion value ÷ clicks. [L0745.033]
- Cost per transaction = ad cost ÷ number of transactions. Example: $50 spent on 100 clicks that produce one conversion gives a cost per transaction of $50. [L0721.004]
- Cost per sale for a keyword group = ad spend on that group ÷ number of sales it produced. Example: $300 spent on quality keywords that produced one sale gives $300 per sale, which signals that those keywords and their landing product page need review. [L0626.022]
- Of all paid traffic metrics, cost per transaction and ROAS are the two most important, because together they show whether a sale is actually profitable. [L0721.003] (2021-22, older)
- A target cost per transaction can be set from break-even or from a desired profit margin per sale, and a target ROAS can then be derived from it. [L0721.003] (2021-22, older)
- Cost = sum of all clicks multiplied by their cost per click. Example: 454 clicks at an average CPC of $0.32 equal a total cost of $144.72. [L1341.013, L1341.014]
- Cost per all conversions = spend ÷ number of conversions. Average CPC = average amount paid per click. [L1341.013]
- Impressions = how often the Shopping ad was shown. Clicks = visits to the website. [L1341.013]
- Judge a platform's profitability by checking its ROAS, not just cost per click, although CPC should still be kept under control. [L0665.100] (2022, older)
- Judge a store's Google Ads health from profit and ROAS rather than the optimization score, since some stores with poor scores are very profitable. [L1090.133] (2021, older)

**ROAS and conversion reference values**

| Context | Value | Ids |
|---|---|---|
| Google Shopping minimum ROAS for a winning product | at least 10x | L0856.092 (2024); L0665.099 (2022, older) |
| Alpha campaign target | about 15x, never below 10x (at least $10 sales per $1 spent) | L0746.004 |
| Beta campaign target | runs profitably at about 8x | L0746.004 |
| Example threshold for a profitability alarm | 9x, but derive it from your own target cost per transaction | L0721.005 (2021-22, older) |
| Example target ROAS and actual | target 6.6, actual 4.2: room to reduce bids | L0745.010 |
| Shopping campaign target conversion rate | 2.5% or better, measured separately from the store-wide rate | L0626.010 |
| Mature Shopping campaign example | $12,000 spend, $31,000 revenue, 4.9% conversion rate | L0745.007 |
| Store average conversion rate used as baseline in a search text ad example | around 2% | L0923.004 |

### Reporting setup in Google Ads

**ROAS columns in the product group view**

1. Click **Columns**, then **Modify columns**. [L0750.021]
2. Under Conversions, select **Conversion value** and **Conversion value / Cost**. [L0750.021]
3. Move **Conversion value** directly below **Cost**, and move **Conversion value / Cost** below it. [L0750.021]
4. Remove **Benchmarks max CPC**. Keep **Search impression share**. [L0750.021]
5. Click **Save your column set**, give it a clear name such as ROAS, then click **Save and apply**. [L0750.022]
6. The product group view now shows Cost, Conversion value and Conversion value / Cost (ROAS) in one table. [L0750.022]

- Default product group columns in the Shopping ad group view are: product group, max CPC (default $0.40), impressions, clicks, cost, average CPC, benchmarks max CPC and search impression share. [L0750.023]
- Keyword report columns for decision making are Cost, CPC, conversion value (revenue) and a calculated ROAS column (conversion value ÷ cost). [L0856.094]
- Product group reports show impressions, clicks, cost, cost per all conversions, conversion rate, average CPC, benchmark max CPC, click share and search impression share per product. These columns help decide which individual product bids to raise or lower. [L0747.020]

**Where to find the numbers**

- Go to **Goals > Summary** in the left navigation. It lists every conversion action set up on the store and how many conversions each one has recorded. [L0470.003]
- To view cost per click over time, change the date range and set one of the graph lines to CPC. No spreadsheet export is needed. [L0470.045] (2023)
- Google Ads reports which individual ad produces conversions and how much revenue each ad brings in, so you can compare ads directly and cut the ones that spend without returning money. Conversion tracking must be set up on the site for these per-ad numbers to be reliable. [L1455.017]
- Once ads are approved, the account shows how many people see the ads, click them and buy. All optimization decisions should be based on these numbers. [L0715.002]
- Google Shopping performance is judged by a store's own traffic and sales data. Reporting in Google Ads usually shows the same reports over and over, and Google Ads optimization is driven by these reports rather than by separate tools. [L1114.061] (2023)

**Time zones and reporting delay**

- Google Ads reporting is not real time. Its dates use the account's time zone, which was GMT-04:00 Eastern Time in one example. Allow for this delay and time zone before comparing daily figures. [L0719.047] (2021-22, older)
- Google Ads reporting is not updated in real time. All dates and times follow the account's time zone, which was GMT-05:00 Eastern Time in another example. Set date ranges and compare results with this time zone in mind, not with the local time of the operator. [L0744.062] (2021-22, older)

**Spreadsheet and multi-channel views**

- Use a spreadsheet outside the ad platform to compare product-level profitability before deciding budget allocation in Google Shopping campaigns. [L0575.008]
- Multi-channel feed dashboards combine performance across channels in one view, so revenue can be compared per channel. Google Shopping was expected to remain the largest channel, followed by Bing. Use this to decide where to direct budget. [L0454.031]

### Campaign structure and budgets (Alpha and Beta)

**Principles**

- Allocate the largest share of daily budget to high-profit products in dedicated Alpha and Beta campaigns. Allocate a significant but lower share to mid-profit segments in separate campaigns. Keep low-profit products in the product feed but expose them only to free Google Shopping traffic with zero paid budget. [L0224.005]
- Set campaign budgets and default cost-per-click bids based on historical performance data from existing campaigns. Match these values as closely as possible to current Alpha and Beta campaigns to ensure comparable data during the learning phase. [L0224.012] (2024 and undated sources, not older)
- Review keyword and CPC performance from historic ad data to see which products get acceptable impressions, cost per click and conversions. Scale the parts of the account that produce profitable results. [L1283.101]
- Ad spend must match results. If a product gets clicks or sales, keep spending. If it gets no impressions or clicks, check the product feed, keywords and bids before increasing spend. [L0806.091] (2024)
- Before relaunching ads, review historical analytics and Google Ads data for what brought the first sales. Put most of the budget into the ads, products and devices that already proved to work. [L0806.090] (2024)

**Setting up Alpha and Beta**

1. In Google Ads, create two separate Alpha and Beta campaigns. [L0274.057] (2023)
2. Set a shared budget across both (for example $30 per day). [L0274.057] (2023)
3. Set Alpha to High Priority and Beta to Low Priority. [L0274.057] (2023)
4. Create Alpha 2 and Beta 2 for a different product group. [L0274.057] (2023)
5. Set a separate shared budget for Alpha 2 and Beta 2. This allows flexible budget allocation across product groups. [L0274.057] (2023)

**Default CPC and Beta control**

- The default CPC of the Alpha campaign can be raised to increase its traffic. If the Beta campaign converts profitably, leave its CPC unchanged. If it takes most traffic without converting well, lower its default CPC. [L0971.017] (2024)

**Launch expectations**

- A new Shopping campaign on about 1,000 products with a $20 daily budget may need roughly four weeks for Google's learning phase, and some products can get almost no impressions during that time. Check impressions per product, not only clicks. [L1287.069] (2025)
- For a new high-ticket store, results from Google Ads may be weaker in the first two to five months. As the conversion rate and the store's track record improve, ad profitability tends to rise over time. [L1228.011] (2021-22, older)
- Google Ads profitability improves only when ad changes, website changes and other parts of the business are fine-tuned together. Improving the ads alone while the store stays weak will not produce the expected return. [L1228.015] (2021-22, older)

**Budget-limited campaigns**

- A campaign at ROAS 9.3 that is limited by budget should get more budget, since a well-performing campaign should almost never be budget-limited. [L0745.010]

### Product group and product-level analysis

**Per-product data**

- After the item ID subdivision, each product gets its own row, so impressions, clicks and sales are visible per product. Based on that data you can exclude weak products or raise and lower individual product bids. [L0746.024]
- Track ad spend and returns at the product level so you can see profit per product in Google Ads. This is set up inside the Google Shopping campaign structure. [L1277.111] (2020, older)
- Track search performance, cost per click and clicks by product in Google Ads. Use those numbers to decide product-level bids and to spot which products need attention. [L0801.139] (2021, older)
- Use click-through rate, conversion rate and return on ad spend per product to decide bid changes. Products with good click-through and conversion rates justify higher bids. [L1094.106] (2023)
- Use the spreadsheet method from L0575.008 to compare product profitability before deciding budget allocation. [L0575.008]

**Ordering the work**

- Sort product groups or campaigns by cost first, to focus on the biggest spenders ("big fish") and work down the list. Compare each campaign's ROAS against its own target ROAS, since the target is not always labelled in the report. [L0745.009]
- Usually only one or two of many ad groups drag results down. Focus effort there rather than reviewing every ad group or thousands of products line by line, which costs a lot of time for little return. [L0719.022] (2021-22, older)

**Zero-conversion products**

- A product with a conversion rate of zero that spends a large share of the ad budget pulls the overall rate down. Identify it at product level and either lower its cost per click or pause it. [L1092.081] (2022, older)
- Example: a $300 non-converting product removed from a campaign changes spend from $1,050 to $750. The same $4,500 revenue then gives a ROAS of 6.0 instead of about 4.29. [L0745.033]
- A product that consumed about $500 in ad spend with no sales is the kind of heavy-traffic, non-converting product that compare-period reporting can expose. [L0721.013] (2021-22, older)

**Worked product examples**

- Top product example: $300 spend, ROAS 15.0, 9.6 conversions. Its CTR of 3.1% beat the competitor benchmark of 1.18%, while its CPC of $1.99 was above the competitor average of $1.68. Paying more was acceptable to capture more coverage. This product was the only converting product of its brand. [L0745.012]
- Mature campaign example: the campaign still had room to improve by segmenting weak products and reducing bids slightly to raise CTR and ROAS. [L0745.007]
- Early-stage bootstrapped example: $2,800 spend over one month generated $14,708 revenue, roughly a month or two after launch. The budget was raised from $1,000 to $2,800 once profitable. Across all Shopping campaigns the total ROAS was about 5.2. [L0745.008]

**Product-level actions**

- Action items in order:
  1. Shopping boost: compare your CPC against the benchmark and aim for a 20% CPC discount. [L1031.037]
  2. Shopping boost: add customer search terms to Shopping ad titles. [L1031.037]
  3. Conversion rate boost: aim for a 26% lift by running DPAs and optimizing for CTR. [L1031.037]
  4. LTV boost: aim for a 10% lift by making sure the opt-in and email marketing work properly. [L1031.037]
- Optimized titles and descriptions help Google Shopping results and do not hurt them, because Google Shopping pulls product data from product pages. [L0963.033] (2022, older)
- Changes that raise click-through rate or traffic without moving rankings still reach the bottom line, because the same visitors arrive more often. A 5% CTR increase is permanent and compounds over time. [L0575.003]

**Sample size before decisions**

- Google Shopping example math: 250 clicks at $1 CPC give $250 spend. Three sales of $1,000 each give $3,000 revenue, which is 12x ROAS and above target. But 120 clicks is still too small a sample to decide. [L0969.047] (2022, older)

### Search term and negative keyword analysis

**When to act on search terms**

- After about three weeks of Google Ads with several thousand impressions and hundreds of clicks, there is usually enough search term data to decide which terms to exclude as negatives. Make the negative keyword changes first and hold off on bid and budget changes until their effect shows. [L0807.053] (2025)
- Use pivot tables and filters in Google Sheets on the audited search terms:
  1. Filter Brand or non-brand to see how much spend goes to branded searches in prospecting campaigns. [L0876.022]
  2. Filter Intent stage to see which terms sit at top, middle or bottom of funnel. [L0876.022]
  3. Filter Relevant? = no and Recommended action = add as negative to build the negative list. [L0876.022]
  4. Filter Recommended action = promote to own ad group to find strong terms that deserve their own ad group or campaign. [L0876.022]
- Do not raise prices just because conversion is low. First analyze the campaign data, such as impressions, clicks and sales per search term and product, to find the real cause. [L0671.091] (2021, older)

**Brand search terms**

- Brand name Search campaigns typically have the highest return on ad spend (ROAS) but the smallest budget, because they capture high-intent searches. Use this as proof that the strategy works even though overall spend may be low. [L0210.030]
- Do not rule out brand-level search terms because of an assumption about their conversion rate. In the lesson's example, a brand-level ad group reached a 1.79% conversion rate and a US$65.46 cost per conversion, against 1.01% and US$91.36 for a model-level ad group. The test result should decide. Compare cost per conversion and conversion rate together, and check them against the cost per click. [L0880.030] (2021-22, older)

### Impression share, click share and lost impression share

**Definitions**

- Click share = the percentage of eligible clicks the account actually receives compared with competitors. [L1341.016]
- Search impression share = the percentage of eligible impressions where the ad appeared, for example for the search term "infrared saunas" when the store sells infrared saunas. [L1341.016]
- Click share of 16.19% means you win only about 16% of the clicks available in the auctions where you appear. Search impression share of 34% means you appear in only about 34% of the auctions you are eligible for. A very low search absolute top impression share means you almost never show in the top position. [L0745.002]
- Search absolute top impression share shows how often the ad appears in the first position. [L1341.018]
- Search impression share shows what percentage of the potential search traffic for a product the ads are actually reaching. Raising bids increases it, for example from 50% toward 60% or 70%. [L0750.024]
- Search Impression Share is called Search Impression Share in Google Ads and has an equivalent term in Microsoft Ads. It is used to judge bid levels. [L0836.055] (2024)

**Reading the levels**

- A Search Impression Share in the mid to high 90s means the ad group is effectively maxed out, and 100% is rarely seen. A share as low as 26% on high-value terms means bids are too low compared with competitors. [L0744.044] (2021-22, older)
- Impression share is the key scaling metric for a Google Ads campaign. A campaign with strong revenue but low Impression Share, for example 32% against a possible 66%, has room to grow and can be pushed by improving the campaign. Review it alongside revenue each month. [L0463.002]
- Search impression share below 10 percent, with no change after doubling the budget, means the bids or ads are not the main problem. Look at irrelevant search terms and negative keywords first. [L0806.089] (2024)
- Impression share after removing negative keywords shows how far the budget falls short of full coverage. [L0357.028] (2019, older)

**Procedure for campaigns above target**

1. In the custom report, find the campaigns, ad groups or products whose ROAS is above your target. [L0719.023] (2021-22, older)
2. Open them in Google Ads and check the search impression share. [L0719.023] (2021-22, older)
3. If impression share is well below 100%, there is room to raise bids, since more impressions will bring more clicks and sales. [L0719.023] (2021-22, older)
4. Expect cost per sale to rise and ROAS to fall when bids go up, and accept that as long as ROAS stays above target. [L0719.023] (2021-22, older)
5. If impression share is close to 100%, no more traffic is available on that product, so look at other areas. [L0719.023] (2021-22, older)
6. Use profit from strong products to fund bids on other products to test more exposure, while keeping the healthy ones where they are. [L0719.023] (2021-22, older)

**Worked example**

- An ad group sits at 20x ROAS against a 10x target, and its search impression share is 40%. This leaves a buffer to raise bids and get more traffic, accepting a lower ROAS while total profit rises. [L0719.024] (2021-22, older)

**Lost impression share**

- Lost impression share due to rank moves opposite to search impression share, but the two figures do not add up exactly to 100%. Lost impression share is therefore a useful signal rather than a precise complement. [L0879.033] (2021-22, older)
- Search impression share lost due to rank shows impressions lost because of quality score or cost per click. Cost per click is the main lever the advertiser controls. In the example, 65% of impressions were lost due to rank, meaning bids were too low relative to competitors. [L1341.018]
- Diagnosis example: with a CPC around $0.30 against a competitor benchmark of $0.91, but only 16% click share and 34% search impression share, the bids are likely too low and should be raised. [L1341.017]
- Benchmark max CPC shows the bid competitors are placing for the same products. If the account pays around $0.32 while the benchmark is $0.91, the account is getting clicks more cheaply than competitors. [L1341.015]

**Acting on click and impression metrics**

- If the cost per click looks too high to raise bids, the problem is likely conversion rate. The ads may be showing for the wrong keywords, bad search terms should be cleared out, or the website should be optimized. Benchmark CTR shows competitors' click-through rate for comparison. [L1341.019]

### CPC and CTR trends

**Benchmarks**

- Benchmark max CPC in Google Shopping is the average CPC the competition is bidding. Any benchmark value means the average of competitors. Compare your own CPC and CTR against these competitor averages before changing bids. [L0745.001]
- Benchmark CTR of competitors in the example was 1.18%, while the own CTR was 0.76%. This signals an issue to investigate. [L0745.005]
- Benchmark CPC is based on the product category, not on the specific brand. A brand can therefore see its CPC rise sharply even when its CPC is close to the category benchmark. [L0879.034] (2021-22, older)
- Google may show no benchmark data for a product with too little information. In that case the benchmark columns appear empty. Add the CTR column in the report to compare your own CTR with the competitor benchmark. [L0745.013]

**Rising CPC case**

- A top-selling brand was on back order, while a major retailer listed it as in stock and sold below the MAP price by about $100. The manufacturer did not enforce MAP despite repeated requests over several months. The CPC of that brand's campaign rose from $0.75 to over $2.00, and about two months later sales for the brand had almost stopped. [L0879.035] (2021-22, older)
- If a product's return on ad spend drops sharply from week to week, for example from 15x to 5x, check Google Shopping for competitors selling it below MAP. A sudden drop often traces back to a price violation. [L0968.072] (2021, older)
- Audit all ad accounts every week or every two weeks. When sales stop, check whether traffic dropped and look for causes such as a competitor breaking MAP by around 10%. [L0429.029] (2022, older)

**Bid moves on CPC**

- Procedure example: with a $0.50 CPC and ROAS of 15X, there is room to increase the bid to 60 to 70 cents to get more impressions. Monitor ROAS continuously to ensure profitability. [L0274.023] (2023)
  1. Go to Google Ads. [L0274.023]
  2. Look at historical campaign data over a 30-day period. [L0274.023]
  3. Calculate ROAS: revenue ÷ ad spend. [L0274.023]
  4. If spending $0.50 CPC and ROAS is 15X, have room to increase bid to 60-70 cents to get more impressions. [L0274.023]
  5. Monitor ROAS continuously to ensure profitability. [L0274.023]
- Example campaign with target ROAS 6.6 and actual ROAS 4.2 indicates room to reduce bids to improve ROAS. [L0745.010]
- Products with good click-through and conversion rates justify higher bids. [L1094.106] (2023)

**Search text ads**

- In a search text ad example, a max bid of $5 produced an average cost per click of about $3 in second place. Store average conversion rates of around 2 percent were used as the baseline for these calculations. [L0923.004]
- Search text ads usually have a higher cost per click than Shopping ads, and in at least 80% of cases this holds true. [L0751.009] (2021-22, older)
- Because the cost per click is higher, search text ads need a solid conversion rate to pay back the spend. Calculate the conversion rate needed to support the cost per click before scaling spend. [L0751.010] (2021-22, older)

**Cost trends over time**

- In 2020, online sales rose during the pandemic while ad costs first fell to multi-year lows before rising again. Monitor cost trends monthly rather than assuming a fixed level. [L1277.112] (2020, older)

### Search vs Shopping and other channels

**Comparison**

| Channel | Cost per click | Notes | Ids |
|---|---|---|---|
| Search text ads | usually higher than Shopping (in at least 80% of cases) | needs a solid conversion rate to pay back spend | L0751.009, L0751.010 (2021-22, older) |
| Shopping | usually lower | Shopping Actions provide no click data, see below | L0751.009 (2021-22, older) |
| Brand name Search | not stated | highest ROAS, smallest budget | L0210.030 |

- Shopping Actions provide no click data and no detailed reporting. Merchant Center shows only order-based information, which can be imported into Shopify, and the ads do not appear in Google Analytics. [L0738.008] (2021-22, older)
- Shopify's built-in analytics is useful for sales and product reporting, especially on higher plans, but it does not contain paid traffic data such as cost per click or ad spend. It cannot judge paid campaign performance. [L0721.002] (2021-22, older)
- Google Ads results cannot be compared between markets because many factors differ, including competition, demand and product type. A store should measure its performance against its own goals and history, not against other markets. [L0713.008] (2021-22, older)

**YouTube campaigns**

- Google Ads Audience Manager is used to build audiences. Affinity audiences (interests), in-market segments (active purchase research) and life-event audiences are available. For retargeting, the core audience is one's own website visitors and customer list. [L1510.026]
- Procedure for optimizing YouTube campaigns in Google Ads:
  1. Check each campaign's cost per view and spend. [L1510.038]
  2. Pause ads that are not working and keep the ones that do. [L1510.038]
  3. Review demographics and exclude age groups that underperform. The 65+ group was excluded in the example. [L1510.038]
  4. Review Topics and exclude irrelevant ones. [L1510.038]
  5. Review placements, which is where most time goes. Find expensive placements (for example ones with 20-cent views) and exclude them. [L1510.038]
  6. Use bid adjustments for devices only where cost per view differs meaningfully. [L1510.038]
- Example small YouTube campaign: about $300 spent with an average cost per view of $0.03. The campaign was an audience-based pre-roll for an audit video, with no keywords because audience-based targeting was used. Placement review revealed some 20-cent views that were excluded to lower average cost per view. [L1510.039]
- Cutting expensive placements is the fastest optimization. Exclude placements with high cost per view (for example 20 cents) to push average cost per view down, and keep the cheaper traffic. [L1510.040]

### Google Analytics custom report for ROAS

**Why GA**

- Google Ads does not provide ROAS as a field, so a custom report in Google Analytics is needed to measure it. The report must be set up manually, is not a standard report, and is built around the attribution and conversion tracking setup. [L0719.018] (2021-22, older)
- Google Analytics can calculate ROAS and cost per transaction for paid traffic, which Google Ads does not provide as a field. GA is therefore preferred for reporting paid campaign performance. [L0721.001] (2021-22, older)
- Standard tracking via the Google Ads conversion tag is usually under-reporting compared with GA, so the GA report is often more reliable for conversion counts. [L0721.001] (2021-22, older)
- The GA custom report is considered slightly more accurate than the Google Ads interface. It counts sales that came from Google ads and excludes draft orders. [L0719.019] (2021-22, older)

**Structure**

- The report breaks performance down by campaign, then ad group, then individual product. [L0719.019] (2021-22, older)
- The custom report pulls in Google Ads data only for campaigns in the Google Ads account linked to the GA property. It can be drilled down from campaign to ad group, then to individual products (for Shopping) or keywords, and finally to search terms. This shows cost, ROAS and cost per transaction at each level without manual calculation. [L0721.009] (2021-22, older)

**Build steps**

1. Go to Customisation, click **build a report** or **New custom report**, and give it a title, for example Google Ads Performance. [L0721.006] (2021-22, older)
2. Add metrics via **+ add metric**: Clicks, Impressions, CPC, Cost, CTR, E-commerce Conversion Rate, Transactions, Cost per Transaction, Revenue and ROAS. [L0721.006] (2021-22, older)
3. Add dimensions via **+ add dimension** in this order: Campaign, Google Ads: Ad Group, Keyword, Search Query. [L0721.006] (2021-22, older)
4. Add filters: Traffic Type exactly matches `paid` (include), and Source/Medium exactly matches `google / cpc` (include). [L0721.006] (2021-22, older)
5. Save the report and set the date range to the period you need. [L0721.006] (2021-22, older)

**Metric names and tabs**

- In the GA custom report metric list, use the plain metric names: Clicks, Impressions (not the variants offered in the drop-down), CPC, Cost, CTR, E-commerce Conversion Rate, Transactions, Cost per Transaction, Revenue and ROAS. [L0721.007] (2021-22, older)
- Metrics like Clicks and Impressions are incompatible with Map Overlay report tabs, so use Explorer or Flat Table tabs. [L0721.007] (2021-22, older)
- The conversion metric is labelled Transactions, the conversion rate is labelled E-commerce Conversion Rate, and revenue should be the plain Revenue metric. [L0721.008] (2021-22, older)
- The same custom report layout can be reused for other traffic sources by changing the traffic filter, for example organic search or email marketing. Build a separate report for each source so that paid, organic and email results are not mixed together. [L0721.034] (2021-22, older)

**Compare option**

- Compare two date ranges side by side to see how ROAS, conversion rate and other metrics changed, for example month against month. This helps spot products that receive heavy traffic but do not convert. [L0721.013] (2021-22, older)

### Audit and troubleshooting procedures

**Management approach**

- Check the overall account ROAS first against the profitability target. If the account is above target, no further action is needed. [L0721.011] (2021-22, older)
- If the account is not above target, drill into the campaigns below target, then their ad groups, then individual products. Fix only the specific underperformers instead of changing the whole account. [L0721.011] (2021-22, older)
- Example from the lesson: with a target of 9x ROAS, a campaign running at 16x and another at 12x are fine. An ad group at about 3x shows ad spend that is not profitable. The action is to find which products sit in that ad group and improve their conversion rate, instead of changing the whole campaign. [L0721.012] (2021-22, older)
- Example: an account with a 10x ROAS target shows 15x overall, but one of three Shopping campaigns runs at 3x while the other two run at 14x and 14x. The 3x campaign is pulling the account result down, so that campaign is the one to examine first. [L0719.021] (2021-22, older)

**Drill-down procedure for an account below target**

1. In the custom report, start at the account or campaign level and compare total ROAS to your target. [L0719.020] (2021-22, older)
2. If the total is at or above target, leave it unless you have time for a deeper review. [L0719.020] (2021-22, older)
3. If it is below target, find the campaign with ROAS below target, since one weak campaign can drag the whole account down. [L0719.020] (2021-22, older)
4. Open that campaign and find the ad groups below target. [L0719.020] (2021-22, older)
5. Within those ad groups, list products that get clicks but do not convert. [L0719.020] (2021-22, older)
6. For each product, check whether the margin is good enough to sell it. If not, stop advertising it. [L0719.020] (2021-22, older)
7. If the margin works, research who else sells the product and compare price and offer, then improve your offer until ROAS is above target. [L0719.020] (2021-22, older)
8. If that is not worth the time, lower bids so cost per click falls while conversion holds, or pause the product. [L0719.020] (2021-22, older)

**Checking the numbers**

- Verify cost per sale against spend ÷ sales before making decisions. Example: $152 spend with 2 sales gives $76 and $54 with 1 sale gives $54. These do not match the reported $74 and $52, so the account figures should be checked. [L0748.004]
- Spend check: over three months the Alpha campaign spent $152 and the Beta campaign spent $54, a total of $206, or about $69 per month. A $10 daily budget would allow about $300 per month, so the campaigns were heavily underspending. [L0748.005]
- Keyword and CPC performance should be reviewed from historic ad data before changes. [L1283.101]
- A store's traffic and sales should be reviewed over about seven days before making bid changes. Changes made after less than a week of data can be based on noise and lead to poor decisions. [L1114.062] (2023)

**Change history and recent drops**

1. Open Google Ads. [L0272.030] (2022, older)
2. Click **Campaigns** in the left sidebar. [L0272.030] (2022, older)
3. Click **Change History** to view all account modifications by date. [L0272.030] (2022, older)
4. Scan recent changes (bid adjustments, negative keywords added, budget changes). [L0272.030] (2022, older)
5. If you see a change that correlates with the performance drop, click the revert button to undo it. [L0272.030] (2022, older)
6. Run for a few days and see if performance recovers. [L0272.030] (2022, older)
7. This method identifies whether your own changes caused the issue. [L0272.030] (2022, older)

**Tracing a sale**

1. Open each ad account (usually Google Ads and possibly Meta) and see which one sent the traffic. [L0429.015] (2022, older)
2. Open the keyword breakdown in the campaign and find which keywords brought visitors who bought the same product. [L0429.015] (2022, older)
3. If one product sold repeatedly through a Google Shopping campaign, put that product in its own product group to monitor it. [L0429.015] (2022, older)
4. Keep the traffic level consistent over the next weeks. A drop from 100 visitors to 20 is a warning sign. [L0429.015] (2022, older)

**Review order for a Google Shopping account**

1. Impressions: how often the Shopping ad was shown. [L1341.020]
2. Clicks: how many visitors reached the website. [L1341.020]
3. Cost: total clicks multiplied by their cost per click. [L1341.020]
4. Cost per all conversions: spend divided by the number of conversions. [L1341.020]
5. Average CPC: the average amount paid per click. [L1341.020]
6. Benchmark max CPC: the bid competitors place for the same products. [L1341.020]
7. Click share: the share of eligible clicks the account receives compared with competitors. [L1341.020]
8. Search impression share: the share of eligible searches where the ad appeared. [L1341.020]
9. Search absolute top impression share: how often the ad appears in the top position. [L1341.020]
10. Search impression share lost due to rank: impressions lost because bids or quality score are too low. [L1341.020]
11. Benchmark CTR: the competitors' click-through rate for comparison. [L1341.020]

**Monitoring cadence**

- KPI monitoring for non-MAP products:
  1. Check Google Ads KPIs more often than for MAP products, on a fixed schedule such as every Monday, Wednesday and Friday, or at least twice a week, scaled to traffic volume. [L1051.009]
  2. Focus on the Google Shopping campaigns and watch for sharp drops in clicks or sales, which can signal that a competitor has undercut you. [L1051.009]

**Targets for conversion and cost per sale**

- Target conversion rate for Google Shopping campaigns is 2.5% or better. Measure this campaign rate separately from the store-wide conversion rate. [L0626.010]
- Cost per sale for a keyword group that spends $300 for one sale signals a review of those keywords and their landing product page. [L0626.022]

## Email, retargeting and owned-channel metrics

### Email setup and tracking settings

- Shopify Messaging includes 10,000 free emails per month; additional emails cost $1 per 1,000. [L0147.002]
- Keep UTM tracking on and Smart Sending off for every email in every flow and campaign, so email traffic and sales are tracked consistently and every send follows the schedule set in the flow or campaign. [L0607.039, L0607.040]
- Consolidate customer data for retention, loyalty and analytics into one view. Many separate tools make it hard to know what is working and how to improve it. [L1232.007]

### Email revenue and earnings per subscriber

- Earnings per subscriber = monthly email revenue ÷ active subscribers. [L0378.081]
- Example: a list earning $70,000 to $130,000 per month with about 6,000 subscribers returns roughly $11 to $22 per subscriber per month. An average list is closer to $1. [L0378.081]
- Track earnings per subscriber and compare it with the average for your market. About $2 per subscriber per month is already twice a typical list. [L0378.082]
- Rule of thumb when communication funnels run correctly: about $1 of profit per month per name on the list. 200 names ≈ $200/month, 1,000 names ≈ $1,000/month, 10,000 names ≈ $10,000/month. Some markets earn more than this rule of thumb. [L0523.022]
- A list that grows without offers attached to its emails produces no revenue. Track subscriber count and revenue per subscriber, and measure opens and clicks alongside sales to see which story types work. [L0523.133]
- *Older advice (2021-22):* Judge email revenue against spend, since email is commonly evaluated by revenue returned per dollar invested. Track results per email so optimisation decisions rest on data. [L0609.017]

### Lead value

- A well-executed lead generation machine should generate about $2 per lead per month through email follow-up in the first year. The source range is printed as "$2-$2". [L0240.005]
