- A correctly running lead generation system earns about $2 to $5 per lead per month on average. Example: 1,000 leads emailed twice a week should produce roughly $2,000 to $5,000 in email revenue per month. Use this as the baseline to judge the system against. [L1076.018]
- Use the $2 (first-year) figure as the early target and the $2 to $5 range as the mature-system baseline. [L0240.005, L1076.018]

### Sign-up rates and welcome-flow benchmarks

- With a sign-up pop-up, expect roughly 8 to 15 newsletter sign-ups per 100 visitors. Plan the email list size with this range. [L0605.003]
- Average email open rates are about 20 to 30%, while SMS open rates are about 60 to 80%. SMS converts better but is harder to collect, because phone numbers are harder to get than emails and compliance rules apply. [L0605.004]
- Case (one test store, about two months old, 23 November to 23 December):
  - Total sales were about $17,000. 28% of revenue came from Klaviyo email (shown as 15% from flows and 14% from campaigns). [L0605.005]
  - In the last 30 days the store made about $14,000, with about 24% from Klaviyo. Of the roughly $2,000 from flows, about $1,447 came from the welcome flow. [L0605.005]
  - Only the basic flows existed: welcome and abandoned checkout. [L0605.005]
- Basic welcome and abandoned checkout flows were estimated to add about 20 to 30% to a store's revenue. This is an estimate from one case, not a guaranteed result. [L0605.006]
- In Klaviyo's flow list, use the Revenue and Revenue per recipient columns to decide which welcome flow to clone. The flow with the highest revenue is the proven base for a new welcome series. [L1470.002]

### Flow and campaign metrics

- Klaviyo flow analytics show Waiting, Delivered, Skipped, Open Rate, Click Rate, Placed Order Rate and Revenue per email. The time windows are 'Analytics (30 days)' and 'Last 365 days'. The 30-day view suits recent tests. [L0567.011]
- Judge each flow email by its own open rate, click rate, placed-order rate and revenue, not by the flow total, so weak messages become visible. Compare path 1 and path 2 email results side by side before changing anything. [L0567.012]
- A 9.5% click rate on a pain-point abandoned cart email is a very good result and a useful benchmark for that email type. [L0567.013]
- Observed 30-day Klaviyo results for later emails in one low-traffic store (A$ values; use as a rough benchmark only, not as a target): [L0567.051]

| Email | Open rate | Click rate | Placed order rate | Revenue |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| #3 Risk mitigation / social proof | 54.3% | 4.3% | 2.1% | A$221.50 |
| #4 Offer 15% | 56.7% | 11.1% | 4.4% | A$254.33 |
| #5 Offer 15% reminder (last chance) | 60.7% | 9.0% | 4.5% | A$262.00 |

### A/B testing and confidence

- In a completed subject line A/B test on the offer email, the winning variant had an open rate about 19.8% higher. The winner was chosen manually. [L0567.014]
- Confidence levels by sample size per variant: [L0570.003]

| Sample size per variant | Confidence |
|---|---|
| Around 1,000 | About 80% |
| Larger samples | About 95% |
| Very large samples | About 98% |

- Confidence is the likelihood that a change judged positive really is positive. [L0570.003]

### Email frequency testing

- *Older advice (2021-22):* Sending too many emails raises unsubscribes, and sending too few raises them too. If people unsubscribe or mark emails as spam while receiving one email per month or less, they have likely forgotten the brand and see the email as unfamiliar. [L0603.013]
- *Older advice (2021-22):* A steady decline in open rate can indicate too many emails and subscribers becoming blind to the sender. Check click-through rate and email conversion rate next, with email performance tracked in Google Analytics. [L0603.014]
- *Older procedure (2021-22):* How to test email frequency: [L0603.025]
  1. Start with 4 automated emails per week for days 0 to 30, 2 to 3 per week for days 30 to 60, and 1 per week from day 60.
  2. Increase frequency gradually rather than all at once.
  3. After each change, check unsubscribes first. If they rise, reduce frequency.
  4. Check open rate over time. A steady decline suggests too many emails are being sent.
  5. Check click-through rate and email conversion rate in Google Analytics.
  6. Keep experimenting until results plateau, which shows how far frequency can be pushed.

### Deliverability baselines and list health

- Before leaving the old provider, record a deliverability baseline of open rate, click rate, bounce rate, unsubscribe rate and spam complaints over 30, 60 and 90 days. Use these figures as the minimum expectation for the new provider. [L0633.007]
- Keep the deliverability target for the affected provider above a 30% open rate. Once it stays above that level, begin reducing the exclusion. The example used a Yahoo segment of 135 unengaged profiles that were not landing in inboxes. [L0864.010]
- Stop sending to a contact segment when its open rate falls to around 15% and its click rate falls to around 0.5%. [L0864.011]
- Read the thresholds together: 30% open is the recovery target for an affected provider, 15% open with 0.5% click is the stop-sending point, and 20 to 30% is the typical average open rate. [L0864.010, L0864.011, L0605.004]
- A campaign with about 40% open rate and about 50 clicks was treated as healthy in one example. Good overall results can still hide a sharp drop in a single age bucket, so check the buckets too. [L0864.012]
- After a large send, an unsubscribe rate higher than usual can still be acceptable if it stays within your normal margins. Check it against the provider breakdown before changing sending. [L0864.014]
- Audience breakdown totals can exceed campaign totals, because a recipient in several lists or segments is counted once for each membership. Do not add up segment numbers to get campaign totals. [L0864.017]
- Open rate for an audience bucket = recipients who opened ÷ total recipients in that bucket. Example: 141 opens out of 430 recipients gives 32.8%. [L0864.024]
- Bounce rate = bounced recipients ÷ sent recipients. Delivered = sent − bounced. Example: in a send of 999 recipients with 2 bounces, the bounce rate was 0.20% and 997 recipients (99.8%) were delivered. [L0864.025]
- The Performance by inbox provider chart on a campaign's Deliverability tab lets you hide either the Open rate or the Click rate series. Comparing one metric at a time across providers makes one weak provider easier to spot. [L0864.026]
- Read open rate and click rate together for each age bucket. A bucket with lower opens can still have strong clicks. In one example the 31 to 60 day bucket fell to about 40% opens but kept a good click rate, which suggests the people still opening remained engaged. [L0864.027]

### Customer satisfaction and surveys

- Net Promoter Score (survey question: "How likely are you to recommend to friends and family?") is the best metric alongside review ratings for measuring how much customers love you. [L0041.005]
- CSAT formula: (number of "very satisfied" + "satisfied" responses) ÷ total survey responses × 100, giving a percentage. Neutral, dissatisfied and very dissatisfied answers stay in the denominator and count against the score. [L0401.001]
- CSAT options are typically a five-point scale: very satisfied, satisfied, neutral, dissatisfied, very dissatisfied. The survey usually asks one question: how satisfied the customer is with the support received. [L0401.002]
- CSAT ratings below 4 (on a 5-point scale) are the most valuable negative feedback to review, since they show what blocks a high satisfaction score. Some reviewers also look at ratings under 5 as a broader signal. [L0401.003]
- Thematic analysis of CSAT text responses: [L0401.006]
  1. Export all CSAT scores as a CSV file from the helpdesk.
  2. Upload the file to a thematic analysis tool based on natural language processing, such as Thematic.
  3. Review the resulting keywords, pain points and recurring themes.
  4. Choose one or two specific themes to focus on at a time.
- The Gorgias Satisfaction view lists every ticket rated 4 stars or lower, which is wider than the below-4 threshold used for analysis. Filter to ratings under 4 before deciding which tickets to review, or the review includes neutral-leaning feedback that does not point to a clear problem. [L0401.016]
- Post-purchase surveys asking why the customer bought and what they will use the product for reveal the real audience and can feed Facebook interest targeting. [L1410.034]
- *Older advice (2021-22):* Send automated customer feedback surveys using recommended tools, set up conditional thank-you emails for survey completers, and determine a reward structure for participation. [L0203.011]
- *Older advice (2021-22):* Track the share of customers who leave a review as a key metric, and compare it before and after starting the post-sale call program. [L1011.012]

### Lead generation and cart recovery costs

- Lead magnet setup (eBook lead generation): [L0240.007]

| Component | Cost | Time to Set Up |
|---|---|---|
| eBook design (outsourced) | $20-$50 | Included in 10-20 hours |
| Landing page (default Shopify or Pagefly) | $0 | 1-2 hours |
| Klaviyo email automation | Free (first 500 contacts) | 5-10 hours for sequence |
| **Total setup** | **$20-$50** | **16-32 hours** |

- Thank-you and follow-up setup: [L0240.019]

| Component | Cost | Time to Set Up |
|---|---|---|
| Email sequence (9 emails in Klaviyo) | Free (first 500 contacts) | 5-10 hours |
| Facebook thank-you video ad | $5/day ad budget | 1-2 hours (filming + ad creation) |
| Phone follow-up calls | $0 (staff time) | 10-20 minutes per customer |
| **Total setup** | **$5-$10/day running** | **16-32 hours setup** |

- Channel setup and monthly investment: [L0240.022]

| Channel | Cost | Setup Time |
|---|---|---|
| Klaviyo abandoned cart email flow | Free (first 500 contacts) | 5-10 hours |
| Facebook Ads (audiences, campaigns, creatives) | $5-$50/day | 5-10 hours |
| Google Ads (audiences, campaigns, creatives) | $5-$50/day | 5-10 hours |
| **Total monthly investment** | **$300-$3,000** | **15-30 hours setup** |

- Target a cost per lead of $4 or less for a good audience and offer, and aim well below that where possible. [L0649.041]
- Reaching prospects within the first few minutes of cart abandonment improves contact success by 100 times compared with a 30-minute delay. Calling within five minutes increases conversion qualification by 21 times versus a 30-minute response time. [L0286.001]
- Abandoned checkout links remain active and clickable for 90 days after cart abandonment. [L0286.002]
- Test the Loom follow-up for about one month and track what happens to those leads. Compare their reply rate and purchase rate with leads who only received the standard reminder email. [L1049.016]

### Abandoned-checkout tracking sheet

- Logging every abandoned checkout in a shared sheet shows how many carts exist, when they were contacted, what happened on each call and how many were recovered. Shopify's default abandoned checkout view only shows whether an email was sent and whether the cart was recovered, so it hides the reasons for lost sales. [L0737.002]
- Tracking sheet layout (headers in row 1; row 2 contains only dashes as a separator): [L0737.004]

| Column | Field | Filled by |
|---|---|---|
| A | Date | automation |
| B | Name | automation |
| C | Phone number | automation |
| D | Email | automation |
| E | Cart value | automation |
| F | Products | automation |
| G | Shipping | automation |
| H | Recovered | paid-order automation or team member |
| I | Link to cart | automation |
| J | Notes | phone team |

  Columns can be added or removed, but these ten cover the core needs of a tracking sheet. [L0737.004]
- An example sheet also includes a column showing whether the customer was emailed, which the standard automated column set does not include. This status column shows which carts received outreach before a call was made. [L0737.018]
- Review the sheet once a day or every few days to see how many carts were abandoned, when outreach happened, what came of each contact, and how many were recovered. Regular review makes gaps in the process visible early. [L0737.019]
- Reviewing the sheet after several weeks reveals causes of low recovery, such as slow response times, not leaving voicemails, or customers citing high prices. Each cause points to a specific process change. [L0737.016]

### Retargeting ROAS and conversion benchmarks

- Retargeting achieves 2 to 10 times higher conversion rates than cold traffic, because users who have already interacted with the site are further along in the purchase journey. [L0033.002]
- The top end for direct-response e-commerce conversion is described as 2 to 3%. Most visitors who are interested but do not buy can be recovered by retargeting, for example via Google. [L0637.040]
- Retargeting makes it reasonable to keep an ad with low attention and click-through rate running, because the cold traffic it brings can later be retargeted as a larger audience. [L0999.026]
- Meta remarketing campaigns should deliver the highest ROAS of the ad channels used. This held even after Apple's iOS 14.5 update, because small budgets aimed at highly qualified visitors produce a very high return on ad spend. [L1020.011]
- Judge remarketing budgets by ROAS. [L0854.095]
- *Older (2022):* Facebook retargeting below 1x ROAS is the threshold for cutting spend. [L0854.095]
- *Older (2022):* A Facebook remarketing campaign should reach at least 10x return on ad spend. 20x to 50x is typical for strong campaigns. Below about 20x points to audiences that are too broad or frequency that is too high. [L1113.065]
- Use the 1x threshold to decide when to cut spend and the 10x minimum to judge whether a campaign is performing. [L0854.095, L1113.065]
- Allocate roughly 10% of your daily Shopping Ads budget to Google Display remarketing. Example: a $10/day Shopping budget gives $2/day for remarketing. [L0189.002]
- *Older (2022):* Facebook DPA example: $100 on Google Shopping versus about $10 on DPA remarketing, showing remarketing is a smaller share of spend early on. [L0969.106]
- Shopify notifies Meta when someone buys on the store, and that buyer is added to the purchase audience. The 7-day purchase audience always holds buyers from the last 7 days, and the same logic applies to the 30- and 180-day versions. [L1023.010]

### Promotion budgets for warm audiences

- Audience budget formula: Audience budget = audience size × frequency × remarketing CPM ÷ 1,000. CPM is the cost per 1,000 impressions, so the result is the total spend needed to show the ads that many times to that many people. [L0757.034, L1204.061]
- Example, Google: 50,000 people × 6 impressions × $1.50 ÷ 1,000 = $450 total campaign budget, about $150 per day over 3 days. [L1204.061]
- Example, Facebook: 130,000 people (Facebook website visitors, Instagram and Facebook engagement audiences combined) × 6 impressions × $5 ÷ 1,000 = $3,900 total campaign budget, about $1,300 per day over 3 days. [L1204.061]
- Worked budget example (180-day visitor audiences, frequency 6, 3-day promotion): [L0757.036]

| Audience | Size | Frequency | Remarketing CPM (historic) | Budget |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Google: Website Visitors - 180 Days | 50,000 | 6 | $1.50 | $450 |
| Google total | 50,000 | 6 | $1.50 | $450 |
| Facebook: Website Visitors - 180 Days | 50,000 | 6 | $5.00 | - |
| Facebook: Insta Engagement - 180 Days | 40,000 | 6 | $5.00 | - |
| Facebook: FB Engagement - 180 Days | 40,000 | 6 | $5.00 | - |
| Facebook total | 130,000 | 6 | $5.00 | $3,900 |

  Daily budgets for this 3-day promotion: Google $150/day and Facebook $1,300/day. [L0757.036]
- ROAS means return on ad spend, and budgeting is done to get the highest ROAS possible. Frequency is the average number of times each person sees the ad. CPM is the cost per 1,000 impressions. [L0757.038]
- Calculating the ad budget for a promotion: [L1204.062]
  1. Count each warm audience. In Facebook Audience Manager, read the size of the 180-day website visitor, Facebook engagement and Instagram engagement audiences. In Google, open the shared library of audiences.
  2. Set a frequency target, typically 6 to 10 impressions per person for a 3 to 5 day campaign.
  3. Look up the historic CPM for warm audiences in the Facebook Ads and Google Ads accounts, using the CPM column.
  4. Apply the formula for each platform and set the budget.
- For a 3 to 5 day promotion, aim for a frequency of about 6 to 10, meaning each person sees the ad 6 to 10 times. This is the planning figure for budgeting. [L1204.063]
- Historic CPM for warm audiences was roughly $2 to $5 in the example account, with $1.50 on Google and $5 on Facebook. Use the actual CPM from your own accounts, not a planning assumption of about $10, which is set high on purpose. [L1204.064]
- Set promotion campaigns with a total campaign budget and a start and end date, not a daily budget. Spend usually starts slightly lower on the first day and ramps up toward the end as the platform gathers data. [L1204.065]
- For a first test with a limited budget, use 7-day or 30-day audiences instead of 180-day audiences; at least 30-day audiences are recommended. In the example, 7-day audiences on both platforms came to about $78 in total. [L1204.066]
- Measuring promotion impact: [L1204.060]
  1. Pull the tracked promotion revenue from Klaviyo, Facebook and Google.
  2. Compare the combined total with the total revenue for the same month to find the promotion share of sales.
  3. Check return on ad spend per platform to see whether the ad spend was efficient.

### Google Analytics audiences

- Google Analytics audiences for remarketing should be built from normal traffic. The audience pool grows from qualified visitors rather than artificial numbers. [L0805.055]
- When creating Google Analytics audiences, enable remarketing in the audience definition. Otherwise the audience cannot be used for remarketing on Google properties. [L1204.007]
- In Google Analytics, a purchaser audience uses the condition transactions greater than 0. It works only when enhanced e-commerce tracking is active, and it is typically built as a 7-day and a 180-day list. [L1204.010]
- Google Analytics can compare revenue and conversion rate of first-time visitors against returning visitors. This shows how much retargeting can lift overall conversion. [L1203.017]
- In one example, returning visitors who came back more than once converted at a rate 50% higher than visitors who came only once. [L1203.018]
- Build a customer avatar in Facebook Analytics: [L1243.012]
  1. Open the newer Facebook Analytics, which offers segments based on user actions on your site.
  2. Create a segment of people who purchased in the past 28 days or the past 90 days.
  3. Review the page likes and demographics of that purchaser segment.
  4. Use those results to describe your customer avatar.
- *Older procedure (2021-22):* Create Google Analytics audiences and send them to Google Ads: [L1390.027]
  1. Go to Admin > Property Settings > Audience Definitions > Audiences.
  2. If remarketing is not enabled yet, click Enable remarketing, then Next step.
  3. Under Audience destinations, select your Google Ads account as the destination before saving. This imports the audiences into Google Ads as targeting options.
  4. Ignore the default All Users audience.
  5. Click + New audience, then Create new.
  6. Build the conditions in the Audience Builder.
  7. Name the audience and click Next step. If Google Ads is linked, select it as the destination and send the audience there.
- *Older (2021-22):* In the example Google Analytics audience list, the All Users audience has a membership duration of 30 days. [L1390.029]
- *Older (2021-22):* Audience Builder categories in Google Analytics: Demographics (age 18-24, 25-34, 35-44, 45-54, 55-64, 65+; gender female, male, unknown; language), Technology, Behaviour, Date of First Session, Traffic Sources, Enhanced E-commerce, and Other Category (Affinity Category, In-Market Segment, Location). [L1390.030]
- *Older (2021-22):* Location targeting under Other Category > Location lets you segment by city, region, country, subcontinent or continent. Use it for offers limited to certain areas, such as free shipping only where you can afford it. [L1390.031]
- *Older (2021-22):* Behaviour options: Sessions, Days Since Last Session, Transactions per user, and Session Duration per user. Example: an audience of users with more than three sessions on the website. [L1390.032]
- *Older (2021-22):* Session Duration is set per session, for example Session Duration > 30 seconds. Conditions can be layered with AND or OR, and Date of First Session can limit the audience to visitors since, before, after or between dates. [L1390.033]
- *Older (2021-22):* Enhanced E-commerce audience conditions include revenue per user, amount spent, products bought, brand and variant. With enough volume, you can build an audience of people who bought one product and offer them relevant upsells. [L1390.035]
- *Older (2021-22):* To target by page, change the condition dropdown from its default to Page, then choose Behaviour. Use contains with part of the URL. For Shopify product pages, enter products, or enter the product name slug after the last slash for one specific product. [L1390.037]

### Giveaway measurement

- The funnel method was reported to generate about 500,000 leads. This figure is an unverified estimate and was not supported with data. [L0797.007]
- Facebook ad set results from the example giveaway campaign (values as shown): [L0797.015]

| Metric | Value |
|---|---|
| Reach | 862,845 |
| Frequency | 2.34 |
| Cost per result | $35.40 |
| Amount spent | $10,832.32 |
| Impressions | 2,017,463 |
| CPM | $5.37 |
| Link clicks | 27,761 |
| CPC | $0.39 |
| CTR | 1.38% |
| Website purchases | 306 |
| AOV | $129.20 |
| Facebook-reported revenue | $39,535.2 |

  Klaviyo results for the same giveaway: [L0797.015]

| Klaviyo metric | Value |
|---|---|
| Leads | 18,726 |
| Net new sales (30-day window) | 145 |
| Revenue (30-day window) | $16,715.70 |
| Net new sales since giveaway to now | 736 |
| Revenue since giveaway to now | $108,424.26 |

- Facebook reported about $40,000 in revenue for the giveaway ad set, while Klaviyo's 30-day revenue was $16,715.70. Klaviyo's default view undercounts because email-driven purchases arrive with a delay. [L0797.016]
- Purchases from email flows may not track straight away, so the real revenue from giveaway traffic can be about double what Klaviyo reports by default. Measuring only cold traffic reveals this hidden revenue. [L0797.017]
- Measuring true giveaway revenue in Klaviyo: [L0797.018]
  1. Build a segment of people who were added to the giveaway list, filtered by date added.
  2. Keep only people who had not placed an order before the giveaway. This removes past customers already known to the brand.
  3. Keep only people who placed at least one order after entering the giveaway, since non-buyers generate no revenue.
  4. Exclude everyone who appears on the lists of earlier giveaways and campaigns.
  5. Read the revenue and net new sales of this segment, which measure absolute cold traffic.
- When giveaway revenue is measured correctly with cold-traffic segments, return on ad spend is about double the default figure, and cost per purchase is about half. [L0797.019]
- Return on ad spend from the same spend, two ways: [L0797.024]
  - Platform-reported ROAS = $39,535.20 ÷ $10,832.32 ≈ 3.65
  - Klaviyo 30-day ROAS = $16,715.70 ÷ $10,832.32 ≈ 1.54
  - Cost per Klaviyo lead = $10,832.32 ÷ 18,726 ≈ $0.58, if every entrant counts as a lead
- Meta counted 306 website purchases, while Klaviyo's 30-day window showed only 145 net new sales, about 47% of the Meta count. Klaviyo revenue since the giveaway ($108,424.26) was about 6.5 times its 30-day revenue ($16,715.70), so the attribution window changes the reported result a lot. [L0797.025]
- Many advertisers abandon instant giveaway funnels because they only read the default platform numbers and cannot see the real results. Measure revenue through the email platform with a cold-traffic segment before judging the funnel. [L0797.026]
- Name every giveaway audience list in Klaviyo with country, campaign sequence number, year and product, for example "UK 1 2023 Product Giveaway". Consistent names make it possible to build exclusion segments that remove earlier campaign entrants later. [L0797.027]

### Owned-channel baselines

- Track baseline numbers for organic traffic in analytics, opt-ins per day or week, remarketing ROAS and units sold, then monitor how they grow over time. These show whether content and email work. [L0941.093]

## Influencer, affiliate, organic and content measurement

### Define objectives and KPIs before launch

- Define clear KPIs before an influencer campaign starts so results can be measured against exact goals. Without set targets, there is nothing specific to check the campaign against afterwards. [L0630.002, L0786.001]
- A clearly defined campaign objective is the basis for accurate measurement and a fair evaluation of success. It also shows how each influencer partnership contributed to the overall marketing strategy. [L0787.007, L0868.009]
- If the goal is sales, track sales and conversion metrics directly. Conversion rate is the key metric for sales from an influencer campaign. If the goal is brand awareness, the focus shifts to awareness measures, and tracking conversions matters less. [L0630.007, L0786.008]
- Set the success metric before a TikTok test starts. A challenge account can be judged on views, but the result should still be checked against sales per million views, because a video can go viral and still fail to produce sales. [L0764.049]
- Record followers, views and orders as separate metrics. A test can add tens of thousands of followers while the product still loses money, so audience growth must not hide a weak product. [L1385.021]
- Track sales per million views as the success metric for TikTok content. A video can go viral and still fail this metric, which means the product or offer is not converting. [L0764.024]
- Test a product by measuring sales per million views, because a product can go viral and still not convert. Only this figure shows whether the product is worth scaling. [L1380.047]

### Vet influencers before a collaboration

- Genuine engagement from an audience matters more for a campaign than a high follower count. Evaluate engagement before working with an influencer. [L0479.026, L0480.020]
- Track engagement rather than follower count. Likes, comments, tags and shares show whether posts reach the right audience, while bought followers from low-value countries do not buy. (Older advice, 2021.) [L0801.133]
- A follower target can be unrealistic and easy to fake. Paid followers from cheap regions have no engagement, so the KPI should measure the engagement of followers. (Older advice, 2021.) [L0801.134]
- Review an influencer's platform analytics before collaborating, because engagement quality can strongly affect how much your customers buy. [L0479.029]
- Check engagement with this procedure:
  1. Ask the influencer for their native analytics: Instagram Insights, YouTube analytics or TikTok analytics.
  2. Review the engagement snapshot on each platform the influencer uses.
  3. Decide on the collaboration only after reviewing these numbers.
  
  Most influencers provide these analytics freely when asked. [L0480.021]
- Ask the influencer to send the engagement numbers of their post (comments, shares, likes, interaction rate), in addition to checking the platform analytics yourself. [L0630.004]
- Social Blade gives a cross-platform overview of an influencer across all channels. It is especially useful before collaborating with a larger influencer. [L0479.027, L0480.022]
- Do not buy likes, comments or engagement from SMM panels. Fake engagement distorts the data and may mislead the platform. Some sellers use it to lower CPM, but this is not recommended. [L0638.044]

### Platform analytics tools

- Built-in tools show a post or account's engagement and audience data: Instagram Insights (business account), Facebook Insights, Twitter Analytics, YouTube Analytics and TikTok Analytics. They show comments, shares, likes and interaction rate. [L0479.028, L0630.003, L0786.003]
- In the TikTok app, open Creator tools > Analytics. The overview shows video views, profile views, likes, comments and shares. The per-video panel shows total play time, average watch time, watch full video and new followers. [L1186.016]
- Instagram's Edits app shows total Reels views, reach and net follower change over a selectable period, such as 60 days. Tap a single reel to see its own insights. [L1453.017]
- Instagram added weekly and monthly recaps and celebration tools in Insights to highlight milestones with followers. [L1504.005]
- Social listening tools track how often a brand, hashtag, keyword or campaign name is mentioned. Brand Watch is paid and widely used. Mention is an automated tool that counts brand and hashtag mentions. Without a listening tool, count mentions manually by searching the brand name, a keyword or the campaign name on each platform during the campaign, and note how often it appears on each specific platform. [L0630.005, L0786.005, L0786.006]

### Set up tracking for each creator and partner

- Use UTM tracking parameters on every link sent to a paid influencer for an ad, so traffic from that specific source is tracked accurately. The format is:

```
?utm_source=[influencer_name]&utm_medium=banner&utm_campaign=trial
```
[L0183.008]

- Example: for an ad placed on the source's own site the UTM tracking link is:

```
?utm_source=yourstore&utm_medium=banner&utm_campaign=trial
```
[L0183.009]

- Use UTM links for bloggers and creators whose results cannot be read from coupon codes alone. [L0790.078]
- Create a UTM link for every partner. Give the tagged link specifically to partners running paid ads, so clicks can be attributed to that placement in Google Analytics. [L0528.041]
- Create a separate affiliate or landing link for each creator or channel. Then check Shopify's order source report for orders per channel, such as Instagram, TikTok and other sources. Referral Candy also reports sales for each link. [L1379.039]
- In Referral Candy, create an affiliate link for any product in the store and give the creator a referral link. Grant the creator temporary staff access restricted to orders, so they can only see sales from the agreed period. [L1379.040]
- Affiliate tracking links or codes show which sales came from a specific influencer, so the sales source is visible directly. Affiliate marketing tools show which source or influencer each sale came from when an influencer shares a tracked link or code. [L0630.008, L0786.010]
- Promo codes help track creator-driven sales, but many buyers do not enter them, so codes capture only part of the impact. Do not use promo codes as the only success measure for long-term partnerships. [L0783.017]
- The embed code for each Refersion banner contains that partner's unique affiliate tracking code. When a customer clicks the banner, the link carries the code automatically, so the sale is credited and the commission paid without the partner placing a custom link. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0288.004]

#### UTM conventions in the sources

Two conventions appear in the sources. Use each for its own purpose and do not mix them in one report.

| Use | utm_source | utm_medium | utm_campaign | Ids |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Free-trial or influencer link (ad placed by an influencer or on a site) | Influencer name, for example yourstore | banner | trial | L0183.008, L0183.009 |
| Paid partner placement, built in the UTM builder | Campaign source = ad type, for example banner ad | Campaign medium = ad format, for example banner | Campaign name = partner domain without http or.com, for example surfingwaves for surfingwaves.com | L0528.039, L0528.040 |

- UTM builder fields: campaign source is the ad type, campaign medium is the format, and campaign name is the partner's domain without http or.com. The resulting link carries these values as utm_source, utm_medium and utm_campaign parameters. [L0528.040]

### Affiliate economics and attribution

- Affiliate payouts process in batches throughout the month or once monthly, not automatically. Payments are delayed from sale completion to verify that customers are genuine, typically withheld for up to 30 days. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0213.005]
- Affiliate software gives each affiliate a unique tracking link. Cookies credit them for sales within a specified timeframe. If a customer clicks the link but purchases after the cookie window closes, you receive the sale but the affiliate receives no commission. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0213.008]
- Set the cookie duration to 30 days, 60 days or unlimited. High-ticket products often convert over 2-3 months, so a 30-day window risks not crediting the affiliate who generated the initial interest. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0213.009]
- Affiliate marketing gives controllable, predictable costs, unlike paid traffic. You know your exact commission rate per sale in advance, which supports cash-flow forecasting and financial planning. Paid advertising has variable daily costs beyond your control. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0213.013]
- E-commerce businesses typically allocate 10% of annual revenue to Google Ads. Affiliate marketing is more cost-efficient, because you pay commissions only on completed sales, not for clicks or impressions. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0213.017]
- Organic traffic usually has a lower cost per acquisition than paid traffic, because each click carries no direct cost. Use organic and other non-paid channels alongside paid ads, especially for traffic too expensive to target with paid ads. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L1507.011]
- Example: if a referred prospect pays 4,000 USD for E-Converts membership, the referrer receives 1,000 USD in commission, which is 25%. [L0214.002]
- During the E-Converts booking call, the field labelled 'Did someone recommend you?' must contain the referrer's Discord name. Otherwise the referral is not tracked and the commission is not paid. [L0214.004]
- About 10 new members join E-Converts each month through referrals from existing members, not through paid advertising. [L0214.008]
- A creator's story with about 5,000 views and an audience that closely matches the niche can achieve around 20 to 100 sales. Small numbers of sales can still justify the spend when the product margin is strong. [L1379.038]

### Paid partner placements: ROAS and CPC

- Cost per click (CPC) equals the monthly ad cost divided by the number of clicks. Example: 200 USD per month with 100 clicks gives a CPC of 2 USD. [L0528.034]
- Return on ad spend (ROAS) equals sales generated divided by ad spend. Example: 200 USD of ad spend that brings in 2,000 USD in sales gives a 10X ROAS. [L0528.035]
- A 10X return on ad spend is treated as an acceptable threshold for paying a partner for an ad placement. [L0528.036]
- Partners usually do not charge per click. Calculate CPC yourself by dividing the monthly ad cost by the clicks counted in Google Analytics for that partner's UTM link. [L0528.037]
- Example: a partner charging 500 USD per month brought 2,000 USD in sales with a 5 USD CPC, which is a 4X ROAS. After the free trial, drop this partner, because it does not make money. [L0528.038]
- Partner placement procedure:
  1. Open the UTM builder and enter the destination URL, which is your store page.
  2. Set campaign source to the ad type, for example banner ad.
  3. Set campaign medium to the ad format, for example banner.
  4. Set campaign name to the partner's domain without http or the.com ending, for example surfingwaves for surfingwaves.com.
  5. Generate the link and give that tagged link to the partner for paid ad placements.
  6. After the campaign, count the clicks in Google Analytics, calculate CPC from the ad cost, then calculate ROAS from the sales for that month.
  7. Keep the partner only if the ROAS justifies the monthly cost. [L0528.039]
- Banner ads on forums and niche sites can be surprisingly cheap, sometimes around a couple of hundred dollars per month. This makes constant visibility in front of a target audience affordable for many early businesses. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0525.033]

### Measure influencer campaign results

- Compare the same metrics before and after the influencer campaign. This before-and-after comparison is especially important for a first influencer campaign. [L0630.010, L0786.011]
- Benchmark influencer results against other channels, such as Facebook ads, TikTok ads and Google Analytics data. This shows whether the influencer budget is worth the spend compared with paid channels. [L0630.010, L0786.012]
- To measure which influencer generated conversions:
  1. Survey customers and ask where they first heard of the influencer or brand, or run audience surveys or social media polls to gauge how the campaign was perceived.
  2. Check brand analytics for traffic and click-through rate coming from the influencer.
  3. If affiliate links are used, read the sales source data from the affiliate tool. [L0630.009, L0786.009]
- Influencer log procedure, kept in one spreadsheet that functions as the influencer database:
  1. Record the influencer's name and whether they have posted for you before.
  2. Log each step: contacted, replied, product sent, product received, photo sent for approval, and post executed.
  3. Record whether the influencer sold anything.
  4. If there were no sales, judge whether the impressions and content quality were worth the cost.
  5. Keep all of this in a spreadsheet, which functions as the influencer database. [L0790.074]
- Judge influencer performance with several measures together: total traffic and social impressions, Google Analytics traffic, new emails collected, and the mix of new versus existing buyers. Judging on direct sales alone undervalues brand awareness. [L0790.076]
- Do not spend a large sum on one high-profile post without a way to measure its results, such as coupon codes or real-time analytics. Without measurement, the spend can feel like money lost. [L0790.080]
- Organic reach is not the same as conversion. Judge a product by orders per thousand views and by whether it is a real purchase, not by follower growth or comment volume alone. [L0655.023]
- Use total reach to see how many people the content is shown to and whether a creator is worth the cost. If the content will be repurposed in paid ads, its quality matters more than the size of the creator's audience. [L0783.019]
- Benchmark creator content costs at 500 to 5,000 USD per million views. Use this range as a minimum when judging the economics of view-based content. [L1367.014]
- Creator ROI procedure:
  1. Measure the return on investment for each influencer.
  2. Compare the influencers and identify the ones giving the highest return.
  3. Shift part of the budget toward those top performers and increase the posting cadence with them. [L0783.020]
- Track engagement metrics per platform for each repurposed video. This shows which videos to renew, keep running or stop. [L0784.022]
- Record multi-product TikTok tests per post in a spreadsheet, not by account totals, since aggregate account views hide which product is working. A weekly review is enough. [L0561.014]

### Audience sentiment and indirect value

- Judge creator campaigns first by audience sentiment, meaning the quality of the comments and the conversation the post starts, before counting likes. A post that triggers buying questions and positive discussion is worth more to a brand than a high like count. [L0783.016]
- Likes, comments, shares and total interactions are the standard social engagement metrics. They are not the main success measure for creator campaigns. Track conversion rate, meaning how many people take the desired action after seeing the creator's content, for each creator where it can be measured. For long-term partnerships this is harder to track, so read it alongside sentiment and reach. [L0783.022]
- Some creator value is indirect. Repeated product exposure builds brand recall, so people may later buy in a store or without a code. For long-term partnerships this effect must be accepted as part of the return, not expected to appear in direct tracking. [L0783.018]
- Use shares as the main metric for audience-building content, because a share shows that people will pass the content on and that its reach will grow. Watch the cost per engagement as a secondary check. [L0487.043]
- Targeting is working when cost per engagement falls to almost nothing. In the example, a post had a cost per engagement under one cent. Judge posts by engagement and shares before looking at sales. [L0487.044]
- Genuine engagement for a campaign matters more than a high follower count. Evaluate engagement before working with an influencer. [L0480.020]
- Rule of thumb: about 10% of likes should convert into followers. An account with only about 1% like-to-follow conversion is performing badly even if views are high. [L0764.022]

### TikTok video signals and diagnostics

- A winning video clearly outperforms the rest of the account. Example: if most videos get about 1,000 views and one gets 30,000, that video is the account's winner and should guide new content. [L0795.018]
- Once analytics show high average watch time, a high completion rate and a good engagement ratio, create more videos of that type. [L0795.022]
- Store sessions are the diagnostic. Millions of views with few store sessions means the product or video does not create interest. Many sessions without sales points to the store itself. Check the sessions-to-views relationship before changing the store. [L0352.004]
- When a video gets many views but few store sessions, the cause is most often the product itself, not the store, because curious viewers who see value click through. Check store sessions for each viral video. [L0795.020]
- Use TikTok analytics to check watch time, which videos perform and trends from the last 7 days. This data shows which videos to recreate or which direction to take. [L1380.028]
- Keep a written log of the upload time of each video, then compare times with results. Re-upload a winning idea at the same time it previously went viral. [L0795.024]
- Check the follower gender split in TikTok analytics to decide who your videos are made for. In the example, followers were 61.1% female and 38.9% male. [L1186.017]
- New TikTok accounts may not show retention charts and can register views incompletely. In one example, an account showed about 30 million views when the real count was 80-90 million. Verify numbers on an older account before drawing conclusions. [L1186.023]
- Track the four engagement signals together per video: likes, comments, shares and bookmarks. Strong numbers across all four matter more than a high like count alone, because TikTok distribution responds to all four. [L1384.050]

#### Engagement ratios

| Ratio | Definition | Benchmark | Ids |
|---|---|---|---|
| Views to likes | Total views ÷ total likes. Example: 1:8 is very strong for videos with millions of views. | 1:8 very strong for millions of views; 1:10 or lower still considered good, and lower is better | L1384.001 |
| Comments to views | A healthy engagement ratio of 1 comment for every 10 views (1:10) | 1:10 | L0795.019 |
| Likes to followers | Share of likes that convert into followers | About 10% is the rule of thumb; about 1% is poor | L0764.022 |

- Worked example: 250,000 views with 25,000 likes gives a 1:10 ratio, still in the good range. The same 250,000 views with only 5,000 likes gives 1:50, a weak ratio that signals the video will likely be pushed less widely. [L1384.051]
- Case: a video with 24.5 million views and 3.2 million likes had a ratio of about 1:8, with many comments, shares and bookmarks. This was a standout result, because ratios that good are rare at that view count. The video was 8 seconds long and reached about 20 million views within 24 hours of first going viral. The creator attributed this to combining a strong ASMR element, good timing and a strong hook. [L1384.003]
- Case: a 51 million view video with about 3 million likes had a ratio of roughly 1:17. It performed because it was a long video with very high watch time. High watch time can offset a weaker like ratio. [L1384.004]

#### Watch time and retention

- Average watch time is the most important metric for judging a video and for TikTok distribution. It can exceed the video's length when viewers rewatch it. The goal is for average watch time to exceed the video's actual length. A video with a weak like ratio can still be pushed widely if its watch time is strong. [L0795.021, L1384.005]
- Completion rate, meaning the share of viewers who watch to the end, is the second key signal. Benchmarks: above 50% is good, around 65% is strong, and up to about 80% is achievable for smaller videos. Completion rates tend to fall as a video reaches millions of views. [L0795.021, L1384.007]
- Watch-through percentage tends to decline as a video is pushed to larger audiences. A strong rate at small scale does not guarantee the same rate at scale. Track the percentage over time and do not expect it to stay constant. [L1384.008]
- Watch-time check: average watch time divided by video length. Example: a 12-second video with a 15.4-second average watch time gives about 1.28. This is strong, because the result is above 1.0 and viewers replay the clip. [L1384.052]
- Example: a 12-second video had an average watch time of 15.4 seconds, considered very good because viewers rewatched it. Average watch time above the video length is the target benchmark. [L1384.006]
- Watch retention is the key metric for short video. Aim for viewers to watch to the end, or at least more than 50% of the video, because higher retention improves distribution. [L1457.002]

#### Traffic sources and distribution

- Track the share of views from the For You Page, not from your own followers. A high For You share, reported around 97% in one example, signals real growth to new viewers. Views mainly from your profile suggest limited reach. [L1384.009]
- In per-video analytics, the traffic source labelled 'Reached audience' shows the share of views coming from the For You Page. A rising share means the video reaches viewers who do not follow the account. [L1384.053]
- Per-video traffic source analytics usually appear within about 24 hours of posting, sometimes up to two days. Detailed analytics were reported to require a minimum follower threshold of around 1,000 followers, though the lesson was unclear on the exact number. [L1384.010]
- Choose Instagram posting times from the audience activity data in Instagram Insights, because the best time differs for every account. There is no universal best hour. [L0796.003]

#### Creator Fund and platform payouts

- The TikTok Creator Fund paid about 30 dollars per million views on average, and one month of Creator Fund earnings reached about 2,000 dollars. Eligibility requires at least 10,000 followers. [L1384.036]
- Creator Fund payments are not recommended for product accounts, because joining may reduce organic reach. Consider joining only after you have proven products and an established account. [L1384.037]
- Reach was reported to drop near the end of the year when Creator Fund budgets appeared to run low, then recover after the fund reset in the new year. This is an unverified observation and should be treated as a warning sign. [L1384.038]
- Snapchat Spotlight payouts now require at least 10,000 unique views and snaps submitted on at least 5 days. Plan the posting schedule around both conditions. [L1504.016]

### Instagram Trial Reels

- Find Trial Reel insights at Profile > Reels tab > 'Drafts and trial reels' > 'Trial reels', where all uploaded Trial Reels are listed. Open a reel and tap 'View more insights' to see the full data. [L1397.014]
- The Trial Reel insights screen shows the banner 'This trial reel is being shown to non-followers' and a 'Share to everyone' button. Use the button only after the insights show the reel is performing well. [L1397.015]
- Example Trial Reel insights, for comparison with a typical reel:

| Metric | Example Trial Reel | Typical reel |
|---|---|---|
| Views | 7,973 | n/a |
| Share of viewers who are non-followers | 100% | n/a |
| Accounts reached | 6,730 (about 6,700) | n/a |
| Total watch time | 14h 41m 0s | n/a |
| Average watch time | 7 seconds | n/a |
| Watch-through rate | 74.1% | 68.6% |

[L1397.016]

- Track watch time, interactions relative to views, the like ratio and the view rate past the first 3 seconds. Compare them against your typical reel, not against absolute numbers. [L1397.017]
- In the example, 8,000 views was high performance for a Trial Reel, and 3,000 views still meant reaching 3,000 people who had never seen the account. Viral reach is unlikely on Trial Reels, because they reach only a small group at first. [L1397.018]
- Share a Trial Reel to the main feed only when its watch-through rate and interactions beat your typical reel. In the example, a 74.1% watch-through rate against a 68.6% typical rate and a 7-second average watch time signalled that the reel would likely do well on the feed. [L1397.019]
- Trial Reel insights take time to build, because the reel reaches a limited, non-follower group. Do not judge a reel on its first hours of data. [L1397.020]
- In Instagram's reel insights, the view rate past the first 3 seconds can be filtered by All, Followers or Non-followers. For a Trial Reel, read the Non-followers value, since that is the audience it reaches. [L1397.023]
- For a Trial Reel, use the 'Accounts reached' figure to see how many distinct non-follower accounts it reached, and compare that with total views. Judge the reel by how many new accounts it reached, not by view count alone. [L1397.024]

### Revenue per million views and product decisions

- Revenue per million views (dollars per million views) = total sales divided by the number of millions of views. Example: 10 million views and 7,000 USD in sales gives 700 USD per million. Use it to compare products and angles, not just raw views. [L0501.019]
- Example: 5,000 USD in sales on 8 million views gives 625 USD per million views, below the 1,000 USD benchmark. [L1444.020]

#### Benchmarks

Sources give different thresholds. Each applies to a different market or purpose, as shown below.

| Context | Benchmark | Ids |
|---|---|---|
| USA, as seen by the author | About 1,000-3,000 USD per million views; sometimes 3,000-4,000 with strong angles; the low end can go under 100 USD | L0501.020 |
| High-income countries such as the UK | Similar to the USA | L0501.020 |
| Other, lower-income countries | Roughly 100-300 USD per million. The author aims at an average of at least 1,000 USD per million, with 500 USD per million as the minimum acceptable | L0501.020 |
| Viable product (general) | About 1,000 USD in sales per million views. Products earning around 100 USD per million are not worth continuing and should be dropped | L1444.019 |
| Rough observed example of a viral product | About 2,000 USD revenue per million views | L0655.019 |
| TikTok product videos, rough benchmark for testing | About 500 USD in sales per million views | L0706.013 |
| Same product, different videos | Roughly 100 USD per million on one video and a few thousand USD per million on another | L0657.016 |

- A 10 million view video should produce at least some sales. In a higher-income country it should produce more than 1,000 USD. Zero sales from a large view count is a red flag. Check the geographic split of views, since a viral video mostly seen in lower-converting countries will not sell well even with many views. [L0501.021]
- Check where views come from before judging a product. A large share from high-income countries (USA, UK, Canada) converts much better than views from other countries. Read the sales-per-view benchmark per geographic market. [L0501.030]
- Track views and sales together and check whether they match. Strong views with no sales point to a problem with the product, store, price or offer rather than with traffic. [L1444.021]
- Comments asking to buy do not equal purchases. Many visitors and commenters who say they want to buy never complete a purchase, so judge the product by actual sales and store sessions. [L1444.023]
- Views do not equal sales. The same product on the same account can convert at roughly 100 USD per million views on one video and at a few thousand USD per million on another, depending on how well the video shows the product. Track revenue per view for each video, not view count alone. [L0657.016]
- Organic views on a viral product are not a conversion result. Judge a product by orders per thousand views and by whether it is a real purchase, not by follower growth or comment volume alone. [L0655.023]
- A product that loses views late in week 2, for example from several thousand views in week 1 down to a few hundred, is a bad sign. Start a new account if you want to keep the product, otherwise drop it. [L0501.023]
- Read a low sales-per-view result carefully. If revenue per view is poor but still steady after 24 hours, the videos may have died down. If old views converted well but have stopped, the product can still be improved rather than dropped. [L0501.029]
- Views do not reliably predict sales. A video with tens of millions of views can sell very little, while a product with a good offer can convert well from far fewer views. Judge a product by its orders and conversion, not by its viral reach. [L0372.003]
- Price check before testing: compare the average unit price with the ad's stated price, because a price mismatch in creative lowers conversion and makes results hard to read. Track views, revenue and the number of creators or ad variations to see which parts of the structure matter. [L0707.027]
- Revenue per 1,000 views = ad revenue divided by views, multiplied by 1,000. For three variants of the same winning creative:
  - 165.67k revenue on 8.38m views gives about 19.8 per 1,000 views
  - 106.90k on 4.78m gives about 22.4
  - 64.66k on 2.99m gives about 21.6

  Rank variants by this number, not by views alone. The variant with the most views had the lowest revenue per 1,000 views. [L0707.034]

#### Cases

- Viral case: a store uploaded 12 TikTok videos and went viral on day four. The video reached about 1.5 million views in the first 12 hours and about 600 sales that day. The video itself converted poorly because it was a joke with a negative angle, and sales were about 500 the next day. The account was then shadowbanned and sales slowed sharply. [L0372.001]
- Race Car case: an honesty-angle video whose message was that the product does not even work had about 1.8 million views and gained about 1,500 followers in 24 hours. The store price was 30 USD and only 2 orders were confirmed, against an expected 10 to 15 orders. Lesson: the marketing angle drew attention but did not convert, and the product itself was a weak buy. [L0655.020]
- Facebook ad case: a Facebook ad for a simple household product reached about 2.3 million views and drew strong comments and buyer interest. The creator saved the video and planned to test it both as a TikTok ad and as an organic post. The video was about 16 seconds long, used background music and a call to action, and showed the product solving a small everyday problem. The product's European AliExpress listing had over 5,000 orders, and another listing of the same product showed about 213 orders. [L0706.033]
- Low-converter case: in one reviewed case, a product video reached about 10 million views and produced around 3 sales, while another account with about 12 million views produced 10 orders. Ratios of only a few sales per million views were the signal that the product was a weak converter, despite the huge view counts. [L1385.010]

### Store funnel after traffic spikes

- Store performance example for one week of a viral TikTok campaign: about 1,220 sessions, 57 add-to-cart events and 47 checkouts, with zero sales. Viral views (22 million in 36 hours on one video) did not produce purchases because the traffic was not relevant. Compare these funnel numbers against the expected conversion rate before concluding the product is the issue. [L1077.038]
- Organic traffic is less targeted than paid traffic. Viewers of a viral video may visit out of curiosity. Track the funnel from sessions to add-to-cart to checkout to sales, and compare those rates to paid benchmarks when judging a product. [L1077.039]
- In Shopify's product admin, the Insights panel stays empty until the product has had recent sales. It cannot judge a newly embedded product video. Use the LyveCom story numbers (views, click-through rate, add to cart, checkout revenue) to judge video performance in the meantime. [L0959.016]
- LyveCom's analytics suite reports revenue attribution, engagement rate for all video content, and the effect of video on conversion, average order value and session time. Use it to see which content has the highest impact on the store. [L0951.011]

### Organic content, SEO and content ROI

- Monitor traffic sources and performance:
  1. Use Google Analytics to track organic, referral, social and direct traffic.
  2. Set goals for traffic composition (target: 80% organic).
  3. Review monthly which keywords and pages drive conversions.
  4. Identify underperforming content and improve or remove it.
  5. Test changes incrementally and measure impact. [L0226.040]
- Content ROI takes time. Blog articles and SEO improvements do not generate immediate traffic. Plan for 2-6 months before seeing significant organic traffic increases. Long-term payoff is free, recurring traffic from Google, unlike paid ads. [L0226.041]
- Compare the cost of content with paid traffic. A blog post of about 500 USD can cost as much as a Google Ads budget that is spent within a month or even a single day, whereas a well-ranked post can drive traffic for years. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0379.018]
- Direct sales are hard to attribute to a single article, so measure content by the number of email signups it produces. Compare signups per article to decide which topics to repeat. [L0382.040]
- In Google Analytics, create a goal for visits to the newsletter confirmation page. Use it to see which pages send the most visitors to that page. [L0382.041]
- Monthly content review:
  1. List all articles.
  2. Sort them by email signups.
  3. Use the top performers to choose keywords in the keyword tools and create similar articles. [L0382.042]
- Use the Google Search Console and Google Analytics data together to audit SEO. Set measurable goals first, then pick the actions that match how competitive those goals are. (Older advice, 2021.) [L0962.113]
- For SEO opportunity analysis, use a Search Console window of about 30 to 95 days, with 90 days as a practical default. Windows of 7 to 28 days are too slow or too small to be reliable. Periods much longer than 90 days blend performance across a year and hide recent changes. [L1269.015]
- Search Console > Performance shows by default the past three months of organic impressions and clicks. A new store will not show meaningful data for the first weeks. (Undated, Source A.) [L1271.010]
- Google Analytics must be installed on the store to track organic traffic behaviour, such as bounce rate and time on page. On Shopify it is very easy to install. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L1272.001]

### Link-building tracking

- Outreach for link building produces a growing volume of data over time. Record each contact, date, subject and result from the start, and keep it retrievable for future campaigns. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0422.001]
- Store outreach tracking files both online in Google Drive and as a physical copy on an external or portable hard drive, because data kept in only one place can be lost. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0422.002]
- Repeat outreach to the same contact is acceptable over time, but only with enough spacing between messages. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0422.006]
- Four Google Drive template sheets are used for outreach tracking (older advice, 2021-22) [L0422.008]:
  - Master Database
  - Outreach Batch folder
  - No Email Database
  - Success Database
- Keep a very large master spreadsheet stored online in Google Drive, but edit it offline in Excel. Big online spreadsheets in Google Drive process slowly, while Excel handles very large files quickly. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0422.010]
- Ahrefs crawls showed that many links are uncrawled or blocked by robots.txt, so read crawl totals with that in mind. The Ahrefs data explorer can export URL lists with columns such as Page Rating, traffic and HTTP code. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L1372.022]

#### Further details

- Organic sales are hard to attribute to a single video. Use split tests with separate stores or accounts to learn which angles and accounts make the most money per million views. [L0352.035]

## Profit tracking systems: per-order sheets, daily P&L trackers and profit apps

### Why profit tracking is required

- Always track profit with either a Shopify app or a spreadsheet. Without profit visibility you cannot scale effectively, and you risk going negative without knowing it. [L0037.010]
- Automate profit tracking once the store runs, so margin is measured per order rather than estimated from totals. [L0532.023]
- Spreadsheets are hard to keep accurate for profit tracking because every payment and cost source must be connected. Profit apps automate this by pulling data from payment providers. [L1213.024]
- Track profit for each individual order, not only total revenue, so that losing products, pricing problems and winning products become visible. A simple spreadsheet is enough while order volume is low, for example a few orders per day. [L1202.001]
- Track the net profit of every sale in a profit spreadsheet, so decisions about which products to scale or cut are based on real per-sale results. [L0970.149]
- A clear view of gross spend and net return is essential once sales start. Without it, a seller cannot tell whether a product is making money or losing it. This matters most when a product is being tested and when a store moves into the scaling phase. [L0678.006]

### Choosing a tracking system

- Older (2021-22): Review the current financial tracking system and check whether it gives a clear view of the business. If it does not work or leaves room for improvement, compare it with a proven tracking system and implement that one if it fits the business. [L0441.012]
- Older (2021): Google Sheets is enough for margin tracking at the start. Accounting software such as Xero can come later. [L0962.111]
- Older (2020): The December 2020 coaching call lists Wholesale Cost Tracking as a resource for tracking supplier (wholesale) costs. The lesson names it without describing its setup. [L0426.007]
- 2023: A profit tracking spreadsheet is enough for a new store's bookkeeping. It costs nothing and tracks revenue, cost of goods, shipping, ad spend and profit. [L0855.033]
- 2023: Use a profit tracking spreadsheet to track business profit. Xero is the accounting platform used for the business. Google Sheets was used for years before that and supported over $10 million in sales. [L0835.121]
- 2024: Use Excel for product-level profit tracking to see which products to scale or drop. The team later moved accounting to Xero, which is not day-to-day work. [L0971.067]
- Shopify's own analytics shows all store reports and activity. When Shopify Payments is used, payments and finances are also visible in the same admin. [L0934.082]
- Profit apps are the alternative to spreadsheets; see the profit apps section below. [L0037.010, L1213.024]

### Per-order profit sheets (Google Sheets)

- Module 7.9 (Profit tracking) calculates profit margin on every individual order, not just blended profit across all sales combined. Apply it retroactively to all January and February orders. [L0208.015]
- Older (2022): Module 7 includes a downloadable Profit Tracking Worksheet, used to track ROI indicators, monitor profitability by product and campaign, and make data-driven scaling decisions. [L0249.080]
- Older (2020): Track sales price, COGS, shipping cost and profit per order in separate columns, and calculate profit margin order by order. [L0268.039]
- Older (2020), procedure:
  1. Track profit per order in Google Sheets with columns: order date, sale price, COGS, shipping, total profit. [L0268.041]
  2. As order volume increases, assign this tracking to a virtual assistant. [L0268.041]
  3. Periodically review overall business profit. Take distributions based on monthly or weekly profit, not on a per-order basis. [L0268.041]
- Older (2022), procedure:
  1. Build a sheet with one row per order. [L1281.095]
  2. Record the sale price, product cost, merchant fees, shipping and ad cost. [L1281.095]
  3. Compare profit per order to see which products make real money and which need a higher price or removal. [L1281.095]

#### Setting up the order sheet

- Setup procedure:
  1. Open the template link and choose File > Make a Copy. Keep the default name "Copy of" and click OK so the file is editable in your own Google Drive. [L1202.002]
  2. Right-click the tab labelled Duplicate this PNL and choose Duplicate to keep an untouched backup of the formulas. [L1202.002]
  3. Right-click the working tab and choose Rename to give it your own name. [L1202.002]
  4. Enter all order data only in the working tab and keep the backup tab as the reset copy. [L1202.002]
- Keep the blank template tab "DUPLICATE THIS - P&L" unchanged and copy it for each new tracking period or store, so the column structure stays the same across all reports. [L0394.006]
- Column structure of the order-level profit sheet, tab "DUPLICATE THIS - P&L", with one example row. [L0394.001]

| Order # | Date | Net Profit | Net Margin | Supplier | Product | QTY | Customer Paid | Promotion Applied | COGS | Shipping | Tax Collected | Merchant Fees |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1200.0 | 36526.0 | 307.7 | 0.3077 | Supplier ABC | Product 12345 | 1.0 | 1000.0 | 10% Off | 500.0 | 100.0 | 63.0 | 29.3 |

- The Date column stores a spreadsheet date serial (36526 = 2000-01-01 in Excel), so format it as a date before reading it. [L0394.001]
- Net profit per order = Customer Paid − COGS − Shipping − Tax Collected − Merchant Fees. Example: 1000 − 500 − 100 − 63 − 29.3 = 307.7. [L0394.002]
- Net margin per order = Net Profit ÷ Customer Paid. Example: 307.7 ÷ 1000 = 0.3077, i.e. 30.77%. [L0394.003]
- Tax collected from the customer must be deducted from order revenue, because it is passed on to the tax authority and not kept as income. Leaving it out overstates net profit and margin on every sale that carries sales tax or VAT. [L0394.004]
- Record every order on its own row with the supplier, product, quantity and any promotion applied. This shows which suppliers, products and discounts actually produce profit, instead of total revenue only. [L0394.005]

#### Entering one order

- Entry procedure:
  1. Replace the example data with the Shopify order number and the date the order was placed. [L1202.003]
  2. Enter the supplier name and the product name. [L1202.003]
  3. Enter Quantity as the number of units the customer bought. [L1202.003]
  4. Enter Customer Paid as the amount shown in the Shopify orders section. [L1202.003]
  5. Enter any promotion used in Promotions Applied. [L1202.003]
  6. Enter COGS from the supplier invoice. [L1202.003]
  7. Enter Shipping and Tax Collected, and leave Merchant Fees to the formula. [L1202.003]
  8. Read Net Profit and Net Margin at the top of the sheet, which update automatically. [L1202.003]
- Customer Paid must be the amount the customer actually paid as shown in Shopify, not the product list price. If the customer paid for shipping, that amount belongs in Customer Paid, not in the shipping field. [L1202.004]
- Promotions Applied is only a reference column for spotting trends, such as a 10% off storewide sale. It does not change any calculation. Record each discount so you can later see which promotions were linked to higher or lower profit. [L1202.005]
- To add more orders: highlight the full row with all order data and formulas, then drag the fill handle at the bottom down to copy the row. The copied rows keep the calculation formulas, so you only overwrite the input cells for each new order. [L1202.012]
- Ad spend is not entered in the per-order sheet because it varies from order to order. Track it separately and analyse it with return on ad spend (ROAS) alongside the net margin figures. [L1202.019]
- Older (2021), period-level formulas:
  - Profit per order = sale price − Shopify card processing fee (2.9% for Shopify Payments credit card transactions in the source year 2021) − supplier cost − shipping cost. [L0962.109]
  - Profit after ads and overheads = total profit per period − Google Ads, Facebook Ads and Bing Ads costs − 29 dollars per month for Shopify − other fees. [L0962.109]
- Older (2021), margin tracking in a spreadsheet:
  1. Create a sheet for each week or month. [L0962.110]
  2. For every sale, enter the sale price, Shopify fees, supplier product price and shipping cost. [L0962.110]
  3. Calculate profit per order. [L0962.110]
  4. Add that period's Google Ads, Facebook Ads, Bing Ads, 29 dollars Shopify fee and other fees. [L0962.110]
  5. Calculate the margin for the period. [L0962.110]

#### Acting on per-order results

- Example: After two months with 12 sales, six sales earned $400 net profit each, three earned $200, and three earned $50. The $50 sales should be investigated for too much ad spend or higher shipping cost than expected. Then ad spend can be cut or increased on the products that earn most. [L0970.148]

### Order-level tracker from Source B (older, 2021-22)

- Tool: The Order Profit Tracker is a spreadsheet that calculates net profit and net margin for each order from the sale amount minus supplier cost, shipping, payment processor fees and other costs. It is used to know profit per order, and its instructions include a way to automate the data entry. [L1246.003]
- Each order is entered with the order amount, any discount, the payment processor fee, sales tax, the supplier total, the cost of products, shipping, other process fees and the amount received. Net profit and net margin are calculated from these entries. [L1246.019]
- The tracker reports a gross profit margin alongside the net margin. Rely on the net margin for decisions, because it shows what remains after shipping costs and all other order costs are subtracted. [L1246.018]
- During the early build phase, calculate the profit of every single order, because margins only become clear once real supplier costs, shipping, processor fees and fraud fees are included. [L1201.001]
- Enter and check the profit of at least the first 5 to 10 orders manually yourself before handing the task to someone else. [L1201.002]
- Logging procedure for one order in the Order Profit Tracker:
  1. When the store sends a new-sale notification, note the order. [L1201.004]
  2. Place the order with the supplier and wait for the invoice or order confirmation that lists all costs. [L1201.004]
  3. Open the Profit Per Order tab and add a new row with the order number and date. [L1201.004]
  4. Enter the customer name, the product and the quantity. Quantity is for reference only and does not change the calculation. [L1201.004]
  5. Enter the order amount: the total you actually received from the customer. [L1201.004]
  6. Enter the discount you gave. It is recorded for reference; the order amount already reflects what was received. [L1201.004]
  7. Select the payment method so it matches what Shopify fills in automatically. [L1201.004]
  8. Enter the sales tax. Leave it at 0 if no sales tax was charged. [L1201.004]
  9. Enter the supplier total: the product cost plus the shipping cost from the supplier invoice, plus the cost of any shipping you paid yourself through a freight forwarder or UPS on your own account. [L1201.004]
  10. Keep the shipping cost in its own separate cell as a reference note. [L1201.004]
  11. Read the automatically calculated values: processor fees, fraud insurance, total cost, gross profit, net profit and net margin. [L1201.004]
- For an order split across several suppliers, enter the supplier total as one sum of all invoices, including each separately paid shipping cost, using a typed equation such as `=1500+500` (or `=1400+100+500` if a 100 shipping cost is paid separately). Example check: 3,000 − 2,000 − 87.30 − 30 = 882.70. [L1201.014]
- Fraud insurance is entered in the insurance section: Signifyd at 1% of the order amount, with a checkbox column "Insure Checks/Financed Orders?". If you do not use fraud insurance, delete that fee. [L1201.017]
- Older (2021-22), dropshipping COGS: Recording COGS on an accrual basis for each individual order is effectively impossible for dropshipping. It requires accurate supplier invoices for every order, invoices change over time, and suppliers do not standardize their data. The work is very large, the benefit is small, and the return on investment is negative. [L0484.009]
- Older (2021-22): Using an order profit tracker to calculate profit on every individual order is not recommended for dropshipping. For the same reason as per-order COGS accrual, supplier invoices change and are hard to keep accurate, so the effort is large while the information gained is small. [L0484.022]
- Older (2021-22), growth phase: Recording every single order has diminishing returns, because the profit per additional sale of the same item barely changes while the time to collect invoices and costs is large. Weigh tracking time against what it returns. [L1246.004]
- Older (2021-22), growth-phase procedure:
  1. If you do not yet have a clear profit per product or order, use the tracker for a while without entering every order. [L1246.005]
  2. Spot check periodically to confirm that a product line, brand or best-selling product is still profitable. [L1246.005]
  3. When adding a new supplier or new product, track it for a while until you know how it performs. [L1246.005]
  4. Return to the tracker after supplier price changes or your own price changes to see how things have developed. [L1246.005]
  5. If you are still not confident about profit per order, use the tracker for a week at a time instead of continuously. [L1246.005]

### Fees and rates to enter

Sources give different fee rates. Each value applies to the case described.

| Source | Rate given | When it applies |
|---|---|---|
| Source B, older (2021-22) | Shopify Payments 2.9% + $0.30; PayPal 2.9% + $0.30; Klarna 3.5% + $1.00 | Setup tab of the order tracker, with the payment method matched to Shopify [L1201.017] |
| Source A, older (2021) | 2.9% Shopify card processing fee on Shopify Payments credit card transactions | Profit per order in the 2021 sheet [L0962.109] |
| Source C | Payment providers typically about 2-3% per transaction | Estimate of what a payout is reduced by [L678.002] |
| Source C | Extra 1% fee when Shopify Payments is not used | Profitability tracker sheet [L875.021] |
| Source C | Shopify transaction fee of 0.5%, 1% or 2%, depending on the plan | Extra charges entered in a profit app [L1213.026] |
| Source D 2026 | Shopify Fee % preset to 0.055 (5.5%); the same tracker lists 5.50% | Daily P&L tracker input [L1123.004, L1030.007] |

- Payment fees come from the Shopify Fee % setting. Their effect depends on the sheet version (see the following statements). [L1123.014]
- Source D 2026, worked day-1 example: profit equals revenue minus COGS minus ad spend ($140), so no fee was deducted there. Verify the Fees formula in your copy before relying on profit. [L1123.014]
- Source D 2026, if the Fees column applies 0.055 to the worked day-1 example: fees would be $300 × 0.055 = $16.50, profit would fall from $140 to $123.50, and net margin would fall from 46.67% to about 41.17%. Check whether your copy deducts this fee before trusting the $140 result. [L1123.024]
- Source D 2026: Before using the tracker, change its payment processor fee formula to match the fee rates of your own payment processor. [L1124.018]
- Source C: The Shopify 1% fee column header in a weekly sheet does not match its sample value. The sample 113.35 equals 0.5% of revenue 22670.38, not 1%. Check the actual fee rate before relying on the column. A 1% fee on that revenue would be 226.70 USD. [L900.019]
- Source C: Shopify income is recorded net of transaction costs, so transaction costs are not a separate income line. Shopify Fees are tracked as a separate expense line in the monthly sheet. [L550.011]
- Source B, older: Changing a fee rate in the Setup tab updates all order rows in real time. [L1201.019]
- Source B, older: The source gives a switch from Shopify Payments to Klarna changing the processor fee from 29.30 to 106.00 on a 3,000 order. Under the same source's rates, a 3,000 order on Shopify Payments gives 87.30 (2.9% of 3,000 + $0.30) and a 3,000 order on Klarna gives 106.00 (3.5% + $1.00). The 29.30 figure matches a 1,000 order at Shopify Payments rates, so verify it before reusing it. The choice of payment method changes net profit either way. [L1201.019, L1201.017, L1201.014]
- Source B, older: Exact processor fees matter only for the automation features. If you do not want to track the fee precisely, leave the payment method set to Shopify Payments, since most processors charge roughly 2.9% plus 30 cents. [L1201.018]
- Source C: Use Shopify Payments if it is available in your country, as it is the default payment processor. Stripe is the alternative where Shopify Payments is not available. In some markets Stripe has been integrated into Shopify Payments. Availability depends on the seller's country. [L678.003]
- Source C: Track the payout from Shopify Payments or Stripe, not the gross sales figure, because the payout already has transaction fees deducted. A 40 dollar sale might yield roughly 2-3 dollars less. [L678.002]

### Daily P&L and funnel trackers

#### Source D daily P&L tracker

- The tracker is a Google Sheets file with a Daily P&L Tracker tab. Open the shared link, use File > Make a copy, and enter all data only in your own copy. [L1123.001]
- Sheet layout, in order: [L1123.008]

| Block | Columns in order | Input or auto |
|---|---|---|
| Header | Source D ELITE title; Daily P&L Tracker tab; Breakeven ROAS (editable); Shopify Fee % (0.055 preset) | Breakeven ROAS input; fee preset |
| Facebook Ads (daily) | Day, Spend, CPC, CTR, CPM, ATC, IC, Purchases, ROAS | Spend, CPC, CTR, CPM, ATC, IC, Purchases input; ROAS auto |
| Shopify Daily Profit (daily) | Day, Revenue, - COGS, - Ad Spend, - Fees, PROFIT, Net Margin %, ATC, IC, Purchases | Revenue, COGS, Ad Spend, ATC, IC, Purchases input; PROFIT and Net Margin % auto; Fees per fee note |
| Cost Metrics (daily) | Cost/ATC, Cost/IC, Cost/Purchase | auto |
| LAST 3 DAYS | Spend, Revenue, Profit, ROAS, Net Margin | auto |
| TOTAL row (above the daily rows) | Spend, Revenue, COGS, Ad Spend, Fees, Profit across all 30 days; cost metrics show totals or dashes | auto |
| AVG row | Averages of Cost/ATC, Cost/IC, Cost/Purchase | auto |

- Daily rows run from 1.0 to 30.0, one row per day. [L1123.008]
- The daily grid holds 30 rows, one per day, so the sheet covers a 30-day tracking period. [L1123.019]
