- The TOTAL row sums spend, revenue, COGS, ad spend, fees and profit over all 30 day rows. The AVG row averages the cost metrics. The LAST 3 DAYS block summarizes spend, revenue, profit, ROAS and net margin for the most recent three days. [L1123.015]
- Daily entry routine:
  1. Make a copy of the shared template (File > Make a copy) and work only in your copy. [L1123.009]
  2. Set the Breakeven ROAS cell to your break-in RAS target. [L1123.009]
  3. Find the row for the current day (1.0 to 30.0). [L1123.009]
  4. In the Facebook Ads block, enter Spend, CPC, CTR, CPM, ATC, IC and Purchases. [L1123.009]
  5. In the Shopify Daily Profit block, enter Revenue, COGS and Ad Spend. [L1123.009]
  6. In the Shopify Daily Profit block, enter ATC, IC and Purchases. [L1123.009]
  7. Read the auto-calculated ROAS, PROFIT, Net Margin % and the three cost metrics. [L1123.009]
  8. Check the cell color: green means above target, red means negative, zero means breakeven. [L1123.009]
  9. Review the LAST 3 DAYS block for the short-term trend and the TOTAL row for the full period. [L1123.009]
  10. Do not touch any dark purple cell. [L1123.009]

#### Break-in ROAS and status

- Break-in RAS is the minimum daily return target. A day only counts as profitable when its ROAS is above that number. The example target is 1.3, so each ad dollar must bring back more than $1.30 in revenue. [L1123.002]
- Enter your break-in RAS in the Breakeven ROAS cell at the top of the Daily P&L Tracker tab. It is an editable parameter, so replace the example value 1.3 with your own target. [L1123.003]
- A day with ad spend but no revenue shows red, because a ROAS of zero is below the break-in target. [L1123.005]
- Cell colors signal status: green means the day is above the target and winning, red means the day is negative and losing, and zero means exact breakeven. [L1123.006]
- The tracker labels each day with one of three statuses: LOSING (red, negative profit), BREAKEVEN (zero, exact break-even) and WINNING (green, above target). Read the color together with the PROFIT value to see the status. [L1123.021]
- Judge each day by profit and net margin, not by ROAS alone. A ROAS of 3.0 can still leave a thin margin once COGS and fees are deducted. [L1123.017]

#### Cell categories and formulas

- The tracker uses three cell categories: input cells that you fill in each day, AUTO-CALC cells (dark purple) that must not be typed into, and PROFIT/MARGIN cells that are calculated automatically and marked separately. Only the input cells change when you enter daily data. [L1123.022]
- Dark purple cells are auto-calculated. Never type into them. Fill only the input cells, because overwriting formulas corrupts the calculations. [L1123.007]
- Some auto-calculated cells pass a value through with an IF formula of this form, so they stay blank until the source input is filled:

```
=IF(source="","",source)
```

  Do not overwrite these cells, because replacing the formula breaks the link to the input. [L1123.023]
- ROAS (RAS) = Revenue ÷ Ad Spend. Example: revenue $300 with ad spend $100 gives a ROAS of 3.0. [L1123.010]
- PROFIT = Revenue − COGS − Ad Spend − Fees. [L1123.011]
- Net Margin % = PROFIT ÷ Revenue. Example: profit $140 on revenue $300 gives a net margin of 46.67%. [L1123.012]
- Cost per metric = Ad Spend ÷ count of the event. Cost/ATC = Spend ÷ ATC, Cost/IC = Spend ÷ IC, Cost/Purchase = Spend ÷ Purchases. Example with spend $100, 14 ATC, 10 IC and 6 purchases: $7.14, $10.00 and $16.67. [L1123.013]
- Worked day-1 example: spend $100, CPC $1.00, CTR 3%, CPM $60, 14 ATC, 10 IC, 6 purchases, revenue $300, COGS $60, ad spend $100. Results: ROAS 3.0 (green, above the 1.3 target), profit $140, net margin 46.67%, cost per ATC $7.14, cost per IC $10.00, cost per purchase $16.67. [L1123.016]
- Cost per ATC, IC and purchase show where money is lost in the funnel. Compare them day over day: a cost per IC that rises while cost per ATC stays flat points to the checkout step rather than the ad. [L1123.018]

#### Fee-aware daily sheet and the 30-day grid

- Daily P&L tracker in Google Sheets, columns and inputs: [L1030.007]

| Field | Use |
|---|---|
| Day | Daily row |
| CPC, CTR, CPM, ATC | Facebook ad metrics from Ads Manager |
| Cost/ATC, Cost/IC, Cost/Purchase | Cost per add to cart, checkout initiated and purchase |
| COGS | Cost of goods sold |
| Ad Spend | Daily ad cost |
| Fees | Payment and platform fees |
| Net Margin | Profit after all costs |
| ROAS | Return on ad spend |
| Breakeven ROAS | Input, the ROAS needed to cover costs |
| Shopify Fee % | Input, set to 5.50% |

- Input cells are filled in by hand, auto-calculated cells update themselves, and colour signals mark rows as WINNING or LOSING. [L1030.007]
- To add up profit over the last three days with data, use this formula in the tracker. The formula was partly legible on screen, so check the column letters against your own sheet before relying on it. [L1030.008]

```
=IFERROR(SUMPRODUCT((ROW(L9:L38)>=MAX(IF(L9:L38<>"",ROW(L9:L38)))-2)*(L9:L38<>"")*(P9:P38)),"")
```

- A single red day can wipe out several days of accumulated profit. Judge a product on whether its daily profit holds above the threshold across consecutive days, not on the running total. Keeping a weak product also ties up budget that could test another product in the same niche. [L1030.033]
- Source D 2026, daily ad-and-profit sheet, one row per day: [L0560.032]

| Column group | Columns |
|---|---|
| Facebook Ads | Spend, CPC, CTR, CPM, ATC (add to cart) |
| Shopify daily profit | Purchases, ROAS, Revenue after discount, COGS, Ad spend, Fees, Profit, Net margin |
| Summary and signals | Breakeven figure at the top, Color signals, WINNING tab, TOTAL tab |

- Colors flag days that are above or below the breakeven, and the net margin column shows profit as a percentage of revenue. [L0560.032]
- Estimated profit for recent days still in the sheet = revenue after discount × 0.9 × 0.25, which equals 22.5 percent of revenue. The cell label reads "As of X days based on revenue, less 10%, less 25%". Check example: 28,199.17 × 0.9 × 0.25 = 6,344.81. [L0560.033]

#### Source E funnel tracker

- A daily D2C funnel tracker spreadsheet with three sections (unit economics, Meta ads, website) serves two purposes: finding the bottleneck in the whole e-commerce funnel, and acting as a scaling guide. It is filled in every day with the key funnel metrics, with a tab per month and per day so performance is visible over time. [L1354.001]
- To calculate unit economics and target metrics: 1) Enter the store listing price per unit, including shipping cost if applicable. 2) Enter the total cost of goods per unit. The sheet then calculates the product margin and the target metrics automatically. [L1354.003]
- Website section of the daily tracker, columns in order: [L1354.016]

| Column | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Add to carts (ATC) | Count of add-to-cart events |
| Purchases | Count of orders |
| Conversion rate (CVR) | Purchases as a share of visits or sessions |
| AOV (average order value) | Average revenue per order |
| Store revenue | Total revenue for the day |

- Track daily spend and top-of-funnel metrics in a shared Facebook metrics spreadsheet. It acts as a scaling guide that shows whether to scale or to put focus back on the ads or the website. [L1016.061]

### Weekly, monthly and product-level sheets

#### Source C daily and weekly profit sheets

- Track profit in a profitability tracker sheet: record revenue, refunds, COGS, ad spend, processing fees and Shopify fees per day or per week. Weekly tracking gives a clearer view of average profit than single-day numbers. [L0445.020]
- The profitability tracker sheet records revenue, refunds, COGS, ad cost, payment processing fees and Shopify fees per day or per week. Profit equals revenue minus all expenses, and profit percentage is profit divided by revenue. Extra 1% fees apply when Shopify Payments is not used. [L0875.021]
- Daily profit tracking sheet in Google Sheets, one row per day: [L645.023]

| Column | Content |
|---|---|
| Date | Day of trading |
| Revenue | Total sales for the day |
| Facebook ad spend | Amount spent on ads that day |
| Product plus shipping cost | Cost of goods sold including delivery |
| Stripe and PayPal fees | Payment processor costs |
| Other expenses | App fees and additional costs |
| Returns and refunds | Money returned to customers |
| Net profit | Revenue minus all the costs above |
| Profit % | Net profit divided by revenue |

- A team member can fill in the revenue, ad data and costs daily, so you can see what a given ROAS means in profit. [L645.023]

#### Weekly profit and loss sheet

- Weekly profit and loss sheet layout, with the sample row: [L900.016]

| Date | Revenue | Refunds | COGS | Ads | PP Fee (Stripe + Paypal + Klarna) | Shopify 1% Fee | Profit/Loss | %PROFIT | Paypal | Monthly | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| July 22 - July 28 | 22670.38 | 0.0 | 6500.86 | 9765.33 | 846.3542 | 113.3519 | 5444.4839 | 24.01584755 | 235.3842 | (empty) | (empty) |

- Weekly profit = Revenue − Refunds − COGS − Ads − Payment processor fees (Stripe + PayPal + Klarna) − Shopify fee. Profit % = Profit ÷ Revenue. Sample week: 22670.38 − 0 − 6500.86 − 9765.33 − 846.3542 − 113.3519 = 5444.4839 USD, which is 24.02% of revenue. [L900.017]
- Refunds must be subtracted from revenue in the weekly profit calculation, not ignored. A week with zero refunds, as in the sample, overstates profit if returns are expected. Track refunds in the same period as the sales they relate to. [L900.018]
- A PayPal figure of 235.38 sits next to the profit column but is not part of the profit formula. Confirm whether PayPal fees are already included in the payment processor fee column to avoid counting them twice or missing them. [L900.020]
- Example, sample week of July 22 to July 28: revenue was 22670.38 USD, with COGS of 6500.86 USD and ad spend of 9765.33 USD. Payment fees were 846.35 USD and the Shopify fee was 113.35 USD, leaving a profit of 5444.48 USD, or 24.02% of revenue. [L900.024]
- Currency conversion, if the ad account is not USD: enter in the field "1 USD equals (in your currency)" the amount of your currency that equals one US dollar. The default value is 1.0 when the account runs in USD. [L900.022]

#### Simple finance sheet for a dropshipping test

- Procedure, in order: [L678.001]
  1. Enter every expense as it happens, daily if possible, otherwise at least once a week. [L678.001]
  2. Record Facebook ad spend, fulfillment costs (with the platform name in the description) and cost of goods sold per entry. [L678.001]
  3. Record sales not as the gross Shopify sales figure but as the payout amount from Shopify Payments or Stripe, which is already net of transaction fees. [L678.001]
  4. Enter the planned test budget per product (for example 500) in the "planned" field so the summary shows the difference. [L678.001]
  5. Enter the starting budget and read the monthly profit from the summary. [L678.001]
- Monthly profit in a simple store finance sheet = net payouts received (after payment fees) + other income − Facebook ad spend − fulfillment costs − cost of goods sold − other expenses. Each part is entered as its own line so the summary can calculate it. [L678.042]
- Overspending is visible when daily entries exceed the planned budget for a product, so the summary shows the difference immediately. A non-red figure against the planned budget means the plan is on track, and spending should be monitored closely during product tests. [L678.005]
- A personal-finance style spreadsheet can track expenses, income and profit for a store, and the same sheet can also be used for personal income and expenses. Marking unused recurring subscriptions in red as they are entered helps identify ones to cancel. [L678.004]

#### Monthly budget sheet

- Monthly budget sheet layout, one month, values from the example: [L550.001]

| Line | Planned | Is | Difference |
|---|---|---|---|
| Facebook Ads | 500 | 600 | -100 |
| Shopify Fees | 0 | 0 | 0 |
| COGS | 0 | 0 | 0 |
| Other | 0 | 0 | 0 |
| Total expenses | 500 | 600 | -100 |
| Shopify (- Transaction Costs) | 700 | 1037 | 337 |
| Agency Client | 0 | 0 | 0 |
| Total income | 700 | 1037 | 337 |

- Top summary block: Start Budget 1000, Start 1000, End 1437, Monthly Result 437, Change in % 0.437 (format as a percentage, 43.7%). [L550.001]
- Monthly Result = total income Is minus total expenses Is (1037 − 600 = 437). End = Start Budget plus Monthly Result (1000 + 437 = 1437). Change in % = Monthly Result divided by Start Budget (437 / 1000 = 0.437). [L550.002]
- Difference is calculated differently per side. On expense lines it is Planned minus Is (500 − 600 = −100). On income lines it is Is minus Planned (1037 − 700 = 337). In both cases a negative value means the line is behind plan, so overspending on costs and underperforming on income both show as negative. [L550.003]
- Planned Monthly Result is planned income minus planned expenses, which in the example is 700 − 500 = 200. Comparing this planned result with the actual Monthly Result of 437 shows whether the month beat its plan. [L550.005]
- Tracking planned against actual amounts each month, with the Start Budget carried forward as the running balance, shows whether the business is growing its cash or losing it. The Change in % line turns the monthly result into a simple growth rate on the starting budget. [L550.010]
- Each expense category is entered once per line. The Total row sums all expense lines for Planned and Is separately. The same applies to all income lines, so the totals always match the sum of their categories. [L550.004]
- Transaction log sheet layout, two side-by-side blocks, each with the same four columns: [L550.006]

| Expenses: Date | Amount | Description | Category | Income: Date | Amount | Description | Category |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 Jun 2023 (stored as 45078) | 500 | Testing | Facebook Ads | 2 Jun 2023 (stored as 45079) | 800 | Sales | Shopify (- Transaction Costs) |
| 2 Jun 2023 (stored as 45079) | 250 | CJ | COGS | 3 Jun 2023 (stored as 45080) | 237 | SP Payout | Shopify (- Transaction Costs) |
| 3 Jun 2023 (entered as 03.06.0203, wrong year) | 100 | Facebook Ads | (empty) | | | | |

- Category names in the logs must match the category names in the monthly sheet exactly, so that totals pick them up. [L550.006]
- Older (2022), Module 7 lesson "Profit Tracking" worksheet: record monthly sales revenue, business expenses (shipping, Shopify fees, transaction fees, ads, COGS), and calculate net profit. [L0272.028]

#### Product-level margin

- Track each product's margin in a spreadsheet using the wholesale price, MAP and a shipping estimate. Update it when product prices or shipping costs change. [L1115.092]
- Excel is used for product-level profit tracking to see which products to scale or drop (see the choosing section). [L971.067]

### Profit apps

#### B Profit

The sources spell the app as B Profit, B-Profit and BeProfit. The sources do not say whether these are the same product, so the B Profit and BeProfit walkthroughs are kept separate.

- B Profit is a Shopify app that automatically tracks profit, KPIs and profit margins, and shows whether the seller is at loss or profitable. Automatic cost fetching is possible for CJ Dropshipping, AliExpress and Auto DS. It can be used for free with a start plan. [L875.023]
- B-Profit is a Shopify app that automates profit tracking. Install it from the Shopify App Store, connect ad accounts such as Facebook Ads, and enter the cost per item, handling cost and shipping price. It also picks up Shopify and external gateway fees, and shows gross profit and costs in one dashboard. A free plan (start free) is available. [L445.021]
- The B-Profit dashboard shows gross profit, all costs and profit margin, so you can see whether you are at a loss or profitable and whether to scale right now. [L445.023]
- B-Profit setup for dropshipping: choose niche, select channel (e.g. Facebook Ads), set inventory type to dropshipping, pick supplier (CJ Dropshipping, AliExpress, AutoDS or "Other"), then enter product cost per item, handling cost (0 in the example) and shipping prices. Finally connect external payment gateways such as Stripe or PayPal. [L445.022]
- Setting up B Profit in Shopify for profit tracking: [L875.022]
  1. Open the Shopify app store via the recommended apps section and install B Profit. [L875.022]
  2. Create an account and open the B Profit dashboard. [L875.022]
  3. Select the niche (for example electronics) and the sales channels, for example Facebook Ads, and connect the ad account. [L875.022]
  4. Add the inventory source: choose the dropshipping provider (CJ Dropshipping, AliExpress or Auto DS can be connected automatically to fetch cost prices), or select Other for manual entry. [L875.022]
  5. Select the fulfillment provider, or choose Other. [L875.022]
  6. Enter the shipping prices and product cost per item (example: cost per item $5, handling cost $0). [L875.022]
  7. Click Next and connect any external payment gateway such as Stripe or PayPal. [L875.022]
  8. Shopify plan fees are fetched automatically. Click Finish and start with the free plan to see gross profit and costs on the dashboard. [L875.022]
- Cost: B Profit costs about $7,200 per month and is aimed at large, established stores rather than beginners. [L1213.025] The free start plan described above is what the sources give for beginners. [L875.023, L445.021]
- Profit app setup as a procedure: [L1213.026]
  1. Connect Shopify Payments, Stripe and/or PayPal to the profit app. [L1213.026]
  2. Enter the transaction fees and any other margin fees. [L1213.026]
  3. For each product and each variant, enter the cost of goods sold (COGS), shipping cost and any extra charges. [L1213.026]
  4. Add extra charges such as Shopify's transaction fee, which the speaker noted as 0.5%, 1% or 2% depending on the plan. [L1213.026]
  5. Let the app calculate profit and loss for every order. After one to two days you will have a reliable picture of your current numbers. [L1213.026]

#### BeProfit (first walkthrough)

- BeProfit setup as taught: [L477.030]
  1. Install app, create or log in. [L477.030]
  2. Enter store information (e.g. niche, home and garden). [L477.030]
  3. Choose ad platforms (Facebook) and marketing tools (Klaviyo). Connect Facebook and Instagram ad accounts and Klaviyo. [L477.030]
  4. Inventory: choose "I dropship my products", select supplier and drop-shipping management tool (AutoDS). [L477.030]
  5. Shipping provider: select if used (e.g. USPS) or "I don't use any shipping platform". [L477.030]
  6. Production costs: choose "individually update" and enter the total drop-shipping price for every variant. [L477.030]
  7. Connect payment gateways (Shopify Payments, Stripe, PayPal) so fees are calculated automatically. [L477.030]
  8. List expenses (e.g. Shopify plan $29, apps, VA, fixed costs) with status and payment frequency. [L477.030]
  9. Set calculation preferences (e.g. exclude unfulfilled orders, optional). [L477.030]
  10. Choose plan (start with free). [L477.030]

#### BeProfit (second walkthrough)

- BeProfit setup as the second source teaches it: [L1297.036]
  1. Click Add app in the Shopify App Store and log in or create a BeProfit account, then click Let's go. [L1297.036]
  2. Enter your store information, such as niche (here home and garden). [L1297.036]
  3. Select your ad platform (Facebook) and marketing tool (Klaviyo), then connect the Facebook and Instagram accounts and Klaviyo. Pause any ad blocker on the site first. [L1297.036]
  4. Choose how you handle inventory: dropship. Select the supplier and the drop shipping management tool (AutoDS). [L1297.036]
  5. Select the shipping provider (USPS in the example), or "I don't use any shipping platform". [L1297.036]
  6. Choose the option that everything is handled by your supplier, then click Next. [L1297.036]
  7. For production costs, choose Individually update and enter the total dropship cost for each variant, for example 69.95. [L1297.036]
  8. Connect the payment gateways (Shopify Payments, Stripe or PayPal) so fees are calculated automatically. [L1297.036]
  9. List all expenses, such as the Shopify plan ($29 per month), other paid apps, a VA, or fixed company costs, with status and payment frequency. [L1297.036]
  10. Set the calculation preferences. The option to exclude unfulfilled orders is available, but the example left all options off. [L1297.036]
  11. Choose a plan. The example started with the free plan and upgrades later. [L1297.036]

#### BeProfit reports, cost of goods and examples

- BeProfit tracks profit and loss by combining revenue, product costs, payment fees and expenses. The Financials section gives the profit and loss report. Settings > cost of goods lets you update costs when supplier prices change. [L0477.031]
- In BeProfit, the Financials section shows the profit and loss report, and Settings > cost of goods is where you change product costs. If variants are missing, fill in their costs there so profit is calculated correctly. [L1297.037]
- Entering the wrong cost of goods in BeProfit distorts profit figures. Enter the actual total dropshipping price for every variant you sell, as quoted by the supplier. [L477.032]
- Example: after entering only the $29 Shopify plan as an expense and no orders yet, the BeProfit dashboard showed a loss of about $27. Costs are counted from the first day, so a store with zero sales shows a loss equal to its fixed expenses. A negative profit before the first sale is therefore expected when only fixed costs are entered. [L477.035, L1297.038]

#### TrueProfit and Triple Whale

- TrueProfit is an app for live profit tracking. It is cleaner than Triple Whale for straight profit tracking. [L1299.027]
- Triple Whale appears only in that comparison. No unit in this material describes its features. [L1299.027]

#### Why apps and what they miss

- Profit apps typically capture about 80 to 90% of order costs automatically from connected payment accounts. The remaining share, such as extra returns, disputes, chargebacks and payment-provider charges, must be added manually to get a true margin. [L1213.081]
- Spreadsheets are hard to keep accurate because every payment and cost source must be connected. Profit apps automate this by pulling data from payment providers. [L1213.024]

### Shopify reports and billing

- Shopify Settings > Billing lists every paid app subscription, what each costs, and when each bill is due. Check it regularly to track recurring app costs. [L1301.013]
- Paid apps that are no longer used keep billing every cycle. Review the Billing page and uninstall or cancel any app that is no longer needed. [L1301.014]
- Shopify analytics shows all store reports and activity. With Shopify Payments, payments and finances are also visible in the same admin. [L934.082]

### Fixed costs and expenses

- Shopify monthly subscription costs and app subscriptions are fixed costs and are not included in the per-order profit sheet. The sheet measures the actual profit per sale, so fixed costs must be reviewed separately. [L1202.018]
- List every recurring cost as an expense with its status and payment frequency, for example the Shopify plan, other paid apps, a VA and fixed company costs. [L477.030, L1297.036]
- The monthly budget sheet holds fixed costs on their own lines, so the monthly result reflects them. [L550.001, L550.002]
- A personal-finance style spreadsheet can also show which recurring subscriptions are unused, marked in red as they are entered, so they can be cancelled. [L678.004]

### Review cadence

- Track profit daily at first, then switch to weekly reviews to see the average weekly profit. Weekly data smooths out daily noise and gives a steadier basis for scaling decisions. [L875.024]
- Weekly tracking gives a clearer view of average profit than single-day numbers. [L445.020]
- Compare cost per IC and cost per ATC day over day to locate the funnel step where money is lost. [L1123.018]
- Judge a product on whether its daily profit holds above the threshold across consecutive days, not on the running total. [L1030.033]
- Periodically review overall business profit. Take distributions based on monthly or weekly profit, not on a per-order basis. [L268.041]
- Spot check periodically to confirm that a product line, brand or best-selling product is still profitable. [L1246.005]
- Return to the tracker after supplier price changes or your own price changes to see how things have developed. [L1246.005]

### Sheet-building rules

- Enter amounts as plain numbers and keep the currency in the header or a note. [L550.007]
- Check every date after entry, and make sure the cell is formatted as a date rather than text. [L550.008]
- Every expense and income row needs a category that matches the sheet. [L550.009]
- Customer Paid must be the amount the customer actually paid, as shown in Shopify. [L1202.004]
- Keep the shipping cost in its own separate cell as a reference note. [L1201.004]
- Keep the shipping column for what you paid out only. Customer-paid shipping belongs in Customer Paid. [L1202.015]
- The Date column stores a spreadsheet date serial, so format it as a date before reading it. [L0394.001]
- Use one row per order, with the supplier, product, quantity and promotion. [L0394.005]
- Keep the blank template tab unchanged and copy it for each new period or store. [L0394.006]
- Keep a duplicate backup tab with the formulas, and do all entry in the working tab. [L1202.002]
- Only enter data in the input cells. Dark purple (auto-calculated) cells must not be typed into. [L1123.007, L1123.022]
- Keep the IF pass-through formulas (`=IF(source="","",source)`) intact so the cells stay linked to their inputs. [L1123.023]
- Fill only the white input cells in the Source B order tracker. [L1201.020]
- Use the fill handle to copy a full formula row for each new order. [L1202.012]
- Enter a fee in the Setup tab for each payment method. Each processor name must match the payment method shown in Shopify. [L1201.017]
- Keep the order-level tracker separate from fixed costs; add fixed costs to the expense list or budget sheet. [L1202.018, L477.030]
- Enter the planned test budget per product in the planned field so the summary shows the difference. [L678.001]
- Keep the sheet's expense list and total in sync: the daily cost is derived from the entered total. [L900.021]
- The 30-row daily grid covers one 30-day period. [L1123.019]

### Template mistakes

- Typing a fixed number over a calculated cell, such as gross profit, contribution margin or break-even ROAS, breaks the formula chain and makes every later figure wrong. Change the input cells instead and let the calculator recalculate. [L0433.019]
- Typing values into the colored, automatically calculated cells breaks all calculations in the tracker. Enter data only in the white cells. [L1201.020]
- Entering the product's list price in Customer Paid instead of the amount the customer actually paid. Discounts and paid shipping then distort the profit result and make the per-order numbers unreliable. [L1202.014]
- Entering shipping that the customer paid in the shipping column. The shipping column should hold only what you paid out, so customer-paid shipping belongs in Customer Paid. [L1202.015]
- Leaving Tax Collected empty or treating collected sales tax as revenue. It must be entered and deducted, otherwise net profit is overstated. [L1202.016]
- Working in the original template tab without keeping a duplicate backup. If the formulas or automations break, a clean duplicate is needed to restore the calculation. [L1202.017]
- Amounts entered with a currency symbol, such as "$250", are stored as text and are not counted in the totals. [L550.007]
- A date typed with a wrong year, such as 0203 instead of 2023, places the entry outside the month and breaks any date-based filtering. [L550.008]
- A log row without a category, like the 100 Facebook Ads entry without a category, will not appear in the category totals of the monthly sheet. [L550.009]
- The Shopify 1% Fee column header does not match the sample value, which equals 0.5% of revenue. Check the fee rate before relying on the column. [L900.019]
- A PayPal figure sits next to the profit column but is not part of the profit formula. Confirm whether PayPal fees are already in the payment processor fee column. [L900.020]
- The total cost of running the business is entered as 400 USD, but the listed monthly expenses add up to 606 USD (605 without the designer value of 1). The expense list and the total must be kept in sync. [L900.021]
- Entering the wrong cost of goods in BeProfit distorts profit figures. [L477.032]
- Older (2021-22): Using an order profit tracker to calculate profit on every single order is not recommended for dropshipping, because supplier invoices change and are hard to keep accurate. [L484.022]

### When per-order tracking stops paying off

Sources disagree on per-order tracking. Each position applies to a different stage and case.

- Per-order tracking applies while the store is still learning which products make money. A simple spreadsheet is enough while volume is low, for example a few orders per day. [L1202.001]
- Per-order tracking applies during the early build phase. Calculate the profit of every single order, because margins only become clear once real supplier costs, shipping, processor fees and fraud fees are included. Older source (2021-22). [L1201.001]
- Once the store is established, tracking each order matters less. Older source (2021-22). [L1201.001]
- Before handing the task to someone else, enter and check the profit of at least the first 5 to 10 orders yourself. Older source (2021-22). [L1201.002]
- Per-order tracking has diminishing returns in the growth phase, because the profit per additional sale of the same item barely changes while the time to collect invoices and costs is large. Older source (2021-22). [L1246.004]
- Per-order COGS accrual is effectively impossible for dropshipping, and the return on investment is negative. Older source (2021-22). [L484.009]
- Per-order tracking for dropshipping is not recommended, because supplier invoices change. Older source (2021-22). [L484.022]
- When volume grows, assign the per-order tracking to a virtual assistant, and review overall profit periodically. Older source (2020). [L268.041]
- Automate profit tracking once the store runs, so margin is measured per order rather than estimated from totals. [L532.023]
- Per-sale tracking is the rule in the 2023 source: track the net profit of every sale in a spreadsheet. [L970.149]
- Adding a new supplier or product: track it for a while until you know how it performs. Older source (2021-22). [L1246.005]
- If you are still not confident about profit per order, use the tracker for a week at a time instead of continuously. Older source (2021-22). [L1246.005]
- Working rule that reconciles the positions: while product profitability is still unknown or changing, track per order; once products and supplier costs are stable, switch to periodic spot checks and weekly or monthly reviews. [L1202.001, L1246.004, L1246.005, L268.041]

### Daily D2C funnel tracker

The daily D2C funnel tracker has five sections: unit economics, Meta ads, website, and the inputs and calculations behind them. Each row lists what the column means and the benchmark stated for it [L1354.029].

| Section | Column | Meaning | Benchmark stated for this tracker |
|---|---|---|---|
| Unit economics | Store listing price | Input, price per unit | $150 in the example |
| Unit economics | COGS + shipping | Input, cost per unit | $35 in the example |
| Unit economics | Product margin | Calculated | 76.67% in the example |
| Unit economics | Target CPP | Calculated, max cost per sale | $115 in the example |
| Unit economics | Break-even ROAS | Calculated | 1.30 in the example |
| Meta ads | Ad spend | Daily budget | $100 per day in the example |
| Meta ads | CPM | Cost per 1,000 impressions | About $20 healthy; reevaluate near $60 |
| Meta ads | CTR | Click-through rate | Above 1% good |
| Meta ads | CPC | Cost per click | $0 to $3 healthy; about $1 very good |
| Meta ads | CPP | Cost per purchase | Must stay below target CPP |
| Meta ads | ROAS | Revenue / ad spend | At or above break-even ROAS |
| Website | ATC | Add to carts | Count per day |
| Website | Purchases | Sales count | Count per day |
| Website | CVR | Conversion rate | 2% in the example; 0.5% in a weak example |
| Website | AOV | Average order value | Should be good |
| Website | Store revenue | Total daily revenue | Input to ROAS |

Each cell is coloured with one of three labels, Good, Improve or Bad, judged against the benchmark in the last column [L1354.029].

### Profit errors after a BOGO launch

- Triple Whale averages cost per acquisition across all orders [L1404.010].
- When many new customers buy two units under a BOGO, the real average cost per order falls [L1404.010].
- Because of this, the averages shown in the tracker become inaccurate [L1404.010].
- Account for this effect when reading profit data after a BOGO launch [L1404.010].

#### Further details

- B-Profit is a Shopify app that automates profit tracking. Install it from the Shopify App Store, connect ad accounts such as Facebook Ads, and enter the cost per item, handling cost and shipping price. It also picks up Shopify and external gateway fees, and shows gross profit and costs in one dashboard. A free plan (start free) is available. [L0445.021]

- B-Profit setup for dropshipping: choose niche, select channel (e.g. Facebook Ads), set inventory type to dropshipping, pick supplier (CJ Dropshipping, AliExpress, AutoDS or 'Other'), then enter product cost per item, handling cost (0 in the example) and shipping prices. Finally connect external payment gateways such as Stripe or PayPal. [L0445.022]

- The B-Profit dashboard shows gross profit, all costs and profit margin, so you can see if you are at a loss or profitable and whether to scale right now. [L0445.023]

- BeProfit setup as taught: 1) Install app, create or log in. 2) Enter store information (e.g. niche, home and garden). 3) Choose ad platforms (Facebook) and marketing tools (Klaviyo); connect Facebook and Instagram ad accounts and Klaviyo. 4) Inventory: choose I dropship my products, select supplier and drop-shipping management tool (AutoDS). 5) Shipping provider: select if used (e.g. USPS) or I don't use any shipping platform. 6) Production costs: choose individually update and enter the total drop-shipping price for every variant. 7) Connect payment gateways (Shopify Payments, Stripe, PayPal) so fees are calculated automatically. 8) List expenses (e.g. Shopify plan $29, apps, VA, fixed costs) with status and payment frequency. 9) Set calculation preferences (e.g. exclude unfulfilled orders, optional). 10) Choose plan (start with free). [L0477.030]

- Entering the wrong cost of goods in BeProfit distorts profit figures; enter the actual total dropshipping price for every variant you sell, as quoted by the supplier. [L0477.032]

- In BeProfit, after entering only the $29 Shopify plan as an expense and no orders yet, the dashboard showed a loss of about $27. A negative profit before the first sale is therefore expected when only fixed costs are entered. [L0477.035]

- Difference is calculated differently for each side: on expense lines it is Planned minus Is (500 - 600 = -100), and on income lines it is Is minus Planned (1037 - 700 = 337). In both cases a negative value means the line is behind plan, so overspending on costs and underperforming on income both show as negative. [L0550.003]

- Amounts entered with a currency symbol, such as "$250", are stored as text and are not counted in the totals. Enter amounts as plain numbers and keep the currency in the header or a note. [L0550.007]

- A date typed with a wrong year, such as 0203 instead of 2023, places the entry outside the month and breaks any date-based filtering. Check every date after entry, and make sure the cell is formatted as a date rather than text. [L0550.008]

- A log row without a category, like the 100 Facebook Ads entry without a category, will not appear in the category totals of the monthly sheet. Every expense and income row needs a category that matches the sheet. [L0550.009]

- Tracking planned against actual amounts each month, with the Start Budget carried forward as the running balance, shows whether the business is growing its cash or losing it. The Change in % line turns the monthly result into a simple growth rate on the starting budget. [L0550.010]

- Shopify income is recorded net of transaction costs, so transaction costs are not a separate income line. Shopify Fees are tracked as a separate expense line in the monthly sheet. [L0550.011]

Daily profit tracking sheet (Google Sheets), one row per day:
| Column | Content |
|---|---|
| Date | Day of trading |
| Revenue | Total sales for the day |
| Facebook ad spend | Amount spent on ads that day |
| Product plus shipping cost | Cost of goods sold including delivery |
| Stripe and PayPal fees | Payment processor costs |
| Other expenses | App fees and additional costs |
| Returns and refunds | Money returned to customers |
| Net profit | Revenue minus all the costs above |
| Profit % | Net profit divided by revenue |
A team member can fill in the revenue, ad data and costs daily, so you can see what a given ROAS means in profit.
[L0645.023]

- Simple finance tracking sheet for a dropshipping test, in order: 1) Enter every expense as it happens, daily if possible, otherwise at least once a week. 2) Record Facebook ad spend, fulfillment costs (with the platform name in the description) and cost of goods sold per entry. 3) Record sales not as the gross Shopify sales figure but as the payout amount from Shopify Payments or Stripe, which is already net of transaction fees. 4) Enter the planned test budget per product (for example 500) in the 'planned' field so the summary shows the difference. 5) Enter the starting budget and read the monthly profit from the summary. [L0678.001]

- Track the payout from Shopify Payments or Stripe, not the gross sales figure, because the payout already has transaction fees deducted. Payment provider fees are typically around 2-3% per transaction, so a 40 dollar sale might yield roughly 2-3 dollars less. [L0678.002]

- Use Shopify Payments if it is available in your country, as it is the default payment processor; Stripe is an alternative where Shopify Payments is not available, and in some markets Stripe has been integrated into Shopify Payments. Whether Shopify Payments or Stripe is available depends on the seller's country. [L0678.003]

- A personal-finance style spreadsheet can track expenses, income and profit for a store, and the same sheet can also be used for personal income and expenses. Marking unused recurring subscriptions in red as they are entered helps identify ones to cancel. [L0678.004]

- Overspending is visible when the daily entries exceed the planned budget for a product, so the summary shows the difference immediately. A non-red figure against the planned budget means the plan is on track, and spending should be monitored closely during product tests. [L0678.005]

- Monthly profit in a simple store finance sheet = net payouts received (after payment fees) + other income - Facebook ad spend - fulfillment costs - cost of goods sold - other expenses. Each part is entered as its own line so the summary can calculate it. [L0678.042]

- Track real profit per sale in a Google Sheets automation after each sale, using wholesale price plus other costs; the Shopify back end holds only the wholesale price as a starting point. [L0856.095]

Setting up B Profit in Shopify for profit tracking:
1. Open the Shopify app store via the recommended apps section and install B Profit.
2. Create an account and open the B Profit dashboard.
3. Select the niche (for example electronics) and the sales channels, for example Facebook Ads, and connect the ad account.
4. Add the inventory source: choose the dropshipping provider (CJ Dropshipping, AliExpress or Auto DS can be connected automatically to fetch cost prices), or select Other for manual entry.
5. Select the fulfillment provider, or choose Other.
6. Enter the shipping prices and product cost per item (example: cost per item $5, handling cost $0).
7. Click Next and connect any external payment gateway such as Stripe or PayPal.
8. Shopify plan fees are fetched automatically; click Finish and start with the free plan to see gross profit and costs on the dashboard.
[L0875.022]

- B Profit is a Shopify app that automatically tracks profit, KPIs and profit margins, and shows whether the seller is at loss or profitable. Automatic cost fetching is possible for CJ Dropshipping, AliExpress and Auto DS. It can be used for free with a start plan. [L0875.023]

- Track profit daily at first, then switch to weekly reviews to see the average weekly profit. Weekly data smooths out daily noise and gives a steadier basis for scaling decisions. [L0875.024]

Weekly profit and loss sheet layout with the sample row:
| Date | Revenue | Refunds | COGS | Ads | PP Fee (Stripe + Paypal + Klarna) | Shopify 1% Fee | Profit/Loss | %PROFIT | Paypal | Monthly | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| July 22 - July 28 | 22670.38 | 0.0 | 6500.86 | 9765.33 | 846.3542 | 113.3519 | 5444.4839 | 24.01584755 | 235.3842 | (empty) | (empty) |
[L0900.016]

- Weekly profit = Revenue − Refunds − COGS − Ads − Payment processor fees (Stripe + PayPal + Klarna) − Shopify fee. Profit % = Profit ÷ Revenue. Sample week: 22670.38 − 0 − 6500.86 − 9765.33 − 846.3542 − 113.3519 = 5444.4839 USD, which is 24.02% of revenue. [L0900.017]

- Refunds must be subtracted from revenue in the weekly profit calculation, not ignored. A week with zero refunds, as in the sample, overstates profit if returns are expected. Track refunds in the same period as the sales they relate to. [L0900.018]

- The column header says Shopify 1% Fee, but the sample value 113.35 equals 0.5% of revenue 22670.38, not 1%. Check the actual fee rate before relying on the column. A 1% fee on that revenue would be 226.70 USD. [L0900.019]

- A PayPal figure of 235.38 sits next to the profit column but is not part of the profit formula. Confirm whether PayPal fees are already included in the payment processor fee column to avoid counting them twice or missing them. [L0900.020]

- The total cost of running the business is entered as 400 USD, but the listed monthly expenses add up to 606 USD (605 without the designer value of 1). The daily cost is derived from the entered total, so the expense list and the total must be kept in sync. [L0900.021]

- Currency Conversion (if ad account is not USD): enter in the field '1 USD equals (in your currency)' the amount of your currency that equals one US dollar. The default value is 1.0 when the account runs in USD. [L0900.022]

- In the sample week of July 22 to July 28, revenue was 22670.38 USD, with COGS of 6500.86 USD and ad spend of 9765.33 USD. Payment fees were 846.35 USD and the Shopify fee was 113.35 USD, leaving a profit of 5444.48 USD, or 24.02% of revenue. [L0900.024]

## Bookkeeping, P&L, balance sheet and cash flow

### Bookkeeping foundations and when to start

- Bookkeeping means setting up accounting software, importing bank transactions, classifying them and reconciling them. It is complex and time-consuming, so many owners hand it to a monthly bookkeeping service. [L0632.004]
- Set up sound accounting practices from the first sale, either yourself or with an accountant or bookkeeper. Basic bookkeeping is enough at the start to track profit per sale and where costs go. [L0702.025]
- Leaving accounting until sales are high usually results in messy books. Without knowing where money comes from and where it goes, the business is harder to grow. [L0702.026]
- Solid accounting shows the profit on each individual sale and where marketing spend is too high. This clarity supports sound business decisions and reduces financial stress. [L1405.033]
- The general ledger is the foundation of business finances. KPIs, budgets, cash management, financing and loans should only be built on top of it, because without that foundation advanced financial analysis is not reliable. [L1246.011]
- The general ledger does not answer every financial question by itself. It answers the basic ones, and more advanced needs are addressed with additional layers built on it. [L1246.013]
- The general ledger can be maintained by the owner in accounting software or handed to an outside bookkeeping firm. Either way, the point is to have the reports it produces available for decisions. [L1246.022]

### Accounting software and bookkeepers

#### Choosing a bookkeeping approach

- When hiring a bookkeeping service, choose one that specializes in ecommerce operations. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0107.009]
- Professional ecommerce bookkeeping services cost a few hundred dollars per month. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0107.010]
- To handle bookkeeping, choose one of three approaches (older advice, Source B 2021-22): [L0107.011]
  1. Hire a bookkeeping service specializing in ecommerce for complete done-for-you management.
  2. Enroll in a self-paced bookkeeping course with group coaching. Dropship Breakthrough members receive free access.
  3. Recruit and train a virtual assistant from Upwork or Online Jobs using bookkeeping course materials.
- Do not hire cheap or unvetted bookkeepers because you lack expertise to audit their work. Problems remain undetected for months, and fixing poorly maintained books costs far more than hiring professionals initially. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0107.012]
- The monthly P&L, balance sheet and cash flow statement are generated by general ledger bookkeeping software if the owner does the books, or delivered by a bookkeeper if one is hired. The owner should ask the bookkeeper to walk through these reports each month. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0484.020]
- If the owner cannot read the monthly reports, or the reports do not explain where the cash went, the bookkeeping is weak. Replace or improve the bookkeeper rather than relying on unclear numbers. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0484.021]

#### Spreadsheets versus accounting software

- Google Sheets can handle accounting for a small business early on, so switching to Xero is optional at the start. (Source A 2021, older advice.) [L0247.114]
- Xero: bookkeeping software that is not necessary when starting. Google Sheets is sufficient until revenue grows enough to justify a bookkeeper. (Source A 2020, older advice.) [L0268.040]
- Excel or Google Sheets also works for tracking earlier in the business. Zero is accounting software used by some members for multiple businesses. (Source A 2023.) [L0804.101]
- Xero can be used as accounting software once volume grows, but a free profit tracking spreadsheet is enough when starting. (Source A 2023.) [L0855.034]
- Xero is used for accounting and bookkeeping with Shopify integration, typically with the help of an accountant. A simple profit-tracking spreadsheet can replace it while the business is very new. (Source A 2022, older advice.) [L1281.093]
- Google Sheets is suitable for day-to-day bookkeeping and monitoring a small business. Send the records your accountant needs to Xero, which they use to manage the books and taxes. (Source A 2018.) [L1274.054]
- Switch from the spreadsheet template to a real accounting platform such as Xero once the store grows, because the bookkeeping approach changes as the business gets more professional. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0763.003]
- An accounting platform such as Xero or QuickBooks, also called a general ledger, records all transactions and provides a P&L, a balance sheet and a cash flow statement. It gives a more complete and balanced view of the business than a P&L spreadsheet. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L1246.010]

#### QuickBooks, Shopify sync and connectors

- QuickBooks Online is used for accounting. A Shopify-to-QuickBooks sync app (transcribed in the source as Sinder, check the exact name) pushes Shopify transactions into QuickBooks, so each order shows its cost of goods, sales, shipping and profit after setup. (Source A, undated.) [L0337.033]
- QuickBooks Online works for accounting, and a connector such as A2X links Shopify to it. (Source A 2022, older advice.) [L1281.094]

#### Owner-run bookkeeping in practice

- The owner uses the Zero accounting app for bookkeeping but still enters supplier bills by hand and categorizes bank transactions the software does not match automatically. The owner reviews the resulting margins personally, so the bookkeeping is not fully automated. (Source A, undated.) [L1473.037]

### Spreadsheet P&L setup (build phase)

#### Template and structure

- The "Financial statements" template by Xero is available in the Google Drive template gallery and runs in Google Sheets. It is a free basic P&L template for a drop shipping store, with tabs for Instructions, Transactions, Dashboard, Profit & Loss, Categories and Balance Sheet. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0763.002]
- The spreadsheet template is a build-phase tool. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0763.003]
- The Transactions sheet has the columns Date, Description, Amount and Category. Category is a drop-down that must be set for every row, and the Profit & Loss sheet only reads transactions that have a category. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0763.005]
- Track business expenses on dedicated cards so that month-end reconciliation between bank records, card statements and the spreadsheet is quick and reliable. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0903.026]
- Ignore the balance sheet tab during the build phase. It needs manual entries in shaded boxes and does not help with tracking income and expenses. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0763.021]
- Review the P&L month by month, because the monthly layout shows how revenue, cost of goods sold and expenses change from one month to the next. The growth rate projection and Dashboard charts are optional extras. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0763.022]

#### Setup steps

Setting up the P&L template in Google Sheets (older advice, Source B 2021-22): [L0763.004]
1. Open the Transactions sheet and delete the sample transactions.
2. Enter all business transactions (date, description, amount) in the Transactions sheet.
3. Select the matching category for each transaction from the drop-down menu.
4. The Dashboard and Profit & Loss sheets update automatically from the transactions.
5. To rename or change a category, edit it on the Categories sheet.
6. To add a new category, first insert a new row below the last row of that category type on the Categories sheet, type the category name and select the category type from the drop-down. Then go to the Profit & Loss sheet, select the row showing #REF! and insert a new row below it. The new category then appears on the Profit & Loss sheet and in the Transactions sheet drop-down.
7. The balance sheet step (enter values in the shaded boxes) is not needed in the build phase.

In the Google Sheets financial statements template, open the Profit & Loss sheet and select cell F4 to choose the first month of the financial year. The other month columns update from that choice. Revenue, cost of sales and expenses are summarized automatically from the Transactions sheet into a Total YTD column. An optional Growth Rate field then creates a Projected column for a projected P&L. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L1246.020]

On the Profit & Loss sheet, select cell F4 to choose the first month of your financial year, and the month columns update from there. Negative amounts are displayed in parentheses, and the Total YTD column sums the year so far. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0763.008]

#### Sign convention and checks

- Enter money you spend, including cost of goods sold, as a negative amount. Enter money you receive, such as sales, as a positive amount. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0763.006]
- Entering expenses and supplier payments as positive numbers gives impossible results, such as a gross profit larger than sales minus supplier costs and a wrong total profit. Check the signs of all amounts before reading the P&L. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0763.007]
- A small orange warning triangle that appears in the template after an entry signals an input problem. In the lesson's example it flagged amounts entered with the wrong sign. Check that all costs are negative and all incoming money is positive before trusting any profit figure. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0763.025]
- Sign error example in a build-phase drop shipping P&L (older advice, Source B 2021-22): a Shopify payout of 2,000 dollars is recorded as positive revenue, but the 1,200 dollar supplier charge and the 500 dollar Google Ads charge are entered as positive numbers too. The sheet then shows a gross profit of 3,200 dollars and a total profit of 3,700 dollars, which cannot be right for these transactions. After both costs are changed to negative amounts, gross profit correctly becomes 800 dollars and the total profit after the 500 dollar advertising expense is 300 dollars. [L0763.024]
- Starting point: a new drop shipping store with no sheet data yet (older advice, Source B 2021-22). On March 1, a Shopify payout of 2,000 dollars was recorded as a Sales transaction (positive). On March 15, Supplier A charged 1,200 dollars, recorded as a negative Cost of Goods Sold. On March 17, Google Ads charged 500 dollars, recorded as a negative Advertising expense. The result is a Gross Profit of 800 dollars (2,000 minus 1,200) and a total profit of 300 dollars after the 500 dollar ad expense. [L0763.010]

#### Categories

Categories in the template, grouped by P&L section (older, Source B 2021-22): [L0763.017]

| P&L section | Categories |
|---|---|
| Revenue | Custom Sales 1, Custom Sales 2 |
| Cost of sales | Cost of Goods Sold, Custom COGS 1, Custom COGS 2 |
| Expenses | Advertising, Office Supplies, Car/Truck, Insurance, Rent-Car/Equipment, Repairs, Legal Expenses, Contractors, Travel, Meals & Entertainment, Utilities, Rent, Loan Expenses, Taxes and Licenses, Other Expenses, Custom Expense 1, Custom Expense 2, Custom Expense 3 |

- Keep changes to the default categories to a minimum. Categories such as Car/Truck, Rent-Car/Equipment and Repairs rarely apply to a drop shipping store, so rename one of them, or use a Custom Expense slot, only when you need a category that is missing. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0763.018]
- Example: the unused Repairs category was renamed Website & Hosting on the Categories sheet, and a 32 dollar Shopify charge was then entered under it. The new name appears automatically in the Transactions drop-down and the P&L after you insert the row as described in the category setup steps. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0763.019]

#### Projection

- The Profit & Loss sheet has a Growth Rate field next to the Total YTD column. Entering a percentage there, for example 20%, adds a Projected column based on the year-to-date total. In the example, a Total YTD of 3,100 with 20% growth gives a projected 3,720. This projection is optional and not needed during the build phase. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0763.023]
- The projected figure equals the Total YTD value multiplied by (1 + growth rate). For example, 2,000 with a 20% growth rate projects to 2,400. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L1246.021]

### What belongs on a P&L

- A profit and loss statement (P&L, also called an income statement) lists revenue, costs and expenses for a period and ends with the profit or loss. In the build phase, when a store is preparing to launch or has just made first sales, a basic P&L is enough to track money in and out. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0763.001]
- A P&L (profit and loss sheet, also called an income statement) lists income, expenses and the resulting profit after all expenses are subtracted. It is the basic financial overview a store owner needs from the start. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L1148.003]
- The profit and loss statement (income statement) is the first report to review each month and for the full year. It lists total income, cost of goods sold, gross profit, operating expenses, operating income, other income and net income for the period. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L1046.001]
- A P&L shows income, expenses and profit, where profit equals income minus expenses. It shows overall profitability and where money is spent, but it is not sufficient on its own to judge the financial health of a business. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L1246.007]
- Looking only at the P&L gives a skewed view of the business, especially when a business is large enough to be scrutinized by a buyer. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L1246.008]
- The following transactions do not belong on a P&L (older advice, Source B 2021-22): owner draws, owner contributions, transfers between own accounts, credit card payments, inventory purchases, loans and COVID stimulus payments. Recording inventory as an expense can have a serious impact on taxes. [L1246.009]
- The P&L does not show cash flow, owner withdrawals or profit distributions, loans or credit card payoffs, investments into the business, or inventory purchases. Its bottom line will not match the bank account balance. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0484.002]
- In a drop shipping business, income means sales and expenses mean every outgoing payment, such as supplier costs, advertising, hosting and co-working spaces. Keep only income and expenses in the sheet during the build phase and leave out internal transfers, unless they go to a supplier. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0763.020]
- Cost of goods sold is what you pay the supplier for the products, including the shipping they handle for you. Large supplier payments made by ACH or wire transfer are recorded in the same way as card charges, as cost of goods sold. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0763.011]
- Money moved between your own accounts is neither income nor an expense, so do not record it. The exception is a transfer sent to a supplier, which is an expense and goes into the sheet as cost of goods sold. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0763.012]
- The P&L follows bank activity, meaning the date money arrives or leaves, not the date an order was placed. A sale may therefore appear in the following month if the payout or supplier charge comes later. Do not expect the monthly totals in the order profit tracker to match the monthly P&L, because the order profit tracker shows when orders were taken while the P&L shows when money came in or went out. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0763.013, L0763.014, L0763.026]
  - Example: a sale made on February 28 with a Shopify payout arriving about three days later lands in the bank in March, so the P&L shows it under March. Monthly differences like this are normal because the P&L follows bank activity, not order dates. [L0763.026]
- Formula, Total Cost of Sales = Cost of Goods Sold + Custom COGS 1 + Custom COGS 2. Gross Profit (Gross Margin) = Total Sales minus Total Cost of Sales. Total Expenses = sum of all expense categories. Total Profit or Loss = Gross Profit minus Total Expenses. Negative results are shown in parentheses. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0763.009]
- Gross margin on the P&L equals total sales minus total cost of sales. For example, sales of 2,000 with cost of sales of 1,200 give a gross margin of 800. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L1246.021]
- Track each advertising cost to the specific product or department it sells, and require every unit of spend to justify itself within a short cycle. Unattributed budget lines are where money is most often wasted. (Source C, undated.) [L1261.153]

#### Annual P&L structure

- Use a profit and loss sheet that deducts Shopify fees as its own line, because a P&L without platform fees overstates your real profit. Replace older versions of the sheet with the updated one. (Source D 2026.) [L1323.006]
- Using a P&L tracker template (Source D 2026): [L1323.007]
  1. Open the template and choose File > Make a copy so you can edit your own version.
  2. Fill in the light purple input cells with your figures.
  3. Leave the other cells alone, since they calculate automatically.
- Break out all variable expenses on the left side, separate from COGS, so you can quickly model the impact of cutting or changing each line on net profit. P&L design must support decision-making. (Source E 2025-26.) [L0218.024]
- Most operating expenses stay roughly flat from month to month, while product cost, outbound shipping and merchant fees move with sales. Review advertising spend and freelancer costs separately, because they are the largest operating items that can be cut or redirected. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L1046.054]
- Card rewards and other non-operating income should be shown separately from sales and product margin. In the example they added 23,514 and were about 17 percent of the 138,142 net income, so they should not be mistaken for operating performance. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L1046.007]
- Annual profit and loss structure from the example store (2020, US dollars). (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L1046.002]

| Line | Amount |
|---|---|
| Sales | 1,826,410 |
| Sales discounts | (34,197) |
| Sales returns and cancelled orders | (237,813) |
| Total income | 1,554,399 |
| Cost of goods sold (product cost) | 1,151,214 |
| Outbound shipping | 98,131 |
| Merchant account fees | 46,733 |
| Total cost of goods sold | 1,296,078 |
| Gross profit | 258,322 |
| Total operating expenses | 143,694 |
| Operating income | 114,627 |
| Other income (credit card rewards) | 23,514 |
| Net income | 138,142 |

- Build the annual profit and loss by summing the twelve monthly columns, then confirm the total column equals the sum of its months before reading any margin. A wrong total or a missing month will distort gross margin and net margin for the whole year. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L1046.055]
- Advertising spend in the example was about 62,000 per year, roughly 4 percent of total income. Freelancer costs (for example virtual assistants) were about 18,000 per year and should be checked against the value they create. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L1046.015]
- In the example store, product cost of 1,151,214 was about 74 percent of total income of 1,554,399, and outbound shipping of 98,131 was about 6.3 percent. Comparing these two cost lines against total income each month shows which one is driving gross margin down. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L1046.058]

### Reading the P&L

- Reading a P&L from top to bottom (older advice, Source B 2021-22): [L0484.018]
  1. Total income (sales).
  2. Cost of goods sold (COGS).
  3. Gross profit (total income minus COGS).
  4. Operating expenses, listed by category.
  5. Net ordinary income (gross profit minus operating expenses).
  6. Other income and expense, such as interest income, miscellaneous income and credit card rewards.
  7. Net income, the bottom line, which is the profit.
- Monthly net results in a seasonal dropshipping business can swing negative because supplier payments follow earlier orders. Judge profitability by the full year and expect it to rise or at least hold steady year over year. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L1046.008]
- Example seasonal dropshipping store, monthly figures in USD (income / expenses / profit), older advice, Source B 2021-22: [L0484.014]

| Month | Income | Expenses | Profit |
|---|---|---|---|
| January | 25,000 | 35,000 | -10,000 |
| February | 50,000 | 40,000 | 10,000 |
| March | 75,000 | 50,000 | 25,000 |
| April | 150,000 | 75,000 | 75,000 |
| May | 260,000 | 120,000 | 140,000 |
| June | 300,000 | 200,000 | 100,000 |
| July | 290,000 | 240,000 | 50,000 |
| August | 250,000 | 250,000 | 0 |
| September | 50,000 | 100,000 | -50,000 |
| October | 80,000 | 100,000 | -20,000 |
| November | 30,000 | 20,000 | 10,000 |
| December | 60,000 | 50,000 | 10,000 |
| Yearly total | 1,770,000 | 1,400,000 | 370,000 |

### Sales reports and margins

- In Shopify's sales breakdown, total sales equals gross sales minus returns. In the example month, $915,682.28 across all channels minus $47,519.91 in returns gave $868,162.37, and the online store alone showed $763,095.30 minus $42,630.12 = $720,465.18. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0400.016]
- Formulas (older advice, Source B 2021-22): net sales = total sales minus returns (for example, $251,671.35 minus $23,721.80 equals $227,949.55). Net profit percentage = net profit divided by net sales, where net profit is what remains after product, fulfillment, shipping and marketing costs (for example, $50,148.90 divided by $227,949.55 is about 22%). Average order value = net sales divided by orders, and conversion rate = orders divided by sessions. [L0458.042]
- Read the store's sales report in this order: total sales, returns, net sales, orders, sessions and visitors, and profit after all costs. Returns must be deducted before calculating the profit percentage, otherwise profit looks higher than it is. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0458.043]
- Gross margin = gross profit divided by total income. The course example shows 14.14 percent by dividing by gross sales before discounts and returns (258,322 divided by 1,826,410). On the same 258,322 gross profit and 1,554,399 total income, the margin is about 16.6 percent. Pick one denominator and use it consistently when comparing months and years. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L1046.003]
- Net margin = net income divided by total income. The example gives 138,142 divided by 1,554,399, which is about 8.9 percent (about 9 percent). (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L1046.004]

### Accrual and cash accounting

#### Definitions and examples

- Under accrual accounting, an order is recorded as income on the day it is placed, regardless of when payment arrives. Under cash accounting, income and expenses are recorded only when money actually reaches or leaves the bank account. For example, an order placed on March 31 with a Shopify payout three days later would appear in March under accrual but in April under cash. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0484.003]
- Cash-based accounting records income and expenses when money moves in or out of the bank account. Accrual-based accounting records income when it is earned and the matching product cost in the same month, which evens out dips. The broker prefers accrual accounting for online businesses, though both work for dropshipping. (Source A, undated.) [L0554.039]
- Cash-based example: in month 1, 10 units sell at $100 each with a $50 cost per unit paid on 30-day supplier terms, so cash profit is $1,000. In month 2, 3 units sell for $300 and the bill of $500 for the month 1 units is paid, so cash shows a $200 loss. (Source A, undated.) [L0554.040]
- Accrual example: the same 10 units in month 1 show $1,000 income and $500 cost, so profit is $500. In month 2, the 3 units show $300 income and $150 cost, so profit is $150. (Source A, undated.) [L0554.041]
- Cash-based accounting becomes a serious problem for businesses with inventory. For example, buying 1,000 units for $20,000 can show a very large negative month, so accrual accounting makes more sense in that case. (Source A, undated.) [L0554.042]

#### Which method applies when

- For dropshipping, record sales income on the order date but record cost of goods (supplier cost) only when money leaves the bank or credit card to pay the supplier. This cash-basis approach to COGS is the practical standard, as opposed to tracking profit per order. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0484.010]
- Most small dropshipping stores record product cost when the supplier charges the card rather than on the order date. This is a hybrid of accrual and cash bookkeeping. Full accrual bookkeeping for every invoice is usually too time-consuming and expensive at scale. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L1046.009]
- Where the sources differ: the broker recommends accrual for online businesses, while the Source B course treats cash-basis COGS as the practical standard for small stores and hybrid as the norm. Use accrual where inventory is large and lumpy, as in the 1,000-unit example, and the hybrid or cash-basis COGS approach for small dropshipping stores where full accrual is too costly. [L0554.039, L0554.042, L0484.010, L1046.009]

### Balance sheet

- Assets are anything a business owns that can generate economic benefit, such as cash, inventory, investments and real estate. Liabilities are amounts owed to others, such as credit card balances, loans, unpaid wages and taxes owed. A rule of thumb is that assets put money in your pocket and liabilities take money out of it, so a home you live in is not counted as an asset in that sense. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0484.004]
- Buying inventory is not an expense. It is a trade of cash for an equal value of inventory, so the net position does not change at the time of purchase. Inventory can be thought of as cash sitting on a shelf, and it only becomes income through the P&L when it is sold. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0484.005]
- The balance sheet is a snapshot of account balances at one point in time. It lists assets (bank and credit account balances, inventory, and other items not on the P&L) against liabilities (loans, grants, and other amounts owed), with equity making up the rest. It shows balances, not increases or decreases, and its totals must reconcile as assets minus liabilities equals equity. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0484.006]
- Check the balance sheet with Assets = Liabilities + Equity. In the example, liabilities of 149,000 plus equity of (88,165) give 60,835, which matches total assets of 60,834 within rounding. If the two sides differ by more than rounding, a line is missing or entered wrong. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L1046.049]
- Current year earnings in equity shows the net income of the current year to date. Retained earnings holds profit kept from earlier years after owner distributions. Review both together when judging whether the business is building equity over time. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L1046.052]
- Year-end cash in the example was 31,631 (business checking 31,176 and savings 456). Business cash rose over the year by a figure the course puts at about 29,000 to 34,000 after owner draws. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L1046.018]
- A balance sheet and a cash flow statement are two additional key reports that accompany the P&L, and both are critical for understanding the business. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L1246.012]

### Cash flow statement

- The cash flow statement shows every cash movement into and out of bank and credit accounts, covering incomes, expenses, asset purchases and liability payments. It is always recorded on a cash basis and is the link that connects the P&L and balance sheet to the bank account. It is divided into operating activities (customer receipts, supplier and employee payments, interest, taxes), investing activities (equipment sales, inventory purchases) and financing activities (dividends, credit card payoffs or borrowing). (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0484.007]
- Cash flow statement arithmetic: net cash from operating activities + net cash from investing activities + net cash from financing activities = net increase (or decrease) in cash. Ending cash equals beginning cash plus that net increase, and the ending figure must match the bank and credit card balances. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0484.019]
- Net change in cash equals operating cash flow plus investing cash flow plus financing cash flow. Ending cash equals beginning cash plus that net change, so the cash flow statement should close exactly to the bank and clearing balances on the balance sheet. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L1046.050]
- The cash flow net from operations should roughly match profit but will differ because of timing. Sales are recorded when a customer buys, while cash only arrives when the payment processor pays out. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L1046.010]
- Net cash from operating activities = cash receipts from customers minus payments to suppliers and employees plus other operating cash receipts. The example gives 1,557,243 minus 1,421,467 plus 23,515, which equals about 159,290 (the course's operating cash figure). This three-line view summarizes the whole profit and loss for cash purposes. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L1046.011]
- Inventory purchases and similar non-P&L cash payments belong in investing activities, not operating activities. For dropshipping stores, the main investing item is payments in transit, which is cash already earned from payment processors but not yet in the bank. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L1046.012]
- Read the signs in the cash flow statement as direction of cash. Negative financing cash means net debt repayment or owner distributions leaving the business, while positive financing cash means new borrowing or owner money coming in. A negative financing line is not automatically bad when it reflects paying down card debt. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L1046.051]

### Three reports together

- The three monthly financial reports (P&L, balance sheet, cash flow statement) can be used together to cross-check one another. If two of them are available, the third can be derived. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0484.008]
- A P&L can show profit while the bank account does not match it, which is a common problem for growing e-commerce stores. Reconciling the P&L against bank balances is a key check. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L1516.005]

### Monthly reporting routine

- Monthly financial review order (older advice, Source B 2021-22): [L1046.013]
  1. Pull the profit and loss statement for the month and year to date.
  2. Calculate gross margin and net margin with one consistent denominator.
  3. Review the monthly gross profit trend against sales to spot timing effects.
  4. Review the balance sheet: cash, clearing accounts, credit card and loan balances, and whether liabilities are growing faster than assets.
  5. Review the cash flow statement: operating cash, investing (payments in transit and inventory), and financing (card balances, loan payments, owner draws).
  6. Check the large cost lines: outbound shipping, advertising spend, freelancers, interest expense and travel.
  7. Turn the findings into actions, starting with the biggest margin lever.
- Accounting software such as Zero can produce these monthly reports from connected store and bank data. A spreadsheet can imitate the same layout for practice or for quick what-if calculations. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L1046.014]
- A growing store creates predictable financial questions: where the money is going when the P&L shows profit but the bank balance does not match, how much is owed to suppliers, and why the bank balance is unexpectedly high. Knowing the answers to these questions is what bookkeeping is for. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L1516.004]

### Reconciliation and data checks

- Export no more than six months of transaction data at a time to avoid confusion during AI classification. Longer periods require too much context and reduce accuracy. (Source E 2025-26.) [L0218.001]
- Processing 22,000 transactions from a six-month period takes approximately one hour with Claude Cowork on Opus 4.6. (Source E 2025-26.) [L0218.002]
- Export Xero transactions (Source E 2025-26): [L0218.003]
  1. Open Xero and reconcile transactions through your target end date (max six months back).
  2. Select all accounts and add Contact as a column.
  3. Click Update.
  4. Select Excel and export the transaction list.
  5. Save the file for use in Claude Cowork.
- Flag any Xero export data more than 30 days old as potentially stale before drawing financial conclusions. Check export dates immediately upon receipt. (Source E 2025-26.) [L0218.015]
- Treat mismatches as urgent priority: revenue recorded on platforms not matching bank deposits, COGS not matching supplier invoices, or ad spend not matching bank statements. Flag these as potential discrepancies immediately. (Source E 2025-26.) [L0218.016]
- Check that the Shopify tax settings are configured, since the store then shows tax data in its reports. (Source A, undated.) [L0970.151]

### AI-assisted finance reports

- Do not copy-paste transaction data into Claude manually. Drag the exported Xero file into the Claude Code project folder, where the AI reads it directly. This avoids manual entry errors and maintains data integrity. (Source E 2025-26.) [L0218.022]
- Ask the AI CFO system questions directly rather than external tools such as ChatGPT. The Claude instance has full context of your financial structure, transaction history and business decisions, enabling more accurate and specific answers. (Source E 2025-26.) [L0218.021]
- Use Excel instead of Google Sheets for Stage 2 (Claude Code forecasting). Excel has better integration with VS Code, superior context window handling, and formatting precision that Google Sheets cannot yet match. (Source E 2025-26.) [L0218.011]
- In the Claude Cowork interface (the source reads Cord/Claude Cowork), click "Work in a folder" so that all output files are automatically organized in a single project folder, which persists across sessions. (Source E 2025-26.) [L0218.026]
- After the AI builds the P&L, require a summary with the following (Source E 2025-26), then review for accuracy before using it in forecasts: [L0218.007]
  1. Total revenue per month (actual vs forecast).
  2. Total expenses per month.
  3. Net profit per month.
  4. List of rows added that were not in the template.
  5. List of rows removed due to no data.
  6. Any transactions the AI remained uncertain about.
- If Claude Cowork formulas break during the first P&L generation, reinforce formula-building requirements explicitly in the prompt and regenerate. The AI will correct this and ensure every total row contains functional SUM formulas. (Source E 2025-26.) [L0218.027]
- For any bulk financial model change in Claude Code (Source E 2025-26): [L0218.018]
  1. Create a PLAN.md file outlining the proposed changes.
  2. Wait for explicit "Approved" confirmation from the user.
  3. Execute only after approval.

### Budgets, business logs and what-if models

- Monthly fixed expenses listed in the sheet (Source C, undated): [L0900.010]

| Expense | Monthly value |
|---|---|
| app 1 | 14.0 |
| app 2 | 12.0 |
| Shopify | 79.0 |
| VA | 500.0 |
| designer | 1.0 |
| inventory | 0.0 |

- Create a business log tab in your P&L spreadsheet recording every change: date, what changed (for example, a pricing adjustment from $100 to $110), and impact details. Include this log when sharing the spreadsheet with Claude Code, as it provides critical context for spotting trends and analyzing profitability shifts. (Source E 2025-26.) [L0218.014]
- Use the CFO system to test profitability scenarios. Analyze what happens if a major expense (for example, the Gorgias customer service tool subscription) is removed, if specific team members are replaced with lower-cost alternatives (for example, a VA in the Philippines instead of a local employee), or if marketing spend efficiency improves.
