The system recalculates net profit impact instantly. (Source E 2025-26.) [L0218.023]

### Annual financial examples

- Fictional but realistic example (older advice, Source B 2021-22): a US-style summer seasonal outdoor dropshipping store with 2020 total income of about 1.55 million dollars (sales 1,826,410, less discounts of 34,197 and returns and cancellations of 237,813). Cost of goods sold was about 1.3 million, made up of product cost 1,151,214, outbound shipping 98,131 and merchant fees 46,733. Gross profit was 258,322, which the course presents as about 14 percent (see the margin note above for the 16.6 percent figure on total income). Operating expenses were 143,694, including advertising of about 62,000 and freelancers of about 18,000, leaving operating income of 114,627. Card rewards of 23,514 brought net income to 138,142, about 9 percent.
  - Monthly pattern: sales were low in January and February, with a January net loss of almost 10,000. Revenue jumped by about 100,000 in April, reached 110,000 to 115,000 in May and peaked at 287,000 in June. It then fell to about 164,000 in July, rose to about 220,000 in August, dropped to 85,000 to 86,000 in September, recovered to 100,000 to 107,000 in October, fell to 67,000 in November and was about 98,000 in December.
  - In the fall the store paid suppliers for earlier orders faster than new sales came in, producing a gross profit deficit of about 32,000 in one fall month.
  - Year-end balance sheet: cash 31,631, total assets about 60,834, and liabilities of 149,000, made up of credit card A 20,359, PPP loan 3,056, credit card B 84,402 and a long-term bank loan of 41,184. Liabilities were more than twice assets. Operating cash flow was about 159,290.
  - The diagnosis was that profitability, not sales, was the main problem. The planned fixes were higher-margin suppliers, supplier pricing and shipping renegotiation, discount limits, price tests and reduced marketing focus. [L1046.019]

## Cash management, Profit First, taxes and budgeting

### Cash flow in dropshipping

- A customer order is charged to a card, the payment processor pays out, the supplier is ordered and usually paid at shipment, and the supplier is often paid on a credit card that settles about 30 days later. Cash comes in before it goes out, but money must still be set aside to pay suppliers later. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L0484.012]
- Inventory-based dropshipping generally has a positive cash flow pattern. Service businesses that pay their team before receiving payment, and often wait around 30 days for payment, have a negative pattern. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L0484.013]
- COGS is recorded when the supplier is paid, so businesses with long lead times show large profit months when income arrives before supplier payments, followed by lower-profit or loss months. This pattern is normal and does not indicate a problem. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L0484.011]
- Dropshipping cash moves in a wave. In a rising season the store collects customer cash faster than it pays suppliers. When demand falls, it pays suppliers for earlier orders. Supplier charges can lag orders by weeks or months, so cash can look worse than the sales trend. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1046.017]
- In a seasonal store, income often rises before expenses because supplier payments lag sales by several weeks (for example 4 to 6 weeks for made-to-order products). Cash builds early and is spent later in the season even as income falls. Some periods show profit while cash decreases, and one profitable month does not mean cash is available to spend. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L0484.015]
- Treating a strong profit month as available cash leads to overspending and a sudden drop in the bank balance when supplier invoices are paid. A loss month after a profit month can be a normal timing effect, so decisions should rest on reports and cash flow, not on single-month reactions. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L0484.016]
- Save cash from profitable periods to pay suppliers later, because supplier payments may come due after the income has already been received and spent. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L0484.017]
- The smooth seasonal profit and cash curve in the source is idealized. Real dropshipping businesses are far more volatile month to month, so plan reserves for sharp swings rather than the smooth average. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L0484.024]
- A surprise supplier bill means you did not track what you owe, and it can turn an apparent profit into a serious loss. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L0632.007]
- A bank balance higher than expected is not automatically free profit, because unpaid supplier invoices may still be owed from that money. Compare the bank balance against what you owe suppliers before deciding how much you can spend or hire. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L0632.021]
- High-ticket dropshipping is a cash flow business with constant money moving through sales and supplier payments. Keep cash flow positive, because spending customer money on other expenses leads to owing suppliers quickly. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1480.029]
- A growing debt balance is a warning sign even when cash looks healthy, because liabilities that exceed assets leave the business exposed. Track card and loan balances every month and aim to pay them down over time. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1046.020]

### Cash planning and startup funding

- Bookkeeping records what already happened to your money. Cash planning decides in advance what each incoming dollar will do. Bookkeeping alone does not prevent cash shortages, so both are needed. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1200.002]
- Cash flow management means knowing how much you can safely take out of the business without harming it. Without a planned allocation, the owner cannot answer that question from the bank balance alone. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1200.033]
- Cash flow is named as the biggest struggle for entrepreneurs. Forecast it realistically, do not assume early sales will last, and keep reserves so that a slow month does not threaten personal finances. [L0832.003]
- You will need to front cash to buy inventory and get it into your warehouse, 3PL, or Amazon until sales profit can cover future orders. Plan cash for this entire period, which can last a while depending on lead times and origin. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1154.018]
- A common mistake is planning the budget around the first order only, such as the cash currently in the bank, without planning for the next orders. Keep a cash plan that covers the whole cycle until the business generates positive cash flow. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1154.019]
- Early ad spend is usually funded from a credit card. The aim is to turn that spend into sales quickly, but the full daily budget does not have to be paid out of pocket every day. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L0438.010]
- Plan about $1,000 in marketing spend for the first few months, about $30 per day. Sales should cover much of this spend as the store starts earning, so little of your own money is needed. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L0458.053, L0771.046]
- Plan for a first-month budget of about 1,000 dollars for collecting data and generating sales. You can start with less, but you will collect data more slowly. *(Older, 2020.)* [L0551.044]
- Keep the starting budget balanced between marketing and inventory. Too much inventory without demand leaves stock stuck, while too much demand without enough product means orders cannot be fulfilled. [L1438.046]
- Professional videos and photos for a clothing brand can cost about $1,000 per month, a workable starting budget for content that drives early sales. [L1438.047]
- Total investment plus advertising spend since the start was estimated at over a quarter million, up to half a million, with the currency not stated. Plan total funding needs with this scale of spend in mind. [L1438.049]
- A US company can unlock funding options such as credit lines from Shopify Capital, 8fig and Sellers Funding. These can finance inventory or scale ad spend. [L1512.034]

### Inventory forecasting and stock tracking

- Inventory forecasting is non-negotiable. Spend at least one hour per week on it, with half a day with your team as the optimum. It is the founder/CEO's direct responsibility and is not delegated alone to an inventory planner. [L0041.024]
- Inventory forecasting directly prevents bankruptcy. It is the single most important operational task for founders because it drives everything else. [L0041.026]
- Inventory forecast process:
  1. Review what inventory and sales happened in the same period last year.
  2. Create predictions for the coming period.
  3. Write the reasoning behind each prediction.
  4. Adjust forecasts weekly based on actual results.

  [L0041.025]
- Days of stock:

  ```
  Days of stock = units on hand ÷ average units sold per day
  Example: 300 units ÷ 10 units sold per day = 30 days, about one month of stock
  ```

  [L1154.015]
- The 80/20 rule for inventory: 20% of products generate 80% of sales. Update inventory in real time only for the top 20% of top 20%, not for all products. *(Older, 2020.)* [L0268.042]
- Track each store's stock daily per supplier. A rapid drop in monthly gross sales, for example from $50,000 to $20,000, signals out-of-stock problems that need new supplier approvals. *(Older, 2021.)* [L0834.097]

### Tax obligations and deductions

- Financial forecasting is essential because tax authorities will not wait for payment. Schedule and predict sales tax and income tax obligations monthly or quarterly. [L0041.027]
- If you cannot forecast inventory and revenue accurately, you cannot forecast the cash available for tax payments. Government can seize products and inventory for unpaid taxes, which forces business failure. [L0041.028]
- In the US, shipping costs, Shopify fees, transaction fees (2.9%), ad spend and product cost are all tax-deductible business expenses. You pay income tax on revenue minus total expenses. *(Older, 2022.)* [L0272.029]
- Net profit, and what is taxed:

  ```
  Net profit = gross revenue − deductions (COGS, shipping, ad spend, merchant fees, other expenses)
  Example: 1,000,000 gross − about 800,000 deductions = 200,000 net profit
  Only the net profit is taxed.
  ```

  [L1344.005]
- Taxes are not included in the ROAS or ad budget calculation, because they are paid out of profit. [L1115.035]
- In the Profit First allocation, taxes take a set share of real revenue (see Profit First allocations). [L1200.007, L1334.034]

### Payout timing and payment processor reserves

Payout timing differs by source and by year. Use the row that matches your platform and date.

| Platform or source | Payout timing | Status of source |
|---|---|---|
| Shopify Payments | Within 48 hours of a sale | Older (2019) |
| Shopify | Roughly 3 days after the order | Older (2021-22) |
| Shopify and similar platforms (Source C) | Paid out right away, unlike models with payment terms | Undated |
| PayPal, new accounts | May be delayed about 4 to 7 days; exact delay uncertain, verify it | Older (2019) |
| Amazon FBA | Every two weeks by direct deposit or check | Undated |

[L1068.068, L0484.012, L1508.011, L1068.069, L0228.043]

- Amazon pays FBA sellers every two weeks. Use that cycle to fund reorders: pay the 30% supplier deposit immediately after the product launches, then cover the 70% balance when Amazon deposits sales revenue into your account 14 days later. [L0228.043]
- Store sales through platforms like Shopify are paid out right away, so ad spend that returns well can be reinvested quickly. [L1508.011]
- A 10% reserve on payouts ties up roughly $0.80 of profit for every unit sold when profit is about $8 per product. The reserve is calculated on payouts, so it grows with sales volume. [L0351.003]
- Prepaying Google Ads is an optional billing method, not the default. For a new store it is generally not advisable, because the normal billing-threshold setup keeps cash free until spend builds up. *(Older, 2019.)* [L1068.080]

### Profit First allocations

- Setting spending as percentages of real revenue creates a budget limit, because money not allocated to an account should not be spent. This counters the tendency for spending to grow to match whatever cash is available (Parkinson's law). *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1200.004]
- Real revenue is monthly revenue minus cost of goods sold. With COGS at 80% of revenue, real revenue is 20% of revenue, and the profit, owner salary, taxes, staff and operating expense allocations are all percentages of real revenue that must total 100%. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1334.033]
- COGS and real revenue formulas:

  ```
  COGS amount = income in the period × COGS percentage
  Real revenue = income − COGS amount = income × (1 − COGS percentage)
  Example: income of 100,000 per month at 80% COGS gives 80,000 COGS and 20,000 real revenue.
  Example: income of 25,000 per week at 80% COGS gives 20,000 COGS and 5,000 real revenue.
  ```

  *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1200.008]
- The percentages assigned to real revenue accounts must add up to 100%, so every dollar of real revenue has a job. Each account amount equals real revenue multiplied by that account's percentage. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1200.009]
- Example allocation of real revenue. The percentages are examples, not fixed rules. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1200.007]

  | Account | Share of real revenue | Example: real revenue 20,000 per month | Example: real revenue 5,000 per week |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | Profit | 10% | 2,000 | 500 |
  | Owner pay | 20% | 4,000 | 1,000 |
  | Taxes | 10% | 2,000 | 500 |
  | Staff | 30% | 6,000 | 1,500 |
  | Operating expenses | 30% | 6,000 | 1,500 |
  | Total | 100% | 20,000 | 5,000 |

- Profit First example, monthly, COGS at 80% of revenue. The source's own allocation differs from the table above: owner salary is 10% and operating expenses 40% here, against owner pay 20% and operating expenses 30% in the example above. Both total 100%. Use the allocation that a Profit First assessment gives for your business. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1334.034, L1334.036]

  | Line | Share | Example at $20,000 revenue | Example at $40,000 revenue |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | Revenue | 100% | $20,000 | $40,000 |
  | Cost of goods sold | 80% of revenue | $16,000 | $32,000 |
  | Real revenue | 20% of revenue | $4,000 | $8,000 |
  | Profit | 10% of real revenue | $400 | $800 |
  | Owner salary | 10% of real revenue | $400 | $800 |
  | Taxes | 10% of real revenue | $400 | $800 |
  | Staff | 30% of real revenue | $1,200 | $2,400 |
  | Operating expenses | 40% of real revenue | $1,600 | $3,200 |
  | Total allocation | 100% | $4,000 | $8,000 |

- Allocation percentages depend on the business, and no universal set of numbers can be given. Use a Profit First assessment to set them before implementing the plan. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1334.036]
- Weekly example with 45,000 in sales and 80% COGS. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1200.014]

  | Item | Amount |
  |---|---|
  | Sales for the week | 45,000 |
  | COGS (80%) | 36,000 |
  | Real revenue | 9,000 |
  | Profit (10%) | 900 |
  | Owner pay (20%) | 1,800 |
  | Taxes (10%) | 900 |
  | Staff (30%) | 2,700 |
  | Operating expenses (30%) | 2,700 |

- Lowering the COGS percentage from 80% to 75% on 45,000 in weekly sales raises real revenue to 11,250 and lowers the COGS amount to 33,750. Lowering operating expenses from 30% to 20% frees 10% of real revenue to allocate elsewhere, for example to profit. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1200.015]
- Regular allocation routine. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1200.011]
  1. Check how much income has arrived in the income account since the last allocation.
  2. Multiply that income by your COGS percentage and transfer that amount to the COGS account.
  3. The remaining money is real revenue; calculate each other account's share from it.
  4. Transfer the profit share to the profit account, the owner pay share to the owner pay account, the tax share to the taxes account, the staff share to the staff account, and the operating share to the operating expenses account.
  5. Pay suppliers only from the COGS account, staff only from the staff account, and other business costs only from the operating expenses account.
  6. Pay the owner's salary from the owner pay account at every allocation, and leave profit untouched until the quarterly distribution.
  7. Repeat the allocation on a fixed rhythm, either twice a month or weekly, and review the percentages once a quarter.
- Allocations can run twice a month, as the book suggests, or weekly, as the presenter's company does. Choose one fixed rhythm and keep it, because balances are checked and split at that interval. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1200.012]
- For planning, use a monthly rather than weekly or fortnightly calculation, based on the whole month's income. Start from a long-term target such as a lump-sum exit or a revenue and profit goal, then work backwards to a monthly revenue or profit target. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1334.039]
- Review allocation percentages once a quarter, and adjust them when a balance keeps building up unused or when margins change. Surplus from an account that is not being used can move to profit or owner pay. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1200.016]
- Keep allocating in this way even when the business is young. Keep profit low during startup to reinvest in the business, but never take zero profit; owners should always pay themselves some profit. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1334.037]
- Operating expenses in the Profit First plan must include advertising and PPC costs. Make sure the operating expense budget can cover paid ads before increasing spend. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1334.038]
- Using accounting software to separate money without real bank accounts is a mistake. Software only shows numbers and does not hold the money apart, so use separate accounts in addition to bookkeeping. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1200.026]
- Taking unplanned, irregular withdrawals, such as a large payout one month and nothing for several months, creates instability and burnout risk. Set a fixed owner pay percentage instead. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1200.018]
- Staff budget formula and example. *(Older, 2021-22.)*

  ```
  Staff budget = real revenue × staff allocation percentage
  Remaining budget = staff budget − total current staff pay
  Example: 30% of 4,000 real revenue = 1,200 budget; a 1,000 customer service hire leaves 200.
  ```

  [L1334.035]
- Profit First planning steps. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1334.040]
  1. Write down the long-term vision, such as target revenue, target profit, or a lump-sum exit amount.
  2. Work backwards from that target to a monthly revenue or profit goal.
  3. Enter current monthly income and the cost of goods sold percentage to calculate real revenue.
  4. Set the allocation percentages for profit, owner salary, taxes, staff and operating expenses, with a Profit First assessment, so they total 100% of real revenue.
  5. Calculate the monthly staff budget from the staff allocation.
  6. Enter the current team and total staff pay, then read the remaining budget.
  7. Enter the monthly cost of the next planned hire, and make the hire only when the remaining budget covers it.
  8. Enter the maximum revenue capacity the current team can handle and the target capacity the owner is aiming for.
  9. Each month, check remaining budget and capacity, then hire when the budget and team allow it.
  10. When the staff budget keeps building up without need, lower the staff allocation (the lesson used 20%) and move more into profit and owner salary.

### Owner pay, distributions and draws

- Pay yourself a fixed salary, for example 2,000 per month in two installments. Set monthly sales or profit goals that trigger a larger distribution when reached, such as an 8,000 bonus after 10,000 in profit, and keep the remaining profit in the business. *(Older, 2021.)* [L0247.119]
- Owner pay is transferred at every allocation, while profit is distributed to the owner only once a quarter. Keeping profit in its account until the quarterly payout builds reserves before any profit is taken. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1200.013]
- Early-stage profit distributions (2020 source): take distributions immediately after generating net profit instead of waiting for a set accounting period. Example: after several weeks of sales generate $5,000 net profit, take $3,000 as a distribution and reinvest $2,000 into growth. This is the older approach; the 2021-22 Profit First method above holds profit until the quarterly payout. *(Older, 2020.)* [L0268.085]
- Owner withdrawals and profit distributions do not reduce the profit shown on the P&L. They appear on the balance sheet through equity and in the cash flow statement under financing, so an owner can take money out and still see a profit for that month. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L0484.023]
- Owner contributions increase equity, while owner draws reduce equity and show up as financing outflows in the cash flow statement. Keep these separate from operating expenses so that the profit and loss still shows true business performance. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1046.053]
- Pay yourself first from the business. Know what your household costs per year, including children's education, food and clothing, and take that amount out as income. Reinvest the rest of the profit into the business, because taking out all profit leads to heavy taxes and slower growth. *(Older, 2019.)* [L0960.055]
- Reinvestment amount:

  ```
  Reinvestment amount = annual business profit − annual personal living costs
  Example: 300,000 profit − 100,000 yearly living costs = about 200,000 reinvested
  ```

  *(Older, 2019.)* [L0960.056]

### Business money versus personal money

- Couples or partners running a store together should set a separate budget for the business and keep savings for emergencies and family needs out of it. Plan the money before starting, not after the first sales come in. [L0832.001]
- Partners should openly discuss how much financial risk each is willing to take before spending money, because tolerance for risk often differs. Agree on spending limits for each risk level. [L0832.012]
- Track profit and expenses in dedicated business accounts so that the store's performance can be measured cleanly over time. *(Older, 2019.)* [L1069.072]
- Do not run a skeleton-budget business that spends every dollar and leaves one dollar after taxes. Lawsuits require cash reserves for legal defense and settlement, so maintain financial buffers. [L0041.046]
- Create a budget and live within your means. Saving what you keep matters more than the amount you earn. Limit small recurring and impulse purchases, such as takeout, daily coffee or replacing equipment that still works, to build savings. [L1343.030]
- For small accounts in a mail business, the difference between profit and loss depends more on how promptly payments are collected than on any other single factor. Set a clear timeline for following up on overdue accounts. [L1369.093]

### Reinvestment and budgeting

- Money is a tool in business, not an emotional object. Spend it when it reliably generates more money. For example, if each dollar of ad spend returns 1.50 dollars, spending more on that channel is a way to reach more customers and grow sales. Limit spending only when returns do not justify it. [L1513.013]
- Some offers pay back immediately, while others take months to recover costs. Know the payback period before spending, fund a campaign according to how long the payback takes, and only take on the cash risk you can carry. [L1261.021]
- With Brex, about 10,000 USD of Facebook ad spend returned roughly 100 to 300 USD in rewards. The reported rate varies with the card and the type of spending. [L1512.025]
- Set a minimum monthly savings amount (3,000 USD per month in the example, with better months saving up to 8,000 USD) and direct it into income-producing assets before discretionary spending. [L1515.038]

### Personal investing and wealth building

**Context and order of priorities**

- US figures cited in the lesson: the average American has under $1,000 net worth, and about 85% of people have under $10,000 in net worth even when many earn $100,000 or more. Treat these as rough US-based context, not current statistics. *(Source undated.)* [L1126.002]
- Order for turning income into wealth:
  1. Cut monthly expenses as far as possible.
  2. Calculate your monthly living expenses and multiply by six.
  3. Keep that amount as an emergency fund in a liquid, no-fee online savings account.
  4. Once the emergency fund is full, send a fixed amount every month to investments instead of the savings account.

  [L1126.006]
- Keep an emergency fund of six months of living costs in liquid savings before investing. Without it, a forced sale of investments during a downturn or early withdrawal penalties can wipe out gains. [L1126.007]
- Store income is semi-passive because automation still needs oversight, while invested capital can grow without monitoring. Investment income is therefore the most truly passive income stream. [L1515.039]

**Compounding and entry methods**

- Compound growth of $1,000 invested every month at an assumed 8% annual return, compounded monthly (lesson calculator output, rounded). The growth comes from compounding, and each year's contribution is added on top of prior growth. Results change with the actual return, so re-run the numbers with a current calculator. [L1126.003]

  | Years | Total contributed | Approximate total value | Approximate interest earned |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | 10 | $120,000 | $186,000 | $65,000 |
  | 20 | $240,000 | $600,000 | about $360,000 |
  | 30 | $360,000 | $1,500,000 | about $1,140,000 |

- Dollar-cost averaging: instead of investing a lump sum at once, invest a fixed amount on a regular schedule to reduce the risk of entering at a bad price. Lesson example: $100,000 invested as $10,000 per month over 10 months. [L1126.014]
- Check the arithmetic of any plan that spreads a lump sum over months. $5,000 per month for six months only covers $30,000, so a $60,000 sum at $5,000 per month takes 12 months. Work out the number of months from the total and the monthly amount before committing. [L1126.036]
- Modern online platforms let small monthly amounts (200 USD or 1,000 USD in the examples) enter assets that once required large sums, such as apartment buildings shared with many co-investors. A large starting capital is no longer required. [L1515.041]
- Real estate investing in income buildings no longer requires large capital. The lesson states that monthly contributions of $200 to $1,000 can be enough to start. The average expected return cited for such investments is 9-10% per year. [L0827.006]

**Index funds, fees and not timing the market**

- Do not try to time the market. Holding large amounts in cash while waiting for a dip can cost more than the dip would have saved, since the lesson describes missing a year with about a 22% market gain by staying in cash. [L1126.015]
- A low-cost index fund holds a small share of the whole publicly traded stock market. Vanguard is cited as one provider, and the strategy is to hold it long term rather than trade it. [L1126.016]
- Over long periods, most highly paid financial advisors, mutual fund managers and hedge fund managers do not beat the total stock market, and fees reduce returns further. Low-fee index investing is therefore the default option the lesson recommends. [L1126.017]
- Low-cost index funds that buy the whole stock market (Vanguard was named) avoid the 2-4% annual fees some financial advisors charge, and fees reduce long-term returns. [L1515.040]
- Selling during a downturn turns a paper loss into a real loss. If you own 100 shares of an index fund and the market falls 50%, you have lost nothing until you sell, so hold unless you need the money. [L1126.019]
- Investment platforms named as examples include the robo-advisor Betterment and the real estate investment platform Fundrise. Check fees, minimum deposits and regulation in your own country before using any platform. [L1126.043]

**Expected returns (sources differ)**

- Long-term expected return on diversified investments such as stocks, real estate or funds is cited as about 10% per year, while the compound interest example assumes 8%. Use a conservative rate when planning. [L1126.020]
- Real estate income buildings: 9-10% per year (see Compounding and entry methods). [L0827.006]
- PeerStreet, a property-backed loan platform, reported rates of about 7% to 8%. Loans carry default risk, and the lesson's claim of never losing money should not be treated as a guarantee. [L1126.021]
- Pure Street, secured hard-money loans: average return given as 7-9% per year, while some banks paid around 2% at the time. *(Source undated; bank rate is a historical comparison.)* [L1515.044]

**Risk limits and speculation**

- Limit speculative investments such as Bitcoin, cryptocurrency and individual stocks to 0% to 5% of total net worth. Never put more into speculation than that, and avoid anything risky you cannot afford to lose. [L1126.018]
- Speculative assets such as cryptocurrencies and newer platforms can lose the entire amount invested, and some were lost entirely when the platforms failed. Limit these holdings to an amount you can afford to lose completely. [L0863.012]

**Property and lending**

- Turnkey rental properties are often the most expensive house on the block, which raises the risk of losing value and tenants in a downturn. A larger apartment building of 40 or more units with a full-time property manager spreads that risk, and the lesson assumes about 95% occupancy even if a few tenants leave. [L1126.022]
- Property-backed loan platforms such as PeerStreet let investors lend against real estate. If a borrower defaults, the lender can take the collateral property and sell it to recover the money. Returns of about 7% to 8% were reported, but this is not guaranteed and depends on the loan and the quality of the collateral. [L1126.044]
- Unsecured peer-to-peer microloans can lose the full amount if the borrower stops paying, while loans secured by property let the lender recover funds by selling the property. Spreading a loan across many investors (about 1,000 USD each) further reduces risk. [L1515.042]
- Pure Street is a platform where many investors each fund a small slice of a secured hard-money loan backed by property. If the borrower defaults, the platform handles eviction and court steps and sends monthly progress updates. [L1515.043]

## Health scorecards and benchmarks: the four revenue buckets, MER and report cards

### Pulling the inputs from Shopify and the ad accounts

Use the Shopify reports exactly as listed here, because the report card depends on them.

**New and returning customer data**

1. Go to Analytics > Reports. [L0222.004]
2. Click "New vs Returning Customers over Time". [L0222.004]
3. Set period to "Last 90 Days" and "No Comparison". [L0222.004]
4. Remove the "Total Sales" metric and remove all dimensions (no by-day breakdown). [L0222.004]
5. Add these metrics: Orders, Gross Sales, Discounts, Total Shipping Charges, Taxes, Sales Reversals (these are returns), Net Items Sold. [L0222.004]
6. Remove visualization. [L0222.004]
7. Apply and copy the data. [L0222.004]
8. In the report card, paste using "Paste Values Only". [L0222.004]
- In Shopify reports, "Sales Reversals" is the metric name for customer returns. [L0222.005]

**Conversion rate, sessions and visitors**

1. Go to Analytics > Conversion Rate Breakdown. [L0222.012]
2. Set period to "Last 90 Days" and "No Comparison". [L0222.012]
3. Remove dimensions. [L0222.012]
4. Add metrics: Conversion Rate, Sessions, Online Store Visitors. [L0222.012]
5. Copy the data. [L0222.012]
6. Paste into the report card using "Paste Values Only". [L0222.012]
- Example values for this extraction: conversion rate 0.81%, sessions over 90 days 1,005,049, online store visitors 705,255. [L0222.013]

**Ad spend and fulfillment**

- Ad spend from TikTok, Snapchat, Pinterest and other platforms goes in the "Other Spend" field. [L0222.011]
- Combine all channels, such as Facebook, Google and other spend, into one total before it is used for MER and the marketing bucket. [L1235.013]
- Fulfillment cost is a formula field and must not be entered by hand. The system calculates it from your fulfillment data. [L0222.021]

**Input checks**

- If homepage data (revenue, ad spend and operating expenses) is wrong, every downstream tab gives wrong results. Verify all inputs before moving to other sections. [L0222.020]
- Take all revenue, refund, tax and cost inputs from the calculator homepage and check them for accuracy. If the results do not match the P&L, check the inputs before drawing any conclusion. [L1235.003]
- Use the last 90 days of data and include both new and returning customers, so the report shows total business performance and not one customer group. [L1235.004]
- Example COGS for a 90-day period: $216,980. [L0222.007]
- Example return rate: 2.1%. [L0222.008]

### Report card setup and workflow

**Opening the template**

1. Click the link to the AI Report Card Google Sheet. [L0222.001]
2. Click "Make a Copy" or go to File > Make a Copy to save a personal version. [L0222.001]
3. Never edit the original template. [L0222.001]
4. Only fill cells showing blue text (manual inputs). [L0222.001]
5. Never modify black text cells, because these contain formulas that calculate automatically. [L0222.001]

**Reading the colours and time windows**

- Blue font marks manually entered cells. Black font marks formula cells. Change only the blue cells. [L0222.002]
- The report card uses a 90-day lookback window for all metrics except operating expenses, which is calculated on a 30-day basis and then multiplied by three. [L0222.003]
- The Fix column records whether each metric must increase or decrease to reach target, and it sets the next priorities. [L1235.022]
- The report card is a Google Sheets template. It combines the calculator inputs, the four revenue buckets, the benchmarks and the growth metrics. [L1235.022]
- The benchmark figures and the contribution margin in the report card come from the new customer contribution margin calculator. Its homepage numbers feed the bucket values, so that calculator must be filled in before the report card is read. [L1235.024]

**90 Day Company Snapshot layout**

Values come from the calculator homepage and the last 90 days of store data. [L1235.023]

| Block | Fields | Source |
|---|---|---|
| New Customer (NC) | Total Gross Sales NC; Returns; Tax Collected; NC Performance Sales; # of orders; Average Order Value; Tax Obligation/Collected; Tax %; Net Items Sold; COGS Amount; Returns % | L1235.023 |
| Returning Customer (RC) | Total Gross Sales RC; Returns; Tax Collected; RC Performance Sales; Shopify COGS Amount; RC Returns % | L1235.023 |
| Fulfillment | Cost to Fulfill Orders | L1235.023 |
| Ad Spend | Facebook Spend; Google Spend; Other Spend; Total Spend | L1235.023 |
| Site Metrics | Visitors; Sessions; Conversion Rate | L1235.023 |
| Team | Position; Salary (one row per person, e.g. owner, ops manager, customer service) | L1235.023 |
| Software / Subscriptions | Name; Area; Bill (e.g. Klaviyo, Shopify, Gorgias) | L1235.023 |
| Totals | MONTH TOTAL; 90 DAY TOTAL | L1235.023 |

**Working through the report card**

1. Fill the calculator homepage with the last 90 days of data for new and returning customers, and check the figures against the P&L. [L1235.025]
2. Fill the 90 Day Company Snapshot with customer blocks, fulfillment cost, ad spend by channel, site metrics, team salaries and software bills. [L1235.025]
3. Read the four financial buckets (profit, COGS, marketing and advertising, OPEX) and confirm they add up to 100% of total revenue. [L1235.025, L1235.001]
4. Note each bucket's colour against its benchmark. Before cutting any cost, check whether a strong bucket such as profit can offset a red one. [L1235.025, L1235.011]
5. Start at the first red bucket and drill down with the decision tree until the most expensive component is found. [L1235.025, L1235.010]
6. Check the growth benchmarks: MER, contribution margin, ads launched, conversion rate, OPEX, promo events and emails per week. [L1235.025, L1235.014]
7. Enter an increase or decrease in the Fix column for each metric that is off target. [L1235.025, L1235.022]
8. Set priorities from the Fix column, make the changes, and repeat the report card later to measure the effect. [L1235.025]

### Derived metrics

- Performance Sales = Gross Sales − Discounts − Returns + Shipping Charges + Taxes. It is calculated separately for new and returning customers. Tax is included here and is removed in later analysis sections. [L0222.014]
- Total revenue for the report card = gross revenue − discounts − refunds + shipping charges − tax. Sales tax is subtracted because it is a pass-through cost and not real revenue for the business. [L1235.002]
- Average Order Value (report card) = Performance Sales ÷ Number of Orders. [L0222.015]
- Return Rate Percentage = Returns ÷ Total Gross Sales. [L0222.016]
- Other AOV definitions in the sources:
  - AOV = total revenue ÷ number of orders in the same period. Example: 40 USD rising to 70 USD means each order brings 30 USD more on average. [L0435.009]
  - AOV = sum of the sale prices ÷ number of sales. Example: sales of $67, $197 and $247 give an AOV of about $170. [L0748.003]
  - AOV = total revenue ÷ number of orders, compared before and after a change on similar traffic sources. [L0860.033]
  - (Older advice, 2021-2022) AOV = total sales ÷ number of orders. Examples from 2021 monthly reports: infrared sauna store 227,949.55 / 134 ≈ 1,701 USD; gazebo store 738,209.32 / 402 ≈ 1,836 USD; air purifier store 65,170 / 137 ≈ 476 USD. [L0771.068]
- Use the definition that matches the sheet. Performance Sales includes tax, and total revenue excludes it. Each is correct for its own section. [L0222.014, L1235.002]

### The four revenue buckets

**Structure**

- Split total revenue into four buckets: profit, COGS, marketing and advertising, and OPEX. Together they must equal 100% of total revenue. If the four percentages add up to more than 100%, the input data is wrong. [L1235.001]
- OPEX is fixed operating cost such as salaries and software subscriptions. The template lists Shopify, Klaviyo and Gorgias. Marketing and advertising is total ad spend. COGS is the cost of the goods sold. [L1235.005]
- Rapid-growth expenses are a different grouping. OpEx in this view includes courier fees, 3PL warehousing, supplier payments and freight forwarding. Avoid long-term contracts with these vendors, because their unit rates drop sharply as you scale. [L0041.006]
- The two definitions of OpEx conflict. Use the report card definition (fixed costs) when reading the buckets. Use the rapid-growth definition when planning vendor contracts and scaling costs. The sources do not reconcile them further. [L1235.005, L0041.006]
- Traffic is the largest P&L expense in ecommerce, followed by people costs. Automation tools reduce the people category by removing repetitive tasks. [L0286.004]

**Benchmarks**

| Category | Benchmark | Reading | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| Profit | 15% | At or above 15% is good | L1235.006 |
| COGS | 40% | At or below 40% is good; above 40% needs work | L1235.006 |
| Marketing and advertising | 30% | At or below 30% is good | L1235.006 |
| OPEX | 15% | Below 15% is good | L1235.006 |

**Colour and reading**

- Each bucket is colour-coded against its benchmark. A healthy result shows green and an unhealthy result shows red. A red category is where to look first. [L1235.007]
- A bucket below its benchmark frees room. The surplus can go to marketing, OPEX or profit. Example: COGS 7% below benchmark means 7 points of revenue that can fund more ads or more profit. [L1235.008]
- A bucket above benchmark can be offset by the others. Example: COGS about 3 points over 40% while profit is at double its benchmark. The business is still healthy, but the gap should be recovered in marketing or OPEX, and COGS should still be reduced. [L1235.009]
- Do not cut costs just because one bucket is red. First check whether another bucket, such as profit, is strong enough to compensate, then decide where the fix is needed. [L1235.011]

**Decision tree**

1. Look at the four buckets and identify which ones are red. [L1235.010]
2. If OPEX is red, break OPEX down into its fixed cost lines and find the largest one. [L1235.010]
3. If COGS is red, break COGS down into shipping, payment processing fees and 3PL fees. [L1235.010]
4. Identify the most expensive component in that breakdown. [L1235.010]
5. Continue drilling down, since each sub-cost has its own benchmark, until the root cause is clear. [L1235.010]
6. Once the bucket problem is clear, move on to the growth metrics (MER, contribution margin, ads launched, conversion rate, emails, promo events) to find the next fix. [L1235.010]

### New customer contribution margin calculator

**Build procedure**

Build the calculator from the last 90 days of data, in this order:

1. Start with the new customer AOV (last 90 days, new customers only, tax-inclusive, as shown in Facebook). [L0433.001]
2. Subtract sales tax (GST around 10% if collected; higher if the tax is collected at a higher rate) to get the post-tax AOV. [L0433.001]
3. Subtract blended COGS: average product cost across SKUs, packaging and supplies (box, tape, inserts), and freight to the customer. [L0433.001]
4. Subtract payment processing fees (about 2.9% of order value, adjusted for heavy Klarna or PayPal use), plus the fixed transaction fee of $0.30 per order (set to 0 if the card fee is a fixed monthly cost). [L0433.001]
5. Subtract Shopify Markets fees, weighted by the share of revenue from Markets orders, not the full rate. [L0433.001]
6. Subtract 3PL pick/pack fees (about $1.50 to $2.50 per pick; 0 if no 3PL). [L0433.001]
7. Subtract refunds, using the new customer return rate. [L0433.001]
8. The result is gross profit per order, which is the basis for all later targets. [L0433.001]

**Key metrics and benchmarks**

- Gross profit per order (after COGS, fulfillment, fees and refunds) should be at least 60% of AOV. Blended COGS including returns should stay at 40% or below. If shipping alone pushes gross profit under 60%, reduce shipping costs by changing 3PL or renegotiating carrier rates. [L0433.004]
- Customer acquisition cost (CAC) = total ad spend ÷ number of new customers in the period. CAC as a share of new customer AOV should be 30% or below. Example: $400,000 total spend ÷ 6,829 new customers = $58.57 CAC, which is 25.73% of a $227.65 post-tax AOV. [L0433.005]
- First order contribution margin = AOV (post-tax) − blended COGS (including refunds) − customer acquisition cost (ad spend). It is the profit left before fixed operating expenses (OPEX). Benchmark for a first order: 15% to 25% of AOV. Higher targets suit businesses without repeat buyers. Brands with strong repeat customers can accept a lower first-order margin. [L0433.006]
- The report card's growth table sets contribution margin at 35%. This is a different measure from the 15% to 25% first-order benchmark, and the sources do not reconcile them. Use 35% for the whole-business view in the report card. Use 15% to 25% for a first order of new customers. [L1235.014, L0433.006]

**Worked case**

- Post-tax AOV $227.65, gross profit per order $130.27, CAC $58.57 (Facebook $300,000 plus Google $100,000 spend). First-order contribution margin: 31.63% or $71.69, above the 15% to 25% benchmark. If COGS were set to 80% of the order, the contribution margin turns negative, so COGS is the variable to fix first. [L0433.007]

**Diagnosis and scope**

- When a metric misses its benchmark, check the line item one step below or above target and fix that first. Example: if CAC is within 30% but COGS is 80%, the problem is product cost, not ad spend. Diagnose the calculator before touching ad accounts. [L0433.008]
- Build the calculator for the whole business first, then split it by collection, product, drop or month when more detail is needed. Operators should be at or above benchmark before scaling spend, because unprofitable unit economics become harder to fix as volume grows. [L0433.012]
- If the business sells in several countries, build a separate contribution margin calculator for each region. Taxes, payment fees, shipping and Markets fees differ by market. Build the whole-business version first, then split by region when needed. [L0433.020]

### Growth benchmarks and MER

**MER**

- MER = total ad spend ÷ total revenue, expressed as a percentage. Target is 30% or below. Example: 400,000 USD ad spend ÷ 1,835,273 USD total revenue = 21.80%, which is 8 points below target and shows green. [L1235.012]
- The marketing bucket benchmark (30%) and the MER target (30%) use the same logic: ad spend against revenue. [L1235.006, L1235.012]
- Example: at a 35% MER, a brand spends 35% of revenue on marketing. If 80% of orders then carry a gross margin of only 31% under a BOGO offer, the brand loses money. Adjust the MER target before running the offer, or skip the offer if the margin is too tight. [L1404.009]

**ROAS**

- Blended ROAS = total revenue ÷ total ad spend, including all traffic sources (organic, referral, paid), not isolated per-channel data. [L0208.002]
- When multi-channel attribution is fragmented, use total monthly revenue ÷ total ad spend. This ratio shows overall ad efficiency regardless of which platform gets last-click credit. [L0257.017]
- Breakeven ROAS sources differ:
  - A breakeven ROAS of 1.6 or lower leaves enough margin for ad spend, apps, staff and processing fees. At scale, Facebook and TikTok campaigns typically reach a ROAS of 2 to 2.5, and very strong campaigns may go higher. [L0445.014]
  - Typical benchmarks are a breakeven ROAS of about 1.2 to 1.3 and an average Facebook ROAS of about 1.8 to 1.9 for a store with healthy margins. These figures are for one store and must be checked against your own margin. [L0693.049]
- Use 1.6 as a breakeven guide when the cost stack includes apps and staff. Use 1.2 to 1.3 only after checking that the store's margin supports it. [L0445.014, L0693.049]
- Ad spend for one established brand was about 30,000 to 50,000 USD per month, depending on the day and whether the account is scaling. [L1431.057]

**Growth table**

| Metric | Target | Reading | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| MER | 30% or below of total revenue | Above 30% needs investigation | L1235.014 |
| Contribution margin | 35% | Below 35%: check whether COGS or marketing is the cause | L1235.014 |
| Monthly number of ads launched | More than 20 for beginners | Below 20 means too few tests | L1235.014 |
| Website conversion rate | Above 2.5% | Below 2.5% needs work on the site | L1235.014 |
| OPEX | Below 15% | Above 15% needs cost review | L1235.014 |
| Marketing promo events per year | At least 3 | Fewer than 3 needs more sales peaks | L1235.014 |
| Emails sent per week | About 3, minimum 1 to 3 | Below 1 per week is too low | L1235.014 |

**Ads launched**

- Expected monthly ads launched = monthly ad spend ÷ 500 to 1,000 USD. The range reflects the average spend per ad in most accounts, so use it as a rough estimate. [L1235.015]
- Worked example: a store spending 20,000 USD a month on ads, divided by 500 to 1,000 USD per ad, has an expected 20 to 40 ads launched per month. At the lower end, the store is only just at the beginner minimum of more than 20 ads per month. [L1235.026]

**Whole-report example**

- Total revenue 1,835,273 USD, ad spend 400,000 USD. MER 21.80%, below the 30% target and green. OPEX 4.12%, well below 15%. COGS about 3 points above the 40% benchmark, and profit roughly double its benchmark, so the surplus covered the COGS gap. Contribution margin 35%, on target. The priority is to bring COGS down further to raise profit. [L1235.019]

### Customer value: new versus returning, AOV and LTV

**Measuring each customer group**

- Track repeat-customer order value separately from first-order value. Repeat customers typically have an AOV about 20 to 30% higher than first-time buyers, according to the estimate given. [L1251.013]
- Measure customer value per buyer by tracking each customer's order count and cumulative spend over time (1st, 2nd, 3rd order and so on). AOV alone hides whether customers return, which matters most for repeat-purchase consumables. [L0334.058]
- Use the per-customer view to find where customers stop buying, and fix that step. [L0334.058]
- Example from a supplement brand: AOV about $70, the most expensive product $49, and one loyal customer spent $7,436 over 3 years. Repeat purchases matter for consumable, high-margin products. [L0334.024]
- Measure success on orders and revenue together, not revenue alone. A single high-value customer does not show consistency. Look for repeating daily patterns (e.g. 20-30 orders daily). [L0324.034]
- Keep profit margins stable week to week alongside revenue. Scaling without margin stability indicates unsustainable growth. [L0324.035]
- Track refund rate and chargeback rate as percentages of orders, not only as raw counts. A rising percentage is the early warning, because each extra order is more likely to become a refund or chargeback, and the damage grows faster than sales. [L0632.020]

**Lifetime value**

- LTV per year = units bought per year × price per unit. Example: 17 units × $8 = $136 per year. [L0524.003]
- LTV is driven by three levers: AOV, churn and how often customers come back. Improving only one usually leaves the others unchanged, so track them together. [L1232.002]
- LTV is the most powerful lever in paid traffic because it improves the other metrics. The strongest advertisers combine high CTR, high Quality Score, high Relevance Score and cheaper CPCs. [L1031.034]
- Know profit per customer and LTV, because these decide how much you can spend on acquisition. A customer who buys repeatedly can justify a first purchase that loses money. [L0643.041]
- Your AOV and LTV set the maximum you can spend to acquire a customer. Raising either lets you spend more on traffic and grow faster than competitors. [L0378.086]
- Judge loyalty rewards over the customer's lifetime, not on one order. A customer who earns points by referring new buyers can be profitable even when the repeat order has a thin margin, because each referred first order brings full-price revenue. (Older advice, 2021-2022.) [L0956.006]
- Cost per acquisition for cold customers can stay high for established brands and remain sustainable, as long as returning-customer value is strong. Judge acquisition costs against the lifetime value of repeat buyers, not only the first order. [L1251.014]
- In the early bootstrapped phase, the brand needed a 3x to 5x return on each marketing dollar to stay solvent. A 25% returning-customer rate showed that LTV exceeded AOV. [L0790.077]

**Returning-customer rates by niche**

- Subscription model: 61% returning customers. Traditional dropshipping: 2-8%. Subscriptions are 7-30x more effective at retention. [L0324.033]
- Health-related niches are often around 10% to 15% returning, compared with 50% to 60% in fashion. New-customer acquisition therefore matters more in health. [L0871.037]

**Margins and store economics**

- (Older advice, 2021-2022) Dropshipping gross margin has an absolute minimum of about 20%. Below that, the business is very hard to run profitably. A gross margin near 14% is a serious warning sign. [L1046.005]
- (Older advice, 2021-2022) A typical full-year net profit margin after all costs is 15 to 20%. Stores that do not actively work on margins rarely exceed about 20%, so a net margin of about 9% signals a profitability problem. [L1046.006]
- (Older advice, 2021-2022) Net profit margins in the examples ranged from 14 to 22%. In high-ticket dropshipping, margins can reach the 20s and up to 30%. A 14% margin is still meaningful at high sales volume. [L0771.045]
- (Older advice, 2021-2022) Gazebo store, about 3 years old (June example year): 402 orders produced 738,209.32 USD in sales (806,805.49 USD gross minus 68,596.17 USD returns) and 103,201.65 USD net profit at a 14% margin. The business needed one to two hours per day of management. [L0771.050]
- (Advice from 2022, older.) A store doing $15,000 to $20,000 monthly revenue with a few thousand dollars of net profit can be a working outcome. Compare with stores doing hundreds of thousands in monthly revenue and tens of thousands in net profit to see the range. [L0803.073]

### Year-on-year, seasonality and saturation

**Seasonality first**

- Before reacting to a sales drop, check whether it is seasonal by comparing at least two years of data. Most markets have predictable seasonal patterns. (Older advice, 2021-2022.) [L0949.025]
- In 2021, sales fell in July and August across roughly 100 Google Analytics and ads accounts in the US. This pointed to market-wide forces rather than store-level problems. The speaker linked it to broader economic conditions and stock delays. (Older advice, 2021-2022.) [L0949.026]
- Read dips in a monthly creative report against the ad account's seasonality. A drop in January and February can reflect an off-season rather than a problem. Compare each region or account with its own past seasons. [L0885.019]
- A store with consistent prior sales should not expect a drop in February. If sales fall, check the optimization lessons in module six for Meta and Google Ads to find what changed. [L0964.050]
- Launch timing matters little for non-seasonal niches, so any month is a good month to launch. Tax refund season can bring a small sales spike. Supplier closures from Black Friday through Christmas matter more when securing stock. [L0807.049]
- Black Friday example: revenue doubled compared with the previous month, while January and February were the best months for the store because competitors were out of stock. [L1363.053]
- Q1 2025 (January through March) sales were up approximately 10% compared with Q1 2024, indicating healthy market conditions despite economic concerns. [L0255.065]

**Holiday auctions**

- When CPM rises during Black Friday and Cyber Monday, ad costs go up because more competitors bid in the same auctions. Check CPM, CPC and clicks year on year to see which part changed. [L0472.063]
- Use the Google Ads date range selector to compare the same range (e.g. Thanksgiving week through Cyber Monday) across two years when measuring seasonal performance. [L0472.064]
- CPM is the cost per 1,000 impressions, meaning what it costs to reach 1,000 people with an ad. CPC is the cost per click. If CPM and CPC both rise during a holiday period, competitors are often bidding for the same auctions, so compare year on year before changing bids. [L0472.069]

**Growth trend and saturation**

- Compare revenue month over month and use the trend to set the next goal. Example: $30,000 in one month and an average of $35,000 in the following month shows growth of about 17%, which becomes the basis for planning the next quarter. Ask the same question every month so growth opportunities are not missed. [L0463.022]
- Without proper forecasts and inventory tracking, early-stage patchy sales hide whether a trend is fatiguing or just seasonal. Track weekly to spot real trend signals and adjust investment accordingly. [L0041.031]
- Year-on-year tracking, excluding seasonality, reveals market saturation. A 20% year-on-year revenue drop in March, followed by a 20-25% drop two months later, signals that the market is fatiguing and the trend is plateauing. [L0041.030]
- When year-on-year drops repeat at similar levels (e.g. 20-25% down in consecutive periods), the trend is fatiguing. Adjust investment toward product innovation, new markets or channel diversification. [L0041.032]

### Test input for the audit flow

Example input metrics for a fictional premium beauty-tool store, used to test the audit flow:

| Metric | Example value | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Conversion rate | 2.6% | L0886.027 |
| Average order value (AOV) | $185 | L0886.027 |
| Traffic by channel | Paid Social 48%, Organic Search 22%, Direct 15%, Email 10%, Influencer & Affiliate 5% | L0886.027 |
| Returning customer rate | 31% | L0886.027 |
| Add-to-cart rate | 8.5% | L0886.027 |
| Checkout completion rate | 54% | L0886.027 |
| Meta ROAS | 2.7x | L0886.027 |

## Market and competitor estimation

### Start with market size and expectations

- Market size predictions are usually wrong. Some products grow far beyond expectations, going from 5k to 10k daily and beyond, while others plateau despite large potential, so focus on actual inputs and outputs rather than trying to predict a ceiling [L0041.029].
- Know your total spend per year per customer, total market consumption, and the share of the market the product appeals to before committing a large budget. Without these figures, the cost of acquiring a customer cannot be judged [L1261.019].
- Roughly 3 million people in the US have hallux valgus (bunion). Use this as a rough upper bound when judging the market for a bunion-related product, and compare it with the sales a product claims before accepting the claim [L1385.009].
- If an ad's response rate is measured in a town or segment, the cost of waste circulation depends on the share of readers who are real prospects. An offer appealing to one in five readers can have a far lower cost per prospect than an offer appealing to one in a hundred [L1261.020].
- Request volume scales with how broad a product's appeal is. One newspaper run of a widely used canned food drew 1,460,000 sample requests, while a narrower product drew only a fraction of that. Forecast sample demand from the product's breadth of appeal before printing [L1261.146].
- Product research and ad metrics use guidelines, not fixed rules. A benchmark such as 300 orders is a starting filter, and products or ads just below it can still have real potential [L1426.031].
- Use viewer and analytics data to decide what to add to the store. When a segment of viewers is large, such as around 30 percent men, add products for that segment [L1469.030].

### Validate demand with Google Trends and seasonality

- Google Trends interest over time between 25 and 100 is a good range for demand validation of a product or niche. Consistent zero interest on a broad keyword over one to two months signals low demand and is a red flag [L1183.014].
- Google Trends can show a niche's search interest over several years. Look for a dip that repeats in the same period each year, not one-off swings. *(Older advice, Source A 2021.)* [L1279.074]
- Check seasonality before choosing products, because sales can drop when weather changes. Slides sell poorly in cold weather, while sneakers sell all year. A cold-weather season can be framed as clearing summer stock [L1212.028].
- Brand and product name searches show organic demand. Rising brand searches show that customers look for the brand directly. The case had about 8,000 monthly brand searches and about 14,000 for the product name [L0871.036].

### Keyword demand and CPC

**Keyword Planner metrics (Source A)**

- Keyword Planner shows average monthly searches (how many people search the term on Google per month), a competition rank set by Google, and a top-of-page bid range with a low and a high value. The CPC in the tracking sheet is the high-range top-of-page bid, which is the current price advertisers pay per click [L1120.006].
- Track keywords using the high-range CPC and never treat it as a budget. Keywords are researched for content, not bought as ads. The low range can be far below the high range, for example 99 cents against $6.08 for standing desk [L1120.007].
- A high CPC is treated as a signal that the keyword probably converts, which makes it worth creating content for. The lower bid range is not used for decisions because it varies more strongly [L1120.008].
- CPC varies because of match type and ad quality. A long-tail search such as a product name plus a city costs less than a broad term, and better ads that get more clicks and keep visitors longer lower the cost per click. These differences explain why two similar keywords can have very different bids [L1120.009].
- A keyword with much lower search volume can have a higher CPC, which suggests advertisers earn more from it and it likely converts better. Example: stand up desk had 33,100 monthly searches at $7.48 CPC, while standing desk had 201,000 at $6.08 [L1120.010].

**Keyword research worksheet (Source A)**

| Keyword | Related keywords | Monthly searches | Competition | CPC (high range) | Content type |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| standing desk | not recorded in lesson | 201,000 | high | $6.08 | not yet decided |
| stand up desk | not recorded in lesson | 33,100 | high | $7.48 | not yet decided |
| L-shaped standing desk | L-shaped standing desk adjustable height; L-shaped standing desks electric; L-shaped standing desk with storage; best L-shaped standing desk | 12,100 | high | $7.49 | Collection |
| electric standing desk | electric standing desk (related search) | 8,100 | high (niche-wide) | $4.51 | Collection (tag-based) |
| laptop stand for desk | laptop stands for desk adjustable; ergonomic laptop stand for desk | 14,800 | high | $2.16 | Blog post |
| standing desk height calculator | standing desk height calculator | 260 | low | $5.17 | Page |

[L1120.011]

- Once the worksheet is complete, remove extra information that is not needed, so only the tracked fields remain. Give each keyword row a clear planned content type [L1120.012].
- The last example row in the worksheet (about 90 monthly searches, high competition) has a CPC value that is garbled in the transcript and reads as 282. Verify this CPC directly in Keyword Planner before using it in any decision [L1120.037].
- At the price-point stage, the average profit column is calculated for information only and is not yet used to choose a niche. The final niche decision combines it with the other filled-in columns, such as seasonality, demand and average price [L1150.014].
- Average price in Google Shopping research should exclude clearly irrelevant listings, such as auctions or products unrelated to what you would sell. Use judgment for each outlier [L1283.100].

**Keyword CPC from other tools (older advice, 2021 to 2022)**

These values come from other tools and older years, so they are not comparable with the Keyword Planner high-range CPC above. Use them only as a rough early check.

- In the 2021 US example, Ubersuggest showed an average cost per click of 1.01 USD for the keyword 'generator'. Use a keyword's CPC as a rough early check of what paid traffic may cost before you plan a budget. *(Older advice, Source B 2021 to 2022.)* [L0771.070]
- For the informational keyword 'how to cook while camping' in the United States, Ahrefs showed a search volume of about 200, a keyword difficulty of 26 (labelled Medium), a CPC of $1.30, and an estimate of about 30 linking websites needed to rank in the top 10. *(Older advice, Source B 2021 to 2022.)* [L0878.030]
- In a keyword spreadsheet, selecting the volume cells of one category (Content, Collection or Product Page) shows SUM, AVERAGE, MIN, MAX and COUNTA in the status bar. Use these totals to compare how much search volume each page type holds and how many terms it contains. Check them after sorting and after adding new CSV exports. *(Older advice, Source B 2021 to 2022.)* [L0758.026]

### Read the Kalodata product and shop metrics

**Date windows**

| Source | Available windows |
|---|---|
| Shop metrics (shop filter) | Yesterday, Last 7 days, Last 30 days; plus Last 90, Last 180 and Last 365 days [L0333.019] |
| Date filter (general) | Yesterday, Last 7 Days, Last 30 Days, Last 90 days, Last 180 days, Last 365 days; Enterprise data reaches 365 days [L1188.056] |

- The example product check used the 30-day window from 08/14 to 09/12 [L0333.019].
- Enterprise data reaches 365 days, which allows a full-year trend check before testing a product [L1188.056].

**Revenue splits and shop panel**

- Kalodata splits a product's revenue by source, both by content type (Creator, Product Video and Ad, Livestream) and by channel. This shows whether sales come from short videos, ads, live shopping or creators [L0333.021].
- Kalodata's Core Metrics panel for a shop shows revenue for the selected period and revenue per day, along with video revenue, sales per day, live revenue, average unit price and conversion ratio. The shop type, such as Local Shop, is listed in the shop information [L0333.022].
- Kalodata splits product revenue into video, product card and live revenue. A live revenue of 0 USD combined with most revenue coming from video and product card supports a test built on organic video sales without live selling [L0354.037].
- Revenue share by channel equals channel revenue divided by total revenue for the same date window. For example, $31.54k of video revenue out of $33.89k total gives a video share of 93.07%, and the remaining share comes from live and product card revenue [L1188.055].

**Reference product (Kalodata, last 30 days)**

- Main Revenue about 1.52 million, Item Sold about 68,250, Avg. Unit Price about 22.27 dollars and Live Revenue about 155,460 dollars. Use these figures as a reference to judge whether a product sells at scale before testing ads [L0707.026].
- Average per day equals the 30-day total divided by 30. For the reference product, 1.52 million revenue divided by 30 is about 50.7k per day, and 68,250 items sold divided by 30 is about 2.27k items per day. Use this to check a product's daily scale against the Per Day figures before testing ads [L0707.033].
- Revenue per day for a product in Kalodata is the 30-day revenue divided by 30, which gives a quick daily average to compare products. For the liver detox example, 46.09k revenue over 30 days equals about 1.54k per day, matching the tool's display [L1187.025].
- Per-day numbers in Kalodata are the 30-day total divided by 30. For example, $33.89k divided by 30 gives about $1.13k per day, and 28.3 items per day corresponds to roughly 849 items in 30 days [L1188.026].

**Product card checks before a test**

- Check the following on the Kalodata product card before testing a product: price range, commission rate, shipping fee, rating, number of creators, creator conversion ratio, revenue and units sold. These metrics show whether the margin, competition and sales pace are workable for a test [L1187.026].

**Top products, growth and ROAS**

- In Kalodata's last-30-day view for the US (July 5 to August 3, 2025), the top products reached about $849K to $872K in revenue each, and the top video list started around $200K. One video was cited at about 500K in revenue and 11 million views [L1479.026].
- In Kalodata product lists, the Revenue Growth column shows the percentage change in revenue over the selected period. Negative values mean revenue is falling, so filter for positive growth when you look for rising products [L1479.027].
- In Kalodata, Ad ROAS is the return on ad spend for a product's ads (revenue divided by ad spend). Read it together with Ad Spend, because a high ROAS on a small budget may not hold when the budget grows [L1479.029].

### Estimate competitor sales from order numbers

**Test-order method**

Use a test purchase to read a store's order number and estimate its order volume.

1. Place a test order on the competitor's store (cost e.g. 20-30 USD) [L0501.026].
2. Note the order number. Shopify order numbers start at 1,000, so orders placed = order number minus 1,000 [L0501.026].
3. Revenue in that window = (order number minus 1,000) multiplied by the product price. Example: order 1,500 for a 20 USD product gives 500 orders, about 10,000 USD [L0501.026].
4. Place a second test order 24 hours later and subtract the first order number from the second to get orders in the last 24 hours. Multiply by average product price for daily revenue [L0501.026].
5. Use the total to compare with the views of the account. Factors such as older viral videos are not visible in this method [L0501.026].

- Estimate a competitor's order count by placing a test purchase and reading the order number. Subtract the store's starting number, about 1,000 in the example, then use the result as an approximate order count. Multiply that by the average order value to estimate revenue [L0352.057].
- To estimate total orders from a store's order numbers, take the latest order number you see, subtract the lowest number seen earlier as a baseline, then subtract one for your own test order. In the example, 3,493 minus 1,000 minus 1 gave about 2,492 orders in roughly one month [L0655.024].
- Revenue estimates from order-number sequences should use the highest likely unit price (for example $13) as a conservative upper-bound when average order value is unknown [L0655.022].
- Some stores show only a confirmation number at checkout and send the real order number by email later. Wait for that email before deciding a test purchase failed, and check the spam folder if it does not arrive [L0655.025].
- Revenue from a viral product can only be estimated from public signals such as follower counts, view counts and posting frequency. Exact sales cannot be read from the outside [L0656.030].

**Worked cases**

- Infinity Pencil case: a corn-themed video using a trending sound reached about 6.8 million views in two days. The store sold at $13 with a cost of $2 to $3 from AliExpress, had about 10 reviews, and recorded 2,492 orders in about one month, roughly $32,000 in revenue (estimated with the maximum price of $13 per order). Key points: the trending sound drove reach, the cheap price lowered the purchase barrier, and the broken product link was a missed-sales problem that likely reduced results [L0655.021].
- Competitor check on a viral toy product (monkey product): the account had about 200-300 million views over a year of uploading, but the order numbers showed about 600 orders at about 40 USD, which is roughly 24,000 USD total revenue. That is about 120 USD per million views, below the author's average of about 180 USD per million, which confirmed the product was low-converting. Another product tested with a dummy order had only two orders in 24 hours, so it was dropped [L0501.027].

### Check tracker accuracy and limits

**Accuracy**

- Cross-check store sales with at least two tools, because no sales tool is 100 percent accurate. Estimates for stores around $1,000 per day are roughly 50 to 70 percent accurate, and accuracy drops as stores scale up [L0335.009].
- Tracker estimates from Peekster are about 50 to 70 percent accurate, so use them as a directional signal rather than exact revenue [L1177.047].
- Peak Star tends to underestimate sales. A store showing $800 there is likely doing at least that much. Shop Hunter and AfterLive store tracking gave the most consistent numbers in the mentor's comparisons [L0335.010].
- Do not treat tracker revenue, spend or traffic figures as exact. Use them to rank stores and products, then cross-check before committing budget [L1164.007].
- Revenue from tracker estimates is only a hypothesis until confirmed. Sales tools typically track a store for a limited window of one to two hours and extrapolate to a full day, so any single estimate is only a hypothesis. For important decisions, compare two or three tools before you act [L0335.011].

**New stores and large stores**

- Sales trackers need about 24 hours before they show reliable figures for a newly opened store. Judge a new store's sales only after that window, not from the first hours of data [L0335.056].
- Stores reporting $1 million or more per day in sales tools are usually established brands with big launches, multi-channel promotion or celebrity support, not new stores. Use such figures only as a sign of what is possible, not as a benchmark for a beginner [L0335.027].
- Winning Hunter's Sales Tracker only estimates revenue for stores doing $10k to $100m per month, because the old tracker capped at $1m per month. Each estimate falls between two revenue brackets, such as $9,750,000 to $13,000,000 per month, so read it as a range [L1164.006].
- In Winning Hunter's store list, the Est. Revenue column can be switched between 1d and 30d views and shows MIN and MAX estimates. Compare the 30d figure with its MIN and MAX range rather than a single number, because the revenue is a bracket estimate and not a tracked figure [L1164.051].

**Trend tracking**

- Track the same store's daily sales across several days to see the direction of the trend. A sharp rise or fall is a reason to recheck the numbers in a second tool before acting on them [L0335.064].
- A store that moved from 2.6k to 5.2k in sales over two days can be monitored for trend changes, and comparing those numbers with a second tool gives a more reliable figure [L0335.021].

**Tool list**

- PPSpy (Chrome extension), Shop Hunter and Dropship.io estimate the sales of other stores, and PeakStar also tracks store sales. Use them to check whether a competitor's ads are producing real sales before you copy the product [L1436.014].
- PP Ads is a Chrome extension that estimates a Shopify store's sales. The presenter says the data can be inaccurate, and a Shopify snippet that checks best-selling collections is used for more accurate results [L1184.053].

### Confirm ad activity and brand signals

**Ad counts**

- An AI chat cannot query the Meta Ad Library live, so any ad count it gives is unverified. Count the paid Meta ads for each competitor yourself in the Meta Ad Library, which takes about five minutes per niche [L0354.017].
- Use the 'Active' filter and recheck it regularly to see whether competitors keep running ads for a product. Continued spending signals demand [L1173.038].
- Competitor active ads for one product indicate its stage: 20 or more means the competitor has moved from testing into scaling, 30 or more is solid proof of concept, and 50 or more suggests roughly $1,000 to $2,000 or more in revenue from the product (time period not specified) [L1174.030].
- High engagement on competitor ads is a positive signal for a test product, while the competition level (low, medium or high) should be recorded to inform the decision [L1162.008].
- The Ads Launched figure on a brand overview counts the ads a brand started within the selected period, and it is separate from total and active ad counts. Compare it with active ads to judge how quickly a competitor is adding new creative [L0374.047].

**Brand tracking setup**

- A brand added to the tracker needs at least 48 hours before its data is complete. During the first hours, the top hooks, personas, angles, concepts, desires, emotions, awareness stages and USPs are still being filled in, and the totals do not add up [L0374.007].
- For EU and UK ads, Winning Hunter models spend from reach at a CPM of $11, and some ads show no EU or UK data. Treat those spend figures as estimates [L1164.034].
- Check the installed pixels and tracking tools. The list shows which ad platforms and analytics tools a brand uses: Meta Pixel, TikTok, Google Analytics (GA4 / UA / GT), AppLovin / Axon, Klaviyo, Shopify Analytics, Triple Whale, Microsoft Clarity and Web Pixels Manager [L1164.035].

**Proof of offer**

- A store that has consistent sales for a long time is proof that the offer works. Its ads and landing page can be used as a baseline for testing your own, with changes to offer and branding [L1184.055].

### Check traffic sources

- Traffic sources can be estimated with Similarweb, and the mix shows where the brand's growth is coming from. In the case, roughly 53% of traffic was direct, 29% social, and 66% organic in total [L0871.035].
- Traffic estimates from third-party tools can differ widely between country sites and are not exact. Example: one brand showed about 20,000 monthly visits on its UK site but 183,000 to 193,000 on its US site, so check each market separately [L0524.007].
- When SimilarWeb hides a competitor's traffic, Ubersuggest gives an estimated monthly visit count for each country site. Pull several months of data and check the trend, because a steady rise points to a growing store [L0524.066].
- A competitor site without a country rank has too little traffic in that country to be ranked. This does not indicate a problem with the site itself [L0856.098].
- Competitors visiting your site can usually only be identified at the IP and city level through standard tracking. Few businesses analyze individual visitors this deeply, so repeat visits from rivals are not a practical concern [L0837.062].

### Cross-check before you act

- Judging a product only by its viral view count led to wrong conclusions when a competitor's account with 10 million views made just 12 sales. Always verify sales with a tracking tool before copying a product that looks like a winner [L0372.005].
- A bunion-corrector product appeared to be going viral on many accounts, and one store was estimated at about 800,000 dollars in sales within one to two months of its first upload. Across several accounts the product had over 100 million views, which seemed to explain the sales. A brand-new account with 10 million views for the same product had only 12 sales, and PPAds later showed that the best-selling store earned most of its money from a different product, most likely through paid ads that were not visible on TikTok [L0372.004].
- Use an AI assistant with web browsing to find a quick market figure, such as how much of a product people use per year. Confirm that figure with a second source before putting it into a profit model, because an AI answer is a starting point, not verified data [L0524.067].
- For important decisions, compare two or three tools before you act [L0335.011].

### Turn estimates into profit

- Estimated minimum profit = revenue x profit margin. Example: $134,000 x 30% is about $40,000. Typical margins range from 15 to 30 percent, so use the lower end unless organic traffic supports a higher figure [L0352.034].

## Staffing, agency and outsourcing economics

### Financial reporting before any hire

- Before you hire an agency, put proper financial reporting in place: ROAS, contribution margin by channel, fixed costs and incremental profit. Without this data you cannot align incentives or measure whether the agency adds value. [L0217.005]
- Establish one shared reporting tool or dashboard as the source of truth for all metrics. This prevents disagreements about performance and keeps both parties accountable to the same numbers. [L0217.023]
- Percentage of ad spend is a vanity metric, not a performance metric. It rewards dollars spent rather than business outcomes and can create misaligned incentives with your agency. [L0217.004]

### Hire cost against profit

- Break-even revenue for a hire = monthly cost of the hire ÷ profit margin. Worked examples from the lesson: a $5/hour entry-level hire at 160 hours per month costs $800, and at a 20% margin the business needs $800 / 0.20 = $4,000 monthly revenue to cover it. A $15/hour US hire at 160 hours costs $2,400, so $2,400 / 0.20 = $12,000 monthly revenue is needed. [L0337.012]

Hire cost comparison (160 hours per month, 20% profit margin assumed):

| Hire | Hourly rate | Monthly cost | Revenue needed per month | Orders per month (example) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Entry-level, from another country | $5 | $800 | $4,000 | 2 to 4 (about one order per week covers it) |
| Experienced, US-based | $15 | $2,400 | $12,000 | 6 to 8 (or 2 to 3 per week; one per month for higher-ticket items) |

Order counts assume order values in the range of the example. [L0337.013]

Owner time versus profit at $20,000 monthly revenue and 20% margin (the three scenarios are alternatives, not stacked):
