| Scenario | Monthly revenue | Margin | Gross profit | Hire cost | Profit after hire | Owner hours per month |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Owner alone | $20,000 | 20% | $4,000 | $0 | $4,000 | 160 |
| Entry-level hire ($5/h) | $20,000 | 20% | $4,000 | $800 | $3,200 | 120 |
| US hire ($15/h) | $20,000 | 20% | $4,000 | $2,400 | $1,600 | 80 |

The hire at $5/h frees 40 owner hours per month (10 per week); the hire at $15/h frees 80 hours per month (20 per week). [L0337.014]

- A hire that costs profit can still be the right decision when the owner's freed hours are worth more than the profit lost. Compare what the owner could do with 10 or 20 extra hours per week against the wage cost before judging a hire. [L0337.015]
- Budgeting determines when you can afford each hire, such as one, three or ten virtual assistants, a store manager or a PPC agency. Use your numbers to decide when to hire, not your hopes. *(Older advice, Source B 2021-2022.)* [L0632.006]
- Describe who manages the finances in 10 years, either an in-house finance team or an outsourced provider. Also consider which options the profit creates, such as developing your own products when you currently resell, or investing in assets like a warehouse or a network of warehouses. *(Older advice, Source B 2021-2022.)* [L0439.020]

### Agency fee math

- Hiring an agency increases fixed costs and raises the break-even revenue threshold. If the agency does not generate incremental contribution margin equal to or exceeding its fee, you make less profit than if you managed the work yourself. [L0217.008]
- Example: with a 25% contribution margin, justifying a $10,000 monthly agency fee requires $40,000 in incremental revenue that month. This is break-even before considering other business benefits. [L0217.009]
- Spend-to-retainer ratio should be at least 1:7 (seven dollars of ad spend per one dollar of agency fee), ideally 1:10 or higher, for the agency to be profitable for your business. [L0217.006]
- The ideal spend level is $10,000 to $15,000 per month in ad spend for every $1,000 paid monthly to an agency, balancing efficiency with scale. [L0217.007]
- Standard agency compensation is about 5% of monthly ad spend, or 10% when the agency handles broader services beyond media buying, such as creative, strategy or reporting. [L0217.018]
- Agency fees must be counted in your return on ad spend. The agency has to raise your conversion rate and performance enough to cover its monthly fee, otherwise the fee eats into your profit. *(Older advice, Source B 2021-2022.)* [L1053.015]
- Evaluate agency performance over a typical period of 60 to 90 days. This gives both parties time to learn processes and results to emerge, while remaining short enough to make quick decisions. [L0217.010]

#### Example: ROAS Agency Package B

- ROAS Agency Package B costs $250 per month, has no set-up fee, and charges a 4% ad spend fee. The sources do not state whether the 4% is added on top of the $250 monthly fee, so confirm this with the agency contact before committing. [L1253.008]
- Estimated monthly cost of ROAS Agency Package B, if the fees are additive: $250 + (4% × monthly ad spend). Example: at $10,000 monthly ad spend, the fee is $400 and the total is $650 per month. [L1253.009]

#### Deciding whether to hire a specialist

1. Record your current monthly ad spend.
2. Record your current ROAS and calculate your monthly revenue from it (revenue = ad spend × ROAS).
3. Record your profit margin, or your cost of goods, to derive the margin.
4. Calculate your current monthly profit after ad spend.
5. Estimate the spend and ROAS a hired specialist would need to reach, add the agency fee, and compare the resulting profit with your current profit to decide whether hiring is profitable. [L1448.002]

- Monthly profit = revenue × profit margin − ad spend − agency fee. Revenue equals ad spend × ROAS, so a higher ROAS raises revenue at the same spend. Use this to test whether a proposed improvement in spend or ROAS covers the fee. [L1448.003]
- Starting point: monthly revenue of $7,000 at a 20% profit margin, which the lesson treats as about $400 profit per month after ad spend, or $4,800 per year. Scenario: a specialist doubles monthly ad spend from $1,000 to $2,000 and improves ROAS from 7 to 10 at the same margin, which gives $20,000 revenue instead of $7,000. Adding a $1,200 monthly service fee, the lesson states profit rises from $400 to $825 per month, roughly doubling it and freeing the owner's time for SEO, email marketing and conversion work. [L1448.004]
- Verification note: the $400 figure only reconciles if the margin is applied to revenue minus ad spend. Under the same formula the scenario gives $800 rather than $825, so recheck the numbers in your own model before relying on them. Also, 20% of $7,000 alone is $1,400, so the $400 figure implies the margin is applied after ad spend; confirm which definition you use. [L1448.004]

### Compensation and incentives

- Base compensation plus incentives avoids a conflict of interest. If an agency is paid as a percentage of ad spend and you reduce spend to improve profitability, its income falls while its labour may stay the same or increase, which creates natural resistance to spending discipline. [L0217.039]
- Measure the outcomes that trigger payment, such as completed installations or delivered orders with no cancellation. A salesperson with fewer sales and more completed installations can earn more than one with many unfinished sales. [L0378.084]

### Virtual assistants: time tracking

- Track and record how you spend your time every day for 30 consecutive days to establish a baseline and identify patterns in your work. *(Older advice, Source B 2021-2022.)* [L0223.001]
- Do not analyse time-tracking data until you have at least 30 days of tracking. Analysing after only one or two weeks is premature and will not reveal actual work patterns. *(Older advice, Source B 2021-2022.)* [L0223.002]
- Time-tracking analysis reveals activities that consume more time than you realised. Some may be unnecessary, and others may be reduced to free hours for higher-priority business activities. *(Older advice, Source B 2021-2022.)* [L0223.003]
- A team member, such as a virtual assistant, who spends much time every week on profit tracking but never reviews the results is wasting time and effort. Always check what time and team effort cost against what they return. *(Older advice, Source B 2021-2022.)* [L1246.006]
- Give the virtual assistant access to ad accounts, fulfilment costs and Shopify costs. Have them produce a monthly Excel sheet for accounting. [L1431.056]

### Virtual assistants: sourcing channels

- Virtual Staff Finder is a matchmaking service for offshore assistants. It charges a one-time flat fee of about $400 to $500, delivers about three candidates matching your criteria, sends three more if none fit, and offers no trial period. You pay the hire directly, so it suits general skill sets better than very specific ones. [L1359.025]
- Freelance marketplaces such as Odesk, Elance and Freelancer, plus Craigslist and onlinejobs.ph for postings, give you a large pool and trial periods. Because you do the screening yourself, this path takes more time, and you should hire about three people to keep one or two. [L1359.026]

### Virtual assistants: hiring funnel

1. Post a job ad that requires a specific detail, such as a unique question or a local reference, that every applicant must include in their title, email or first message.
2. Reject everyone who misses the required detail without reading their resume.
3. Require a timed, objective test, such as a free online grammar test, and reject those who skip it or do not send the result.
4. Interview at least three candidates for each hire, in a tight time window.
5. Hire and train in batches of two or three, then keep the winner and release the rest. [L1359.027]

- Use more than one hidden requirement in the ad. Applicants on large marketplaces are used to a single one and copy-paste their replies, while requiring extra effort filters out people who do not read messages carefully. [L1359.028]
- Grammar test thresholds used for Philippine-based staff: 70 or better for roles without phone or client contact, and 80 or better for roles with phone or client contact. The test takes about an hour. [L1359.029]
- Interview at least three candidates for every position, with two as an absolute minimum, so you can compare them. Do not hire the first acceptable person you meet. [L1359.030]
- Arriving late to an interview is a disqualifier. Schedule interviews in a tight block, about 15 minutes apart, so a missed slot does not waste a whole day. [L1359.031]

### Virtual assistants: checking comprehension

- The yes-sir problem: in some cultures a yes can mean maybe or no, because people avoid disappointing you. Do not ask whether they understood, since they will almost always say yes. [L1359.032]
- To check comprehension, ask the candidate to repeat the task back in their own words and to explain exactly how they would do it. Later in the interview, ask about a detail you mentioned at the start to test attention. [L1359.033]

### Apprentices

- Apprentice hiring example: 17 people applied after an ad that required a completed form and a short video. Four or five dropped out at that stage, so the founder never had to screen their resumes. [L1359.034]
- Recruit apprentices through communities of lifestyle entrepreneurs, forums and Facebook groups, not general job boards like Monster, whose users look for conventional office jobs. [L1359.035]
- Apprentice package: a starting stipend of $1,000 per month, with housing, food, laundry and daily necessities covered. The effective cost to the employer is about $1,500 per month, and pay rises later. [L1359.036]
- Arrange housing and daily life before a relocating apprentice arrives, and keep them close during the first phase so that training and the move are as easy as possible. The less friction for the apprentice, the more willing they are to relocate. [L1359.037]
- For apprentices, the final interview should focus on personal fit and whether you can work well together, since they are hired more for fit than for a specific skill set. Fit matters because an apprentice is an important, long-term hire. [L1359.038]

### Team cost in profit targets

- Monthly profit must cover team salaries. For one owner, a $1,500 per month team manager required at least about $15,000 of profit per month to be affordable. [L1431.058]
- Starting point: a solo owner building a drop shipping business earns about $20,000 per month with no team, 80% COGS, and an allocation of 10% profit, 10% owner salary, 10% taxes, 30% staff and 40% operating expenses. [L1334.041]
  1. Step 1: the owner hires a customer service VA at about $1,000 per month, which leaves about $200 of the $1,200 staff budget.
  2. Step 2: when revenue doubles to about $40,000 per month and the VA is overloaded, the owner adds an order processing VA at about $600 per month, bringing staff pay to $1,600 and leaving about $800 in budget. The owner stops answering customer and order questions and uses the time for marketing work.
  3. Step 3: the owner's maximum revenue capacity rises to about $50,000 with the target set to about $75,000. At about $50,000 per month a catalogue manager is hired at about $1,200 per month to fix outdated product information that was sending customer questions to the wrong place.
  4. Step 4: at about $75,000 per month with about $1,700 to $1,800 left in the staff budget, the owner hires an SEO person at about $800 per month and then a PPC person. PPC comes first because it pays back faster, while hiring an agency would cost more.
  5. Step 5: the owner trains a hire through the marketing modules and grows revenue to about $125,000 per month, with about $3,900 of unused staff budget. The owner adds a sales rep at about $1,000 per month and splits sales from customer service. Capacity is then raised again, with the target set to about $250,000.
  6. Step 6: with budget building up, the owner lowers the staff allocation to 20% and resumes taking profit and owner salary.
- *(Older case, Source B 2021-2022.)*
- The max revenue capacity and target capacity figures in the Profit First planning sheet do not feed any formula. They only show the team which level the business is aiming for, so budget calculations must be done with the allocation percentages and staff pay alone. *(Older advice, Source B 2021-2022.)* [L1334.046]

### Reporting requirements for outside help

- Send ad performance stats to the support team every 24 hours once ads are live, and keep doing so daily until told otherwise. Reporting is the most important ongoing obligation in the testing phase. [L1355.001]
- The first three days after launch are the most critical reporting period, so stats for Day 1, Day 2 and Day 3 must be sent without gaps. Early data is what allows a losing product or ad to be caught before significant budget is lost. [L1355.003]
- Failing to report that ads are running lets spend continue unnoticed, and advertisers have lost several hundred dollars this way. Stats shared earlier could have saved more than $100 in such cases, and this happens several times a week. [L1355.004]
- Outside help depends entirely on visible data. Without daily stats, the support team cannot tell an advertiser to pause a product or turn off ads before losses grow, so the advertiser must share the numbers first for any intervention to be possible. [L1355.005]

#### Measuring automated support in Gorgias

- In Gorgias, Statistics > Live > Overview only loads periods of one day or less and shows "Failed to retrieve statistic" for longer ranges. For multi-week automation results, open Statistics > Automation > Automation Add-on, choose a range such as the last 28 days, and export the flow data as CSV. [L0752.035]
- Analytics for the Automation Add-on: Statistics > Automation > Automation Add-on (from the dropdown in the top left). Key metrics: total add-on interactions, automation rate, volume per flow, automated add-on interaction, served by an agent, drop-off, and per-flow performance (Quick response flow, Article recommendation, Track order, Report order issue, Return order, Cancel order). Data can be exported as CSV. [L0752.029]
- Automation rate = automated add-on interactions ÷ total interactions in the same period. Example: 59 automated and 9 handled by an agent make 68 total, and the report displayed an add-on automation rate of 86%. [L0752.036]
- A typical agent reply to a single ticket takes at least 30 seconds, usually about 1 to 1.5 minutes and up to 2 minutes, so each automated interaction saves that time. [L0752.031]
- Estimated agent time saved = automated interactions × minutes an agent would need per ticket. A single reply-wait-reply ticket takes about 1 to 1.5 minutes and up to 2 minutes, so 59 automated interactions save roughly 59 to 88 minutes at 1 to 1.5 minutes each. [L0752.037]
- Case: a new skincare brand (Toti) on Gorgias Automate. Starting point: a brand with a short history, using quick response flows, order management flows, a flow builder return policy flow, help centre, contact form and auto reply rules. Results after 28 days: 68 total interactions compared with 28 days before; 86% of interactions were resolved by add-on features; 59 interactions were automated and 9 needed an agent. Quick response flows: 52 automated and 7 not. The average agent reply time for a single "where is my order" ticket is about 1 to 1.5 minutes, or up to 2 minutes, because the agent replies, waits and replies again. Automation saved 59 agent interactions in 28 days. [L0752.030]

## Valuing and selling a store: numbers buyers check

### Valuation basis and formula

Store valuation is monthly net profit times a multiple, plus the value of qualifying assets.

```
Store valuation    = Monthly Net Profit x Multiple + Assets
Monthly Net Profit = Revenue - (COGS + Expenses)
COGS               = product cost + shipping + extra costs
Expenses           = marketing + software + other business costs
Monthly Net Profit is averaged over the last 12 months.
```
[L0553.004, L0858.004, L0858.005]

- The version without an asset line is Valuation = (Income - Expenses) × Multiple, with the multiple based on monthly net profit. [L0231.001]
- Assets are added only when they qualify (see Assets in the sale). [L0553.004, L0553.008]
- Net profit per month is what determines a business's value, and a business making no net profit is worth nothing in a sale. [L1111.155, older: 2020]
- Net profit is what buyers price; a valuation built on revenue is not used. [L1111.155]

### Multiples: ranges and what moves them

Sources give different ranges. Use the one that matches your store's profile and the date of the source.

| Source | Range or value | Basis | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| Source A | 21x to 35x | Monthly net profit, dropshipping | Current [L0231.001] |
| Source A | 22x to 30x typical in last 12 months; 20x to 50x general range | Monthly net profit | Current [L0553.005] |
| Source A | 22x to 30x | Monthly net profit | Current [L0858.006] |
| Source A | About 30x | Monthly net profit | Older: 2022 [L0854.093] |
| Source A | About 30x | Average monthly net profit | Older: 2020 [L1111.156] |
| Source B | 2.5x to 3.5x annual profit (single traffic source, sold to a broker) | Annual profit | Older: 2021-22 [L0511.018] |

- The 2.5x to 3.5x figure uses annual profit, so it is not directly comparable with the monthly multiples. For reference, 30x monthly equals 2.5x annual. [L0511.018, L0553.005]
- Diversified traffic from Google, email sequences, SEO and a stable TikTok or Snapchat flywheel pushes the broker multiple higher than the single-source range. [L0511.018]
- The multiple rises with track record length, store size and diversification, and falls for short history, a single product, a single traffic source, or a six-month-only average. [L0553.007, L0858.007]
- A store bought and resold every 12 to 18 months lands at the lower end of the range. [L0553.007]
- Buyers treat the multiple as a discount for risk. Concentration on one product, one traffic source (for example only Facebook or only Google Ads), one supplier, or a declining profit trend lowers it. Twelve months of stable or growing profit raises it. [L0553.013]

Factors that move the multiple:

| Factor | Larger multiple | Smaller multiple |
|---|---|---|
| Track record | Longer | Shorter |
| Profit average used | 12-month average | 6-month average |
| Store size | Bigger store | Smaller store |
| Product range | More diversification | Single product |

[L0858.007]

- Sites are sometimes valued on the average net profit of the previous three months, so changes need time to show in the figures before a sale. [L1468.017]
- Post-sale support can change the price: a 90-day post-sale period with two calls per month and unlimited email support can add roughly $50,000 to the sale price, because new buyers overvalue the support. [L0553.018]

Worked examples at different multiples:

- $5,000 monthly net profit at 21x is $105,000; at 35x the same store is $175,000. [L0554.002]
- $5,000 monthly net profit at 30x is about $150,000. [L0854.093, older: 2022]
- $10,000 monthly net profit at 22x to 30x gives about $220,000 to $300,000. [L0553.005]
- $25,000 monthly net profit sold for about $750,000 through a broker (about 30x). [L1111.156, older: 2020]

Broker estimate from margin (older source):

- EBITDA equals profit margin times revenue. A 30 percent margin on $1,000,000 in sales gives EBITDA of about $200,000, which implies roughly $450,000 to $700,000 at broker multiples. Higher margins raise the valuation sharply. [L0511.020, older: 2021-22]

Market snapshot, 2018 (older):

- In 2018, 15 dropshipping stores sold, versus 12 in 2017. The average sale price was $138,000 in 2018 compared with $71,000 in 2017, and sales closed 14% faster at 62 days versus 73 days. [L0231.002, older: 2018]

Recent sales (last 12 months, current):

| Metric | Value |
|---|---|
| Stores sold | 26 |
| Total value | $3.03M |
| Value range | $30k to $533k |
| Average value | $116k |
| Multiple at list | 27X |
| Multiple at sale | 24X |
| Average time on market | 66 days |

[L0858.011]

### Two sold stores compared

| Metric | Apparel and Accessories (AliExpress dropshipping) | Home and Garden (Source A) |
|---|---|---|
| Net profit per month | $8.7K | $5K |
| List price | $183,817 (21X multiple) | $141,957 (28X multiple) |
| Sold price | $120,000 (13X multiple) | $133,000 (26X multiple) |
| Age | 12 months | 18 months |
| Days to sell | 126 | 10 |
| Traffic sources | Facebook traffic only | Google Ads, organic, Facebook retargeting |
| Earnings trend | Decreasing | Steady |
| Sale difficulty | Very difficult | Straightforward |

[L0858.012]

- A 12-month-old apparel store on AliExpress with Facebook-only traffic and decreasing earnings made $8.7K net profit per month. It was listed at $183,817 (21X) and sold for $120,000 (13X) after 126 days on the market. [L0858.013]
- An 18-month-old home and garden store with Google Ads, organic traffic and Facebook retargeting, steady earnings and $5K net profit per month was listed at $141,957 (28X) and sold for $133,000 (26X) within 10 days. [L0858.013]
- The second store sold for more despite lower monthly profit because its quality factors were stronger. [L0858.013]

### Net profit averaging

- Use a 12-month average of net profit as the default valuation basis, because it covers seasonality. [L0553.006]
- Shift to a 3- or 6-month average only when a major change makes the 12-month figure unrepresentative of current performance, for example losing organic traffic to a Google update or a sudden viral rise. [L0553.006]
- Conflict: L1468.017 says sites are usually valued on the previous three months' average, while L1111.156 (older) uses the trailing 12 months together with the average of the last three months. Apply the 12-month default unless a major change applies. Where a source uses the three-month figure, changes made now need time to appear in it. [L0553.006, L1468.017, L1111.156]
- Track revenue, costs and owner time separately for each store from the start, because many sellers below $100,000 in revenue cannot say how many hours a specific store needs. [L1468.018]
- Buyers use a 12-month average, so a store that sold badly in the period before listing is valued low. [L0553.006, L0834.101]

### Records and bookkeeping

- A buyer will not trust a P&L built from manually typed figures in a spreadsheet. To sell a business, you must prove your P&L is correct with records. [L0632.008, older: 2021-22]
- When a business sells for six or seven figures or more, buyers scrutinize the numbers strictly. A small sale may pass with little checking, but larger deals will not. [L0632.009, older: 2021-22]
- Keep accurate financial records with all expenses documented; accounting errors destroy buyer trust and reduce the final sale price. [L0231.012]
- Keep accurate financial records from the start, because reconstructing 4- or 5-year-old records is a large amount of work. Accurate records also support decisions such as return on ad spend and profit per product. [L0554.034]
- Track return on ad spend and profit per product, since these are needed for smart business decisions and buyers look for them in financial records. [L0554.035]
- Mistakes in the numbers quickly erode buyer trust, and buyers may suspect that information is hidden. Keep records as you go, including historical data for suppliers and VAs, even when changing work accounts. [L0554.036]
- Use QuickBooks, Xero or FreshBooks, or a spreadsheet, to track expenses. For multiple stores, keep each store's books separate so the buyer can see each one's profit. [L1468.019]
- Keep supplier invoices and bills in one Google Drive organized by month, year, or supplier. This archive makes it easier to pull numbers into the P&L when selling. [L0554.049]
- A broker's vetting includes checking that the Shopify sales numbers are genuine, so buyers of a listed store do not need to verify them on their own. Buyers still run their own due diligence on the overall deal. [L0554.057]
- Keep separate, clear financials for each network of related stores. [L1111.157, older: 2020]

### Metrics to gather before listing

Gather these before selling:

1. Hours worked per week
2. Company start date
3. Average monthly revenue
4. Average monthly expenses
5. Number of products
6. Unique monthly visitors
7. Email subscriber count
8. Number of social media channels with follower counts

[L0231.011]

- A business sells best with 18 to 24 months of history, because buyers look at the trailing 12 months of net profit. [L1111.157, older: 2020]

### Add-backs

- An add-back is an expense that is valid for tax purposes but not for valuing the business, such as a conference trip or a one-off redesign. [L0554.037]
- List it as an expense on the P&L and add it back to net profit to be transparent with buyers. [L0554.037]
- Buyers often negotiate add-backs. A conference trip is an easy add-back. [L0554.038]
- A site redesign can be argued as a one-off, but buyers may treat it as a recurring cost if they expect redesigns every couple of years. [L0554.038]

### Assets in the sale

- Assets that can be added to a store sale are inventory (valued at wholesale), equipment such as laptops or photography gear, and software. [L0553.008]
- An asset counts only if it is included in the sale and necessary to run the business. [L0553.008]
- Discontinued stock and personal costs such as travel are not recoverable. [L0553.008]

### Google Analytics and due diligence

- Google Analytics is essential for verifying traffic sources and visitor trends. Buyers require access to confirm these metrics during due diligence. [L0231.021]
- Install Google Analytics about six months before the sale. Buyers rely on it to detect bot or fake traffic and to see traffic sources, and it is the de facto standard. [L1468.020]
- Preparing Google Analytics access for buyer due diligence takes active time: you must grant the necessary permissions, provide documentation of historical traffic sources, and remain available to verify visitor data accuracy and trends. [L0231.033]
- In Google Analytics, be an admin or know the admin username, and be ready to add buyers as users so they can run their own reports. Screenshots of Analytics can be enough for early review. [L1468.023]
- If a seller refuses Google products, Clicky is an accepted alternative to Google Analytics for buyers' due diligence. [L1468.021]
- Server log statistics such as AWStats, or stats you simply post without a real analytics account, are not accepted for due diligence. Buyers will not trust them. [L1468.022]
- Screenshots of earnings and traffic are easy to fake, while video is harder to fake and more trusted. Record a screencast of earnings and traffic, especially for public auction listings. [L1468.024]
- Every financial figure in a listing must match the others. State the exact date range for earnings and traffic, and say whether traffic is sessions or page views. [L1468.025]
- Brokers verify earnings by logging into the account and checking screenshots themselves, or by having the seller show the data live. Expect this step before a listing goes live. [L1468.026]

### Seasonality evidence

- Buyers of seasonal niches want at least two years of sales data before trusting a spike, because one year cannot show whether the spike repeats in the same period. [L0554.054]
- If the second year shows the spike in the same place as the first, the store is much easier to value. [L0554.054]
- The 12-month average covers seasonality, which is why it is the default basis. [L0553.006]
- Do not sell the store while sales are down, because the price will be lower than what it is worth. [L0834.101, older: 2021]

### Profit-growth levers before sale

Three levers raise store value:

- Raise revenue with profitable products, suppliers and traffic streams.
- Hold or improve margins.
- Lower buyer risk to raise the multiple.

[L0553.009, L0858.008]

Ways to grow profit:

- To grow profit, improve ad ROI and cut unprofitable ads, renegotiate product costs or move to a new supplier, and build relationships that turn customers into repeat buyers. [L0858.010]
- Email list growth, higher average order value, higher customer lifetime value, and better return on ad spend are revenue and margin levers. [L0553.009]
- Adding email marketing to a store can lift revenue by roughly 10 to 20 percent, according to the lesson's estimate. [L0553.010]
- A long-running database of outreach contacts and earned backlinks can add resale value, since a buyer may value it much like an email list. [L0422.003, older: 2021-22]
- A sustainable cost per acquisition, built through upsells, pre-sells and reaching customers before the purchase, raises valuation along with defensibility, brand awareness and profit margin. [L0511.019, older: 2021-22]

Improve margins before selling in four steps:

1. Stop exploratory ad spend that does not return a positive ROI, especially in the 12 months before a sale.
2. Renegotiate product costs by asking the supplier for lower prices on best-selling products, or source an alternative supplier with the same quality.
3. Build customer relationships and retention, since keeping existing customers is cheaper than acquiring new ones.
4. Improve ad return on ad spend.

[L0553.011]

- Eliminate ad spend that is exploratory and does not currently return ROI during the 12 months leading up to a sale, because buyers value proven profit rather than tests. [L0553.012]

### Sale timing and format

- There are three ways to increase store value: grow revenue while keeping margins, grow profit by cutting spending, or improve the multiple the store can sell for. [L0858.008]
- Sell a store as an asset sale instead of the business entity. Stores profitable for at least 12 months can be listed on Empire Flippers, while newer stores can be posted on the Fast Business Forum. [L0854.094, older: 2022]
- A store bought and resold every 12 to 18 months lands at the lower end of the multiple range. [L0553.007]

#### Further details

- Annual return on buying a website equals monthly net profit multiplied by 12, divided by the purchase price. For example, a site priced at $100,000 that earns $5,000 net profit per month returns 60% per year if profit stays flat. The result is a best-case figure because it assumes no growth and no decline. [L1468.048]

## Other details

### Startup, store setup and expansion

- Startup costs in 2020 were small: a Shopify plan at about 29 USD per month, a domain at about 14 USD per year, and a start credit of 100 USD each from Google Ads and Facebook. After that, reinvest profit into ads. (Older advice, 2020.) [L1277.110]
- Judge a store's performance by its setup goals first: suppliers approved, conversion-optimized site, Google Ads optimized, Bing Ads set up, SEO foundations, email campaigns and remarketing running. Then keep improving those, since a store is never finished. (Older advice, 2021.) [L0801.136]
- Use this checklist before starting another store (older advice, 2021):
  1. Get approved with every supplier in the niche, aiming for 20 suppliers.
  2. Optimize the site for conversions.
  3. Optimize Google Ads.
  4. Set up Bing Ads.
  5. Optimize the site for search engines.
  6. Set up email campaigns.
  7. Run remarketing.
  8. Keep auditing and improving the first store before building a second one.

  [L0801.137]
- Start a second store only when the first is set up and its budgets are tuned, and keep improving the first store while doing so. (Older advice, 2021.) [L0801.138]
- Use this setup sequence for a new store (Source A, undated):
  1. Follow the Compliance Checklist.
  2. Setup Google Analytics.
  3. Setup Google Search Console.
  4. Submit Your Products To Google Merchant Center.
  5. Create Your Google Remarketing Campaign.
  6. Set Up Your Bing Webmaster Tools Account.
  7. Create Your Microsoft Merchant Center Store Account.
  8. Submit Your Products To Microsoft Merchant Center.
  9. Create Your Bing Shopping Campaigns.
  10. Create Your Facebook Ads Account.
  11. Connect Your Store To Facebook Ads.
  12. Create Your Audience For Facebook Ads.
  13. Create Your Facebook Remarketing Campaign.
  14. Locate and Contact Top Blogs in Your Niche.
  15. Create Your Promotions.

  [L0188.001]

### Shopify and platform settings

- Under Online Store > Preferences in Shopify, the Customer privacy section offers options to limit tracking for customers in Europe (GDPR, consent required before tracking) and to limit the third-party sale of California customers' data (CCPA). Enable these options if the store targets customers in those regions. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0370.015]
- Track quantity must be turned on for each product under its inventory settings, and the exact number in stock entered there, before the inventory status can display any count on the product page. [L0976.031]
- Enable Shopify order notifications on the phone so that new sales are visible as they happen; this was the alert the seller used during the viral phase. [L0821.009]
- Google Analytics custom dashboards and custom Google Ads reporting are optional. Google's support documentation has current, screenshot-based guides for these. (Older advice, 2022.) [L0429.071]
- Source A tracking sends store data to the course provider so the theme's performance can be monitored. To opt out, untick this option and click save. [L0973.013]
- If the reviewer must examine paid traffic data, grant access to your Google Analytics and Google Ads accounts before the call. If access has not been granted, tell the reviewer explicitly that they need it. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0364.009]
- Use Zapier at about $20 per month for abandoned cart recovery and order tracking. It recovers at least one additional sale from existing traffic, which makes it cost-effective for shops with a consistent abandon rate. [L0286.030]

### Benchmarks (older advice, 2020)

The benchmarks below date from 2020 and are older advice. Check them against current conditions before use.

| Benchmark | Value |
|---|---|
| Alexa rank for a valid niche | under 100,000 |
| Wholesale cost | at most 50% of MAP |
| Ad spend per sale | max 10% of MAP |
| Google Ads conversion goal | 2.5% |
| Unique clicks before judging conversion | about 100 |
| Minimum brands per store | 20 |
| Minimum products per store | 100 |
| Google Shopping competitors per product | under 10 |
| Google Trends floor for year-round sales | 50 |
| Acceptable out-of-stock ETA | 2 to 3 weeks |
| Out-of-stock ETA that removes ads | 8 weeks or more, or unknown |
| Shopify plan | $29 per month |
| Business sale multiple | 30x monthly net profit |
| Payment fee example | 2.9% |
| New store starting ad budget | about $30 per day |
| Suppliers approved before ads | at least 2 with products uploaded |
| Live chat first response | within 30 seconds |
| Email response target | within 30 minutes |
| Inbound calls answered | 95% |
| First ad approval wait | about 2 days |

[L1111.158]

### Supplier, order and lead tracking

- Track supplier outreach in a supplier tracking sheet with notes on who was contacted, when to follow up, and reminders. (Older advice, 2021.) [L0834.100]
- Keep a tracking sheet for supplier conversations, with date, contact, what was said and the next follow-up step. This turns early outreach into a reliable record for later follow-up. (Older advice, 2022.) [L0803.092]
- Keep detailed notes for every supplier contact in the tracking sheet. With many suppliers, you will otherwise forget who said what and what the next step is. [L0928.011]
- Master Supplier List tracking columns to fill for every contact:

  | Column | Field | Content |
  |---|---|---|
  | E | Date of contact | Date you reached out to the supplier |
  | F | Contact person | Name of the person you spoke with at the supplier |
  | G | Notes | What happened on the call or email, plus next steps, such as a callback date, that the form will be emailed, or that a website dealer application must be submitted |

  Always record the next step and any follow-up date, for example "contact us again in two months", so it does not get forgotten. [L0928.010]
- When receiving multiple orders with products from different suppliers, each supplier typically ships separately with its own tracking number and packaging. Customers expect this; they understand they may receive 2-3 packages from 2-3 different suppliers on different days. (Source A, 2023.) [L0251.021]
- Use the paid orders list to identify fulfillment bottlenecks. Cards with dates older than your typical supplier response time (usually 1-3 days) signal a delay, either in sending orders to suppliers or waiting for supplier response. The visual board reveals exactly which stage orders are accumulating, enabling quick investigation and intervention before delays cascade to slower shipping for customers. [L0292.017]
- With more than around 100 orders a month, orders are easy to lose track of without status labels. Use labels so that every order's stage is visible at a glance. [L1000.067]
- A glance at the prospect sheet shows the day's activity, for example 15 calls received, three closed sales and 30 prospects still open. This is enough to forecast the business's near-term workload. [L1000.054]
- Measuring and recording all custom order requests in a database enables continuous optimization: it reveals which follow-up timing works, which approaches convert, and why deals succeed or fail. [L0284.009]
- Add outcome columns to your custom order tracking sheet: record whether each lead resulted in a sale (won/lost/pending) and the specific reason (budget, timeline, feature fit, competitor choice, follow-up timing). This enables you to identify patterns in conversion failures and pinpoint which process improvements matter most. [L0284.015]
- Roughly 80% of sales come from about 20% of products, and within that group a further 80/20 split applies. Monitor live inventory only for your best sellers, and do not try real-time inventory updates for the whole catalog. (Older advice, 2020.) [L0852.036]
- The store in the lesson received fewer than one refund per month, and sometimes about one refund every three months, across thousands of orders. These numbers depend on clear descriptions and clear policies. [L0910.034]
- The store received about two customer inquiries per week against five or more sales per week, which shows the effect of pre-sale and post-sale communication. These figures reflect the store's own experience, not a benchmark. [L0910.035]

### Changelog, baselines and test records

- Record a baseline figure, such as the 90-day average of a metric, in the notes before a change, so the result of the change can be compared against it. Without a baseline, a result like an open rate of 50% cannot be judged as an improvement. [L0410.008]
- Use the changelog to explain sales movements: when sales rise or fall, check which logged experiments were running at that time before making unrelated changes. Attribute a rise to a test to double down on it, and stop a test that appears to cause a drop as soon as possible. [L0410.009]
- Fill the Expected Results cell before a change goes live, because a written prediction is what shows later whether the change met the target or only looked like it did. Without a pre-recorded number, a result such as sales staying flat tends to be explained after the fact instead of judged against the goal. [L0410.015]
- Track every test in a consistent system, because a documented library of past tests supports future decisions and adds value to the business when it is sold. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L1364.030]

### Personal operating metrics and habit tracking

- Habit tracker layout: each monthly block in the Google Sheets habit tracker repeats this structure. Each quarter tab repeats it for three months.

  | (blank) | Month name | 1 | 2 | 3 |... | 31 | Total | Goal | Complete | Reward |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | (blank) | Agenda | (blank) |... | (blank) | | | | | | |
  | (blank) | 30 Mins Development | TRUE/FALSE |... | TRUE/FALSE | | | 0 | 0.75 | 0 | |
  | (blank) | New Idea To Growth Guide | TRUE/FALSE (only on scheduled days) |... | | | | 0 | 0.75 | 0 | |
  | (blank) | Launch New Ad Creative | TRUE/FALSE (only on scheduled days) |... | | | | 0 | 0.75 | 0 | |
  | (blank) | Attend Weekly Call | TRUE/FALSE (only on scheduled days) |... | | | | 0 | 0.75 | 0 | |

  The weekday header row (M T W T F S S) sits above each month's date row, and the first weekday is shifted per month to match the calendar. [L0456.006]
- Each habit row ends with four columns: Total (count of completed days), Goal (set to 0.75), Complete (completion ratio) and Reward (what you give yourself when the goal is reached). Goal 0.75 means a 75% completion target. [L0456.007]
- Each day's cell for a habit holds a TRUE/FALSE dropdown. Set it to TRUE when the habit was done and FALSE when it was not. [L0456.008]
- Aim for a completion ratio of at least 75% per habit. When a habit reaches 75% completion, give yourself a reward that is small and affordable, such as a nice dinner or a pair of shoes. [L0456.011]
- To use the tracker, open the Google Sheet and choose File > Make a copy. The copy saves automatically to Google Drive, so your own version keeps your daily entries. [L0456.013]
- To record why a habit was missed, right-click the cell in Google Sheets and choose Comment, which is shown with the shortcut Ctrl+Alt+M. Write the concrete reason in that comment. [L0456.019]
- The habit tracker spreadsheet is split into tabs named Q1 24, Q2 24, Q3 24 and Q4 24. Each tab holds three monthly blocks, one for each month of that quarter. [L0456.020]
- Daily time-log layout, with the sample Monday values filled in (older advice, 2021-22):

  | Task | Monday | Tuesday | Wednesday | Thursday | Friday | Saturday | Sunday |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | Task 1 (customer service phone calls) | 1.5 | | | | | | |
  | Task 2 (product descriptions for the website) | 1 | | | | | | |
  | Task 3 (updating business social media pages) | 0.5 | | | | | | |
  | Task 4 | | | | | | | |
  | Task 5 | | | | | | | |
  | Task 6 | | | | | | | |
  | Task 7 | | | | | | | |
  | Task 8 | | | | | | | |
  | Task 9 | | | | | | | |
  | Task 10 | | | | | | | |
  | Procrastination (estimated total) | | | | | | | |

  Unit for every value: hours. Each day is one column, each task is one row, and the Procrastination row gets a total for the day. Extra day columns can be added for the full 30 days. [L0442.015]
- Each priority needs measurables: hard, trackable data points with a target value at the end of six months. Measurables can be traffic, number of keywords ranked, number of backlinks, sales count, revenue, profit, profit margin, conversion rate, number of approved suppliers or number of uploaded products. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0447.012]
- Set measurable targets that are achievable rather than aspirational extremes, because a target far out of reach for the period tends to discourage progress. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0447.014]
- Typical business weaknesses to test for include: paid ads that were not profitable, no organic traffic so all visits depend on paid traffic, unreliable suppliers or too few supplier options, and too few working hours put into the business by the owner or the team. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0800.015]
- Complete a time-tracking exercise before starting the annual review, so that you can check how you actually spent your hours. The review then relies on recorded time rather than memory. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0800.023]
- Identify your best-selling product from data rather than memory:
  1. Open your Shopify store's sales reports and view sales by product.
  2. Find the product with the highest sales.
  3. Copy that product's URL for your records.

  If the store is new and has no sales yet, this step does not apply. [L1268.006]
- A founder who works 6 hours a day for weeks and sees no progress can give AI the product, the price and the ad results. AI can then diagnose which element is holding the results back and what to change, so the next step is based on data. [L0887.014]
- A monthly creative output report can be shown as a stacked column chart with one column per month, where static and video output stack within each column. Pair the chart with a table holding the same monthly figures so that the growth or drop in total output is visible at a glance. [L0885.028]
- The store review videos are published every other week, so store owners should expect the next review about two weeks after each publication. [L0838.019]

### Ad performance and direct response

- When an ad exploits existing mass desire, each dollar of ad spend can produce roughly 50 to 100 dollars in sales. When an ad has to create the desire first, it behaves like education and returns about 1 dollar in sales per dollar spent. [L0330.002]
- A mentor can interpret account-specific metrics such as CTR and CPM, including the time window to check and when to analyze ad sets. General videos cannot tell you these thresholds for your own product. [L1213.080]
- Winning products often cannot be predicted in advance and must be tested in the market to see whether they sell. Ad creative should create a problem or desire the buyer feels, because the product itself is usually simple and the demand is created through the ad. [L0780.003]
- Historical Facebook ad example: a seasonal product was launched with about 2,000 to 3,000 dollars in budget, and about 50,000 dollars was reinvested in ads from 211,000 dollars in revenue within 30 days. This was in an unsaturated market with cheap cost per click and image ads; it is not a current benchmark. [L0561.043]
- Early internet scam-style ads (e.g., "win free iPhone" or "get free trip") achieved 10 to 30 percent conversion rates because users clicked readily and could not identify untrustworthy websites. This is a historical example, not a guide for current ads. (Source A.) [L0257.002]
- The 2021 Bing figure is 37.5% of U.S. desktop searches, the most recent figure cited at the time. This is a 2021 desktop-only share. [L1034.005]
- Do not use the 37.5% Bing share as the share of total search traffic. It covers only U.S. desktop searches, not all U.S. searches including mobile. [L1034.006]
- To check whether readers finish an ad, offer a small cash reward, such as 5 dollars, to anyone who writes in saying they read it through. Very few replies show that most readers skip the copy, and the headline or opening needs to be stronger. [L1261.152]
- When you focus on B2B, remove low-priced products from paid ads and keep the same ad approach for the rest. Advertise in trade publications, or set up affiliate deals with them, to reach business readers. (Older advice, 2020.) [L0961.015]
- A friend's drop-shipping store generated about $476,900 in revenue in 30 days, and nearly all of its traffic came from Facebook (referrals from mobile.facebook.com, facebook.com and l.facebook.com). The result came from running multiple stores with Facebook as the main traffic source. [L0643.045]
- One client grew from zero to $1.1 million in sales in 10 months. Another grew from $12,000 to $60,000 in monthly sales. Both results were given as examples of Facebook ads working with careful targeting, and the method was not fully described in the lesson. [L0643.046]

### Copy and response measurement

- Track the hit ratio, meaning the share of sales letters or promotions that generate sales. One copywriter reported a hit ratio of 20 to 40 percent before using a structured copy method and 60 to 80 percent after applying it to every project, so measure it per promotion to compare methods. [L1365.052]
- Breakthrough Advertising Marketing Plan: a Google Sheets template to map your market positioning and messaging strategy. Complete it after reading the Breakthrough Advertising copywriting framework to align your positioning with your market's awareness level. https://docs.google.com/spreadsheets/d/1ocIg8b57icetsD1lAyLUpYlbZCXc3dHLzFHuUs9p4xE/edit#gid=1874141477 [L0258.018]
- Readability is measured with Flesch Reading Ease, calculated manually in this order:
  1. Count the words, treating contractions, hyphenated words, abbreviations, figures, symbols, and their combinations as single words.
  2. Count the syllables in each word as it is normally pronounced, counting abbreviations, figures, and symbols as one syllable; if a word has two accepted pronunciations, use the one with fewer syllables.
  3. Count the sentences as units ending in a period, colon, semicolon, question mark, exclamation point, or dash, ignoring paragraph breaks and internal punctuation.
  4. Divide total syllables by total words to get average syllables per word.
  5. Divide total words by total sentences to get average words per sentence.
  6. Combine both averages in the formula to get the score.

  [L0340.060]
- Readability comparison of two ad paragraphs about the same offer:

  | Paragraph | Sentences per paragraph | Words per sentence | Characters per word | Flesch Reading Ease | Flesch-Kincaid grade |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | 1 (plain, short sentences) | 7.0 | 13.1 | 4.1 | 72.1 | 6.4 |
  | 2 (dense, long sentences) | 3.0 | 25.3 | 5.3 | 34.1 | 14.7 |

  [L0340.064]
- Test a new mailing with a small list before committing a large budget, for example 5,000 names instead of 50,000. A small test shows whether the offer, the copy and the list work before the full cost is at risk. [L1366.098]
- Measure readership by how many people travel through the full copy, not by circulation or impressions. Only readers who go through the copy can buy, so a large audience that reads little makes a weak ad. [L1366.099]
- An illustrated four-page letter with a stamped post card cost about 4 cents per piece including postage in 1913 US mail, sent first class with no enclosure. (Historical example.) [L1369.087]
- Follow-up response for a direct mail sequence to the same list of old leads (historical example):

  | Mailing | Content | Orders per mailed piece |
  |---|---|---|
  | 1 | First letter to old leads 1 to 2 years old | 4.5% |
  | 2 | Free examination letter with enclosed circular | 2.5% |
  | 3 | Later letter with circular enclosure | 1.5% |
  | 4 | Large folder with only a post card | 1% |
  | 5 | Long four-page mop-up letter | 2% |

  [L1369.088]
- Record orders per mailing on a record sheet for every letter, headline, envelope and variant, and let that record decide which versions stay. A letter's worth is measured only by the orders it brings back. [L1369.089]
- Test one element at a time, such as the headline, envelope, letterhead, stamp or opening, and compare each against a plain control letter. Keep only what increases orders and drop what does not. [L1369.090]
- Judging a letter by polish or by how well it follows traditional rules is misleading, because a plain letter can outsell a polished one. Only the response measured in orders should decide. [L1369.091]
- In the first six months of one mail-order campaign, the profit on sales reached 34.5 percent. [L1369.092]
- Track response by test cell and count the response over time, because most sales letter response comes in quickly and a trickle follows weeks later. Compare the immediate response with the expected profit, since the trickle is usually small in profit terms. [L1370.112]
- Use no-cost pretests before paying for a mailing test, since a small direct-mail test of 5,000 to 10,000 pieces can cost a lot. Pretest results sometimes reveal a letter is so weak that it must be rewritten entirely. [L1370.113]
- Use split tests to compare variations, such as a neat, organised layout versus a messy, busy one, because the result is often the opposite of what designers expect. Test pricing formats, guarantees and premiums the same way. [L1370.114]

### Customer contact and reputation

- Entrepreneurs should expect odd or hostile customer contacts once they open the business to the world, such as calls at odd hours or suspicion about free shipping on heavy items. These usually do not reflect the business's legitimacy. (Older advice, 2022.) [L0429.075]

### Brand protection and infringement tracking

- Tracking log for brand infringement reports (one tab per platform, same column setup on every tab):

  ```
  Columns: Item (the infringer or page name) | Complaint ID (received after filing) | Person (who handled the report) | Detection Date | Reporting Date | Removal Date | Location (if detectable) | Link (to the infringing content) | Infringement Type (Copyright or Trademark) | Status (Detected, Reported, Resubmitted, Escalated, Removed).

  Also keep a brand information tab with the details needed for reports: brand name, rights owner, official online presence, registration numbers, mailing address and reporting email.

  Use the status 'Resubmitted' when the platform asks for more details, and 'Escalated' when outside help is needed.
  ```

  [L0991.014]
- Monthly, weekly or yearly analysis of the infringement log:
  1. Count the infringements by platform.
  2. Count the infringements by type (copyright or trademark).
  3. Identify the most often infringed products.
  4. Identify where the infringers are located.
  5. Use the results to refine the detection and reporting process.

  [L0991.015]
- Tabs in the infringement tracking board:

  | Tab | Purpose |
  |---|---|
  | Brand Information | Brand name, rights owner, official online presence, registration numbers, mailing address, reporting email |
  | Amazon, eBay, Bol.com, TikTok, Etsy, Shopify | One tab per marketplace or channel searched |
  | Facebook, Instagram | One tab per social platform, sorted by year groups such as 2024 |
  | Other Marketplaces | Other channels not listed above |
  | Stand Alone Websites (current year) | Standalone shops found in the current year |
  | (CLOSED) previous year tabs | Archive of finished years, for example (CLOSED) 2023 Stand Alone Websites |

  Every platform tab uses the same column setup. [L0991.035]

### Finance

- Amex Gold points on advertising spend = 4 x advertising spend, counted only up to $100,000 of spend per year. The maximum is therefore 400,000 points per year. [L0699.015]

### Case results and examples

- Case study: Resistance Bands, Day 18. Revenue: $507 on 17 orders. Average order value: $29.85 (full price, no discounts or giveaways). This demonstrates that sustainable growth does not require artificial tactics like free giveaways or deep discounting. [L0228.035]
- Case study: Resistance Bands, Week 5-6: 96 units sold, $2,900 revenue, 10-18 units per day (average daily orders remained above 10 for 6 of 7 days). The product price increased from an initial $24.95 to a $30.17 average, then to $35.95, without significant order drop-off. Week 6 single day: 12 orders at the raised $35.95 price. Net profit after Amazon fees: $22 per unit. Within 6 weeks, the initial investment was fully recovered and a reorder was funded. Weeks 7-8 produced profit payouts from the two-week Amazon payout cycle. Key lesson: price increases do not necessarily reduce orders in a low-supply scenario with high demand. [L0228.036]
- Success case: 108 orders in one month = $325,000 in sales; average order value $3,000 per order. (Older example, 2020.) [L0268.043]
- Profitability timeline: the first sale typically comes within 30 days of launch if the 21-day launch plan is followed. Timeline to consistent profitability depends on reinvestment rate and ad optimization. (Older advice, 2021.) [L0270.045]
- Example: A competitor added extra features to their weighted blanket (making it Queen-size by default), which doubled product cost. You kept it simple (single size), spent the margin savings on ads, and won the market. [L0041.007]
- Creative and product-page measurement, for a product description: aim for a product description of roughly 600 words; this length supports SEO and avoids thin content. Count words in the product copy and aim for that range. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0461.015]
- Snapchat's SPECS AR glasses were reported to cost around 2,000 US dollars. The price is an estimate from the update commentary, not an official figure. [L1504.019]
- At the stage described, the brand had about 185 products on sale and an annual revenue of around 1 million pounds. The founder reported that the business had reached a multi-seven-figure level. [L1438.048]

### Review and analysis

- Judge a store's performance by its setup goals first, then keep improving those, as set out in the store performance section above. [L0801.136]
